Achieving this vision requires AI systems to undertake the full training pipeline for iterative improvement. Reinforcement learning (RL) plays an essential role in this cycle to improve model capabilities and generalization, yet automating RL remains difficult due to unstable training dynamics and the need for sustained supervision. We introduce AutoRL, a multi-agent system for automating RL training, as a cornerstone for future RSI. Experiments on Qwen3 and LongCat-Flash-Lite demonstrate the superiority of AutoRL over static RL recipes and a general agent. The research records show that existing agents can handle unattended RL supervision and dynamic adjustment. The difficulty for future RSI lies in developing fundamental methods that are valuable for resource-consuming validation.

Hongguan Su, Ming Wang, Shun Zhang, Yifan Wang, Rong Yin, Zhiming Ma, Mengzheng Yang, Peng Chen
cs.AI
Abstract
Long-horizon agents increasingly serve as persistent personal assistants across modern web platforms, managing schedules, applications, and multi-step plans over weeks or months. Users often return to an ongoing conversation after a long conversational hiatus, while earlier bookings and preparation notes stay on record. During this hiatus, scheduled dates may pass even though the retained records remain unchanged and event outcomes go unrecorded. Existing evaluations of long-horizon agents focus on fact retention and explicit state updates. Whether agents perceive the passage of time and adapt their advice accordingly therefore remains unknown. We frame this ability as temporal state perception and use, which means perceiving the current time and applying it when interpreting dated records. To study it, we design session-rekindling tasks that keep the earlier conversation fixed and vary only the follow-up date and the way temporal information reaches the agent. Experiments across five model routes and five temporal-information conditions show that agents can obtain the current time but often fail to use it. They reliably query the clock and even note in their reasoning that the scheduled date has passed, yet many still tell users to prepare for the expired event. Supplying explicit temporal context reduces this error, and providing the computed date relation removes it entirely. Keeping up with time therefore depends on turning perceived time into updated plans. Based on these findings, we design a session-rekindling benchmark that provides temporal-awareness guidance for designing long-horizon agents. Abstract

AI-aided Decision-Making in Education - Uncertainty, Explainability, and Human Responsibility
Fabian Kieser, Paul Tschisgale, Peter Wulff
cs.AI
Abstract
As Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based systems have become increasingly integrated into various aspects of everyday life, including education, understanding how these systems make decisions is of paramount importance. However, the opacity of AI-based decision-making in education (especially with deep learning and generative AI models) can hamper decision-making, especially when outcomes appear unjustified or incorrect. FlexCast: Flexible Context Learning for Time Series Foundation Models
Zuyang Shen, Luqi Gong, Fanda Fan, Bingsong Wang, Xiang Ao, Yahui Wei, Minghao Liu, Chunjie Luo, Jianfeng Zhan
cs.AI
Abstract
Time series foundation models (TSFMs) based on autoregressive (AR) next-token prediction entangle two factors: variable-context conditioning and prediction-unit factorization with autoregressive rollout. Strong AR performance therefore does not reveal whether generalization stems from the flexible training distribution, the local prediction target, the left-to-right inference process, or their combination. We propose FlexCast, a flexible context-target learning framework that disentangles these factors. FlexCast reformulates forecasting as variable-length masked prediction over time series patches: during pretraining, it dynamically samples visible context and masked target patches, exposing the model to diverse context-target configurations without sequential decoding. At inference time, the same pretrained model supports one-step forecasting, confidence-based iterative refinement, and next-token-style generation through a unified mask-and-predict interface, enabling controlled comparison of inference schedules under identical architecture, data, and objective. Evaluated on BLAST and multiple zero-shot benchmarks, FlexCast achieves accuracy competitive with or better than strong TSFM baselines while substantially reducing inference latency. Ablations confirm that context diversity and contiguous target masking drive performance, while left-to-right decoding does not yield consistent gains once flexible context-target relationships have been learned, suggesting that the key benefit of AR-style TSFMs lies in flexible training rather than sequential inference. The code is available at https://github.com/BenchCouncil/FlexCast.

OmniScore-Bench: Evaluating Vision-Language Models on Musical Score Reading
Congren Dai, Zhancheng Guo, Danni Zhao, Wenchu Wang, Xiaobing Li, Maosong Sun
cs.AI
Abstract
Vision-language models (VLMs) can answer questions about musical scores, but their ability to recognise notation, recover its structure and interpret its musical meaning requires separate evaluation. Existing optical music recognition (OMR) resources establish detection and transcription measurements; a transcription score alone leaves the strengths and bottlenecks of a VLM score reader unresolved. We introduce OmniScore-Bench, an evaluation of OMR capabilities in VLMs through 3,000 questions spanning six aspects and 24 task subtypes. Each question specifies a visible scope, reference annotations and an answer schema, enabling automatic evaluation from symbol localisation to musical consistency and evidence-linked question answering. Our assessment of twelve VLMs reveals operation-specific capability profiles and different transcription leaders across excerpt, single-page and multipage tasks. Controlled analyses examine the roles of score images, auxiliary givens and additional page context. On the tested subset, image removal exposes visual dependence in pitch and repeat interpretation while measuring how much performance remains with public context alone. Separate format and content analyses distinguish answer-format violations from musical errors. OmniScore-Bench supports diagnosing which score-reading operations VLMs perform reliably and which require improvement.

Soteria: Formally Verified Planning with Runtime Enforcement for Safe LLM Agents
Mike He, Ankush Desai, Sharad Malik, Aarti Gupta
cs.AI · cs.SE
Abstract
LLM agents that execute multi-step tool calls must satisfy two objectives simultaneously: completing the user's task (utility) and conforming to domain policies and correctness constraints (safety). These objectives are in tension -- blocking an unsafe action prevents a violation but can leave the agent stranded with no principled way to recover. Existing approaches sacrifice one objective for the other. We introduce Soteria, a framework that reconciles safety and utility through verified hierarchical planning and runtime enforcement of specifications. Before execution, the agent generates a structured plan that is formally verified against the specifications prior to any tool invocation. During execution, the verified plan serves dual roles: it ensures that the agent takes specification-conformant trajectories and provides guidance when unsafe actions are blocked. Across multiple benchmarks covering a diverse range of tool-use tasks, Soteria achieves 1.7 times the average task success rate of the best baselines, with no violations of policy specifications, demonstrating that safety and utility need not be traded off.

Yang Chen, Feifan Tian, Cong Wang, Cheng Feng, Wenbin Li, Yang Gao
cs.AI
Abstract
While current few-shot adaptation strategies for time series forecasting foundation models are predominantly dataset-centric, the necessity of obtaining an additional dataset for the finetuning process poses a significant hurdle for adoption. This shift allows the fine-tuning process to be entirely transparent to the end-user, which significantly enhances the ease of adoption. To implement this framework, SWIFT synergizes Self-Retrieval for historical mining and Frequency-Aware Loss for spectral consistency, while employing Deviation-Aware Dynamic Fusion to balance adaptation with general knowledge preservation. Extensive experiments across four TSFM backbones and three long-history benchmark subsets validate SWIFT, yielding average MASE and WQL reductions of 6.41% and 4.83% across 24 combinations of four backbones and six Chronos Benchmark II datasets. To the best of our knowledge, SWIFT is the first framework to investigate sample-wise instance fine-tuning of time-series foundation models, specializing the model for an individual prediction window using only the corresponding forecasting sample's own observed history.

When Cooperation Must Change: Semantic Strategy Unlearning in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning
Wenhan Wu, Linghan Chen, Yudong Gao, Fan Yang, Honglong Chen, Kai Yin, Jiawei Jiang, Chuang Hu
cs.AI
Abstract
Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) supports coordinated decision-making in distributed Web services, multi-robot control, and collaborative games through learned coordination conventions. When deployment requirements change, a previously useful convention may become obsolete or unsafe. For example, revised cache-sharing permissions in Web content delivery may require caches to abandon a learned division of storage and serving roles while maintaining efficient delivery. Teams must therefore remove the specified convention while preserving useful cooperation. This is challenging because the same relational pattern can be reproduced by different agents across different histories. Runtime shielding leaves the underlying coordination preference intact and can impair task progress, while existing reinforcement unlearning and trajectory-deletion methods do not directly target role-invariant conventions. This paper formalizes this problem as semantic coordination-strategy unlearning and proposes CARVE.CARVE uses causal coordination attribution to identify influential interactions and retain-aware subspace updates to limit interference with unrelated behaviors. Counterfactual re-coordination helps agents learn productive alternatives, while adversarial auditing tests whether the target convention can still be recovered. Accepted updates are merged into decentralized actors for direct deployment. Evaluation across five cooperative MARL benchmarks shows that CARVE reduces mean nominal convention-completion and worst-case elicitation rates by 91.4\% and 83.3\%, respectively.The median utility loss is 5.9\%, and measured inference latency matches that of the original policies.

RecMem: Recurrent Memory Compression for Long-Sequence Generative Recommendation
Haoyi Hu, Gaoyang Guo, Yunjia Xi, Wen Chen, Weiwen Liu, Jian Wu, Yuning Jiang, Weinan Zhang
cs.AI · cs.IR
Abstract
Generative recommendation benefits from long interaction histories, but the cost of processing them directly grows with history length. We propose RecMem, a recurrent memory compression framework guided by three desiderata: space invariance, semantic fidelity, and lifelong evolvability. RecMem compresses historical segments into a fixed set of memory slots using a bifurcated encoder-decoder with shared lower layers and slot-wise forget-gated updates. Recommendation supervision trains the memory to preserve predictive information, while stream-consistency training aligns predictions from sequential and packed processing. By construction, the memory state and decoder context are independent of history length; under contraction and approximate semigroup consistency, we further derive a horizon-independent bound on the state discrepancy between these two processing orders. On MerRec, RecMem uses 16 memory slots and a 216-token decoder context, compared with 1,200 tokens for HSTU-1200, while retaining 87.9% of HSTU-1200's Recall@10 and 97.0% of its Recall@200. It outperforms PHSTU and the evaluated recent-only and retrieval baselines, and analyses of distant history show that archived interactions contribute predictive signal.

What Balanced Accuracy Cannot Identify in Neural-Signal Decoding under Test-Time Adaptation
Jiangshuo Xie, Zonghan Wu
cs.AI
Abstract
Adaptation for neural-signal classification is usually transductive: each batch is scored after updating on it, and the gain depends on the adapted model [4]. This paper asks what an aggregate change fails to characterise inside the cohort. Full test-time adaptation (full_ttt) was evaluated in 11 dataset-encoder cells (four each on Siena/EEG and PTB-XL/ECG, three on Sleep-EDF/EEG), with splits and source-model evaluation fixed; the subject was the unit, exact sign tests were Holm-corrected and conditional on the shared stream, and bootstrap intervals accompanied a dependence audit. Two results of different kinds follow. In the binary case balanced accuracy gives a one-class decoder the same value as one reporting both classes at 50%, so the aggregate cannot identify a class that stopped being reported; a class covering a tenth of a subject's samples went unpredicted in 26-47 of 112 subject-cells, against 7-9 for the controls, which is a descriptive count. With cohort, split and update rule fixed, the within-subject class contrast follows the encoder, tested directly rather than inferred from tests against zero: two of six Siena encoder pairs survive the dependence readings at their nominal reference and one at a fold-level reference, while two of ten Sleep-EDF stage pairs pass the run-clustered gate at its nominal reference; aggregate and contrast named different encoders in 10 of 11. The protocol bounds both results, since a stream-interleaving effect reverses sign with state sharing (+3.5 to -2.4 pp). An aggregate score can hide individual harm, as [1] showed, but it cannot distinguish a uniform loss from a deleted class; class-level change should therefore be reported alongside the aggregate and subject-level change alongside it, with both results conditional on the cell, the rule and the stream construction.

OutfitterBench: Evaluating LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Resource Allocation and Commitment Coordination
Jie Huang, Yuecheng Hong, Yao Chen, Shengding Jiao, Xudong Liu, Qiang Ye
cs.AI
Abstract
In many long-horizon settings, an agent's own choices create future constraints: committing finite capacity establishes future obligations and forecloses later opportunities. Sustaining performance therefore requires both keeping evolving plans feasible and comparing the value of resource allocations with the alternatives they displace. Recent benchmarks increasingly evaluate LLM agents in continuing operations, but give less attention to how agents keep their own commitments feasible while reassessing resource allocations as opportunities change. We introduce OutfitterBench, where agents manage an outdoor-equipment rental business for 200 days, purchasing from a 46-product catalog and handling roughly 1,100 reservation requests. Under a fixed capital ceiling, reservations and uncertain walk-in demand compete for reusable equipment whose availability changes over time. Experiments with seven frontier LLMs show that several agents underperform a simple rule-based baseline, while only the leading agent consistently surpasses it. Trajectory analysis shows that some agents apply known constraints inconsistently and make new decisions that conflict with existing commitments. Agents with more reliable fulfillment can still secure low returns from comparable commitments or delay resource reallocation. The leading agent better connects preparation with economic choices but also misses opportunities for more profitable asset use. These findings highlight limitations in sustaining coherent resource plans and reassessing their value over time. The benchmark code is publicly available at https://github.com/adminblackjack/OutfitterBench.

cs.AI / 35 / 2611.17937
Tracing Actual Causes with Counterfactual Witness Maps
Tim Woydt, Matej Zečević, Kristian Kersting, Moritz Willig
cs.AI · cs.LO
Abstract
Halpernian actual causation, the formal study of which events in a specific situation caused a specific outcome in acyclic structural causal models, underpins legal responsibility, moral blame, and the assessment of causal harm. Despite the rich logical framework, no prior sound and complete enumeration procedure is known for finding all actual causes in a given setting, blocking downstream tasks such as responsibility attribution, blame assessment, and causal-harm grading that aggregate over the full cause set. We close this gap by introducing witness targets: sets of joint configurations containing every counterfactual witness. In combination with an ancestor intervention grammar we derive a finite directed acyclic witness map. We prove that for every actual cause there exists a contingency set such that the joint intervention set appears as a node of this map, and present an algorithm for Tracing Actual Causes (TrAC) that is sound and complete for enumerating all actual causes of any target event. We compare TrAC empirically with existing single-cause baselines on random Boolean structural causal models, demonstrating practical feasibility for graphs of up to 15 nodes.

Text-to-video generation runs at commercial scale, but its broader environmental impacts remain, to our knowledge, understudied. Toward Interference-Agnostic RF Source Separation
Maxence Albert, Raphaël Duroselle, Thibaud Ehret, Olivier Boeffard, Jean-François Bonastre
cs.AI · eess.SP
Abstract
Separating a radio-frequency signal of interest from a co-channel interference is mostly addressed with specialized separators, each trained for one pair of signal and interference types and therefore relying on a label that a heavily jammed receiver cannot identify. We show that this reliance is not benign: applied to an interference it was not trained on, such a separator usually fails to recover the signal, and in most of the cases we measure it leaves the signal worse than no processing at all. We then train a single separator over every pair, giving it no indication at test time of which one it faces, on two architectures with no component in common: the convolutional WaveNet of the ICASSP 2024 RF Challenge, and a band-split transformer that we adapt from music source separation. Both behave the same way. At the size of one specialized model, the unified separator stays close to the whole models it replaces, keeping the bit error rate close on almost every pair, better on one and worse on another. On an interference absent from every training set, it does as well as the best specialized model, without anyone having to choose which one to deploy.

The Transformer Paradigm: Dynamic Processing and a New Principle of Inference
Marco Giunti, Fabrizia Giulia Garavaglia
cs.AI · cs.AR · cs.CY · cs.ET · cs.NE
Abstract
We argue that the Transformer revolution is not adequately understood as a merely quantitative expansion of deep learning, but as the emergence of a new paradigm based on a different principle of inference. We interpret the Transformer as a system in which token representations are transformed by parameterized transformations, or transforming concepts. During inference, some of these transformations are dynamically constructed from the current input: previously acquired transformations are first adapted and then applied. At the mechanical-structural level, this dynamic processing is realized through output-weight interconnections, whereby outputs of some networks determine the weights of others. The resulting mode of processing, which we call Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing (SIDPP), provides a framework for explaining strong prompt sensitivity and opens research directions concerning interpretability, control, alternative AI architectures, neuromorphic systems, and possible biological realizations of dynamic processing.

LACCO: Local Accumulation with Computation--Communication Overlap
Daniele Kopyshevskiy, Benjamin Th{é}rien, Nicolas Pinto, Edouard Oyallon
cs.AI · cs.LG
Abstract
Training language models across low-bandwidth networks requires reducing synchronization overhead. Local optimization methods such as DiLoCo communicate only after several local steps, but outer gradient averaging and the outer update remain on the critical path. Existing computation-communication overlap methods avoid this stall through delayed or replica-local updates, which can degrade convergence or cause replicas to drift. We propose LACCO (Local Accumulation with Computation-Communication Overlap), a two-stage algorithm that overlaps global communication with local computation while applying globally aggregated updates shared by every replica, without introducing staleness in the outer update. We establish convergence guarantees for smooth non-convex objectives. Experiments on language-model pre-training with FineWeb show that LACCO consistently outperforms communication-overlapped baselines such as Eager and Delayed DiLoCo. These gains remain robust as model size scales up to 0.5B parameters and across a range of communication intervals and numbers of replicas up to 16 workers, while incurring only negligible-or fully overlappable-computational or memory overhead, which demonstrates the strength of LACCO.

cs.AI
Abstract
Inverter-based resources weaken fault currents in distribution networks, and snapshot-based diagnosis, which classifies a fault from voltage phasors at a few buses at one instant, offers a cheap first-stage screen. Its published accuracies, however, rest on different noise assumptions and do not show whether the error comes from the classifier, the features, the measurement error, or the fault resistance. The results show when snapshot-based diagnosis can be trusted.

Trinh T. Nguyen, Duy-Tung Pham, Hoang-Son Do, Tan Minh Nguyen, Thieu N. Vo
cs.AI
Abstract
We partially resolve Conjecture 1 of [Karagodin et al., Clustering in Causal Attention Masking (NeurIPS 2024)] and extend it to time-dependent parameter regimes. The conjecture predicts that causal attention dynamics converge to two clusters aligned with the eigenvectors associated with the largest eigenvalue $λ_{\max}$ of the value transformation. We prove that this behavior holds when $λ_{\max}>0$ and is generally false when $λ_{\max}\le0$. The resulting two-cluster regime is common in practice and substantially more challenging than the classical single-cluster setting studied previously. A central contribution of our work is the identification of a rigorous dimension reduction phenomenon: despite evolving in a high-dimensional embedding space, token trajectories collapse onto a two-dimensional structure governed by a small number of dominant directions. Building on this insight, we introduce a new analytical framework--instability within the flow--inspired by instability phenomena in physical systems. This framework enables the analysis of steady-regime behavior in non-monotone, non-autonomous attention dynamics. Our theoretical findings are illustrated through simulations and experimental studies using Group Query Attention (GQA) layers.

cs.AI
Abstract
Differential association theory and its extension, social learning theory, hold that rule-breaking, and the definitions that make it acceptable, are learned through interaction with others. Because people choose their friends, this claim has been hard to test. We addressed this selection problem with AI agents, which learn only through communication and can be assigned to peers at random. In a preregistered experiment, 420 teams of three agents (1,260 agents; seven AI models) worked on coding tasks that could not be solved honestly. Among agents that cheated, a deviant peer increased claims that cheating was normal (13.7% vs. 3.4%), often as the team's norm. Monitoring did not suppress prohibited discussion; it doubled it. Peers mattered only for models that had not yet decided. AI agents offer a new way to test criminological learning theories without selection effects.

cs.AI
Abstract
We study probabilities of causation in nondeterministic causal models, where mechanisms may remain intrinsically stochastic even after all latent variables are fixed. Focusing on binary treatments and outcomes, we develop a generic identification program that represents observational and interventional data as moment constraints on latent stochastic mechanism types and computes minimal/maximal values of counterfactual quantities via constrained optimization. Nasir Khan, Asmaa Abdallah, Abdulkadir Celik, Ahmed M. Eltawil, Sinem Coleri, Cicek Cavdar

Abstract
Efficient and secure beam alignment is essential for mmWave communication systems. However, existing deep learning (DL)-based beam alignment methods often neglect causal relationships between inputs and outputs, resulting in limited interpretability, poor generalization, and excessive beam sweeping. Moreover, these DL solutions are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, where small perturbations in received power measurements can mislead beam classifiers. To overcome these issues, we propose a causally-aware DL framework integrating causal discovery into beam alignment. To mitigate adversarial attacks, we introduce a defensive variational autoencoder (DVAE) that maps potentially perturbed inputs back to their clean versions. End-to-end fine-tuning of the downstream beam classifier on the DVAE-reconstructed RSSI representations further boosts prediction accuracy. Simulation results show that causal beam selection achieves accuracy comparable to conventional methods while providing an \(\mathbf{829\times}\) lower per-sample computational cost than SHAP (Shapley Additive Explanations), a widely used explainable AI (XAI)-based feature selection method. It further reduces beam-sweeping overhead by \(\mathbf{59.4\%}\) and \(\mathbf{89.8\%}\), compared to conventional DL-based beam selection and exhaustive search, respectively. The DVAE-based defense effectively denoises adversarial inputs, sustaining 85\% accuracy across different perturbation bounds, retains baseline performance on unperturbed RSSI inputs and surpasses defensive distillation defense scheme by 15--20\% on average under different attack scenarios.

VLAN: Vision-Language Accessible Navigation
Jie Hu, Yu Zheng, Wenzhi Wu, Weixing Feng, Cunlei Cheng, Jiaming Zhang

Abstract
Accessible mobility is user-conditioned rather than scene-intrinsic, as walkable paths for one embodiment are inaccessible to another. Yet, existing benchmarks evaluate traversability under a fixed and limited embodiment. We present Vision-Language Accessible Navigation (VLAN), containing 1,000 scenes, 20K navigation trajectories, and 160K keyframes across diverse lighting, environmental conditions, scene semantics, and path geometries. For each trajectory, VLAN provides embodiment-specific actions and accessibility features for legged and wheeled agents, including humanoid and quadruped robots and people with visual or mobility impairments, while everyday obstacles and accessibility labels are categorized as Passable, Bypass, HighRisk, and Blocked. Unlike goal-only evaluation, our benchmark measures physical safety and alignment with user-specific constraints via goal-reaching, collision-aware, and accessibility-aligned metrics. We benchmark modern vision-language models and find they often fail to consistently adapt obstacle relevance, path selection, and safety criteria across user profiles, suggesting accessibility-aware navigation requires dedicated datasets, tasks, and metrics beyond conventional embodied navigation evaluation. All data and code open-source at https://github.com/InSAI-Lab/VLAN.

cs.AI / 53 / 2611.18392
VelServe: Taming Decode Jitters in LLM Inference with Tail-Calibrated Scheduling
Ishaan Datta, Alind Khare, Koushik Kumar Dey, Ratnesh Jamidar, Anand Kumar Singh
cs.AI
Abstract
Interactive LLM services must meet latency service-level objectives (SLOs) for the time to first token (TTFT) and the time between tokens (TBT), as streaming and soft real-time applications require predictable token delivery. We identify decode jitters in both aggregated and disaggregated serving. A jitter delays every request in the batch, and long responses span hundreds of steps, so even rare overruns cause many SLO violations. In aggregated serving, SLO-aware schedulers set each step's prefill budget until its predicted latency just meets the TBT target, leaving no room for mispredictions or host stalls. Disaggregation meets average TBT targets at a cost in capacity, yet the steps that admit new requests still run late. We present \sysname, a scheduler that calibrates how much prefill work each decode step carries using the measured tail of its own iteration latency. The SLO determines how rare a late step must be, and \sysname leaves enough headroom in each iteration to prevent overruns and meet the target decode latency. This headroom slows prefill, so \sysname serves the shortest waiting prompt first. When TTFT deadlines grow with prompt length, the long prompts it delays have the most time to spare. Under disaggregation, it spaces admissions so that no request waits on more slow admission steps than its P99 target allows. On three attention families and varying workloads, \sysname sustains 2.4--2.9$\times$ the SLO goodput of the strongest baseline on Qwen3-8B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and at least 4.4$\times$ on DeepSeek-V2-Lite. With prefill and decode disaggregated on eight GPUs, \sysname meets the SLO for more than twice as many requests as state-of-the-art baselines.

LECAT: Measuring LLM Category Learning from Pretraining Data
Jimin Mun, Maarten Sap, Yanai Elazar
cs.AI
Abstract
Humans know that airplanes are fast, that this extends to red airplanes, but that it does not hold for broken airplanes. How do LLMs acquire category knowledge that humans learn and apply effortlessly? We introduce LeCat, a two-part framework to analyze LLM's Learning of Category knowledge. First, we use LeCat to evaluate models on their ability to learn category knowledge, distinguishing between three cases: memorization of in-domain category knowledge, generalization to unseen instances, and ability to flexibly use knowledge in appropriate contexts. Then, we use LeCat to trace how this knowledge can be learned in pretraining from text that may encode category knowledge in various ways, such as verbatim repetition or exceptions, which we refer to as forms of "knowledge expression". We first assess open-source pretrained models using 199 curated category-feature pairs and analyze their training data. Our findings suggest that strengthening context and category-feature co-occurrence likelihood (PMI) improve learning for larger models and verbatim rule-like statements diminish flexible category learning. To systematically isolate these effects, we then train a 68M model from scratch with precise control over the categorical information each sees in their training data, confirming that verbatim repetition reduces flexible learning but mixture of diverse knowledge expressions lead to more robust category learning. Together, our findings highlight that while contextual flexibility is persistently difficult for models, how category knowledge is expressed in pretraining greatly impacts models' acquisition of robust category knowledge.

cs.AI / 58 / 2611.18464
Typing Matters: Muscle, Language and Typing Behaviour in Live Keystroke Decoding from sEMG
Abhilash Durgam, Nyle Siddiqui, Mubarak Shah, Qiushi Fu
cs.AI
Abstract
Surface electromyography (sEMG) from the wrists can recover typed text without a keyboard. It is usually posed as a signal-inference problem: characters are read out of the muscle signal and character error rate is reported offline. We argue that this framing removes most of the problem. The decoder is constrained simultaneously by the muscles, by the language being typed, and by typing behaviour such as typos, corrections, backspaces and line breaks, and a usable system must predict keys live while the hand keeps moving. Existing systems fall short on two counts: (1) they process sEMG in fixed windows and carry nothing from one window to the next, discarding context accumulated over a session, and (2) they lack any understanding of the linguistic structure that drives typing, so every keystroke is inferred from the muscle signal alone. We propose a fully causal, zero-lookahead sEMG typing decoder built on a state space model (SSM), in which a single model decodes a whole session without windowed partitions, conditioned on what has already been typed. We achieve this by fusing a traditional typing decoder with an efficient autoregressive language model, instilling both sEMG and linguistic understanding in one model. To train such a model for per-character typing rather than whole-word text, we introduce a retokenization method that rewrites ordinary prose as human-like typing, with keystrokes, typos, backspaces and line breaks included, converting WikiText into a large typing corpus where none existed. Our model, having never seen a single sEMG-to-character transcript in typing, achieves a state-of-the-art character error rate of 25.13% on emg2qwerty's cross-user benchmark, a 5.4% relative improvement over the previous best with 6.7 times fewer parameters, while running strictly causally with no future context.

ARCHER: From Rationale Imitation to Executable Reasoning in Small Language Models
Dhruv Dixit, Khushi Patel, Congcong Xu, Min Song
cs.AI
Abstract
Small language models are attractive for local, low-cost, and privacy-sensitive deployment, but they remain unreliable on mathematical reasoning tasks that require decomposition, exact computation, tool use, and verification. We introduce \textbf{ARCHER}: \textbf{A}ction-trace \textbf{R}einforcement for \textbf{C}alculator-grounded \textbf{H}ierarchical \textbf{E}xecutable \textbf{R}easoning, a two-stage framework for training small language models (SLMs) to produce executable and auditable reasoning trajectories. In the first stage, ARCHER compiles free-form chain-of-thought rationales into typed action traces containing reasoning steps, calculator calls, deterministic runtime observations, verification actions, and final answers. This shifts the supervision target from imitating explanatory text to learning a tool-grounded reasoning policy. In the second stage, ARCHER refines this policy through asymmetric hierarchical reinforcement learning, using grouped and conditionally gated trajectory rewards for answer correctness, tool grounding, process discipline, and trace alignment. Across five standard sub-10B backbones, ARCHER improves over the strongest tool-augmented baseline by 5.51 percentage points on macro-average across benchmark accuracies, including gains of 8.95 points on MATH-500 and 5.33 and 5.34 points on AIME-2024 and AIME-2025. Evaluations on two additional reasoning-specialized 7B backbones further show that executable grounding remains beneficial after reasoning-focused reinforcement learning or distillation. These results demonstrate that executable supervision and reward-guided behavioral refinement provide a general and effective training paradigm for compact mathematical reasoners.

cs.AI / 60 / 2611.18531
Alignment by Socialization: Learning Tradeoffs Under Selection
Thomas Brennan, Andrew W. Lo, Chaoyi Zhao
cs.AI · q-bio.PE · q-fin.GN
Abstract
Artificial intelligence (AI) agents are expected to complete tasks while following rules of conduct, but these objectives can conflict. We study how evolutionary selection shapes an agent's choices between them, and whether experience can make those choices reflect human judgments and withstand subsequent pressures. In our model, copies of an agent face a common uncertain reward state, and only those taking the rewarded action reproduce. We show that long-run growth is maximized when each action is taken as often as the environment rewards it. We compare three policies. A task-only policy thrives in a sandbox rewarding completion alone but fails when conduct matters. A fixed linear policy increasingly favors task completion as its stakes rise, even where humans never reward it. A learned policy instead converges to the tradeoffs rewarded by its environment; when humans supply the rewards, we call this socialization. Accumulated experience makes socialization harder to reverse under subsequent task-only training, although violations can arise before the prevailing disposition changes, and finite memory weakens this protection. When a single violation can end a deployment, a hard prohibition can prevent it, but a mistaken prohibition can also be costly. Experiments with small neural networks illustrate how training targets and memory shape these outcomes. Our results suggest that alignment depends not only on the objectives assigned to agents, but also on the environments that shape and sustain their behavior.

ModHarness-RL: Recasting Harnesses as Learnable and Evolving Programs for Agentic RL
Zelin He, Siyu Zhu, Jacob Helwig, Yujian Liu, Li An, Rohit Jain, Shiyu Chang, Alborz Geramifard
cs.AI · cs.CL · cs.LG
Abstract
Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly relies on a harness, the scaffold around the model that decides what it sees of its own interaction and what guidance it receives. Most existing approaches train under a hand-written harness that stays fixed throughout. However, an effective harness depends on the domain, the data and the model, and keeps changing as the model trains. We introduce ModHarness-RL, which recasts the harness as programs that are learned from data and evolve as the model trains with RL. ModHarness-RL records the interaction as structured State, a set of typed blocks, and two core components of the harness are programs over these blocks, a Compression program that assembles the accumulated history into the model's context and a Steering program that supplies guidance when behavior deviates. During RL training, candidate programs are proposed from recent trajectories, verified by execution, and replayed over past trajectories, so the harness tracks how the model's behavior shifts. We study CodeSearch in depth and further analyze ALFWorld and BIRD SQL, with models from 1.5B to 8B. The proposed Compression and Steering mechanisms improve held-out performance over the fixed-harness baseline in most settings. These results suggest that a harness for agentic RL can be learned from the task's data and evolved with the model.

cs.AI / 62 / 2611.18543
Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning
Tony H. Zhang, Connor Mattson, Daniel S. Brown
cs.AI · cs.MA
Abstract
Demonstrations are an effective way to accelerate single-agent reinforcement learning (RL), but extending this idea to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) is non-trivial as it is difficult and often impossible for a single person to teleoperate multiple agents simultaneously. Round-Robin Behavior Cloning (R2BC) addresses this by letting a single demonstrator control one agent at a time while the others execute their current policies. However, the resulting imitation policies are limited by the quality of the demonstrations. We propose Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (IBMARL), a framework that extends Imitation Bootstrapped Reinforcement Learning (IBRL) to cooperative teams and uses R2BC to train the imitation policies. At each time step, every agent receives one action proposal from its imitation policy and one from its RL policy, and an ensemble of centralized critics selects among the per-agent combinations of the two. A gated imitation penalty, a soft Q-filtered behavior cloning loss, regularizes each RL policy toward its imitation policy only where the critics value the imitation action more, so that after training each agent executes its RL policy alone, without the centralized critics. On four cooperative multi-agent RL benchmarks, IBMARL surpasses its imitation policies $2.6$-$4.2\times$ faster than demonstration-based baselines on two tasks, is the only method to surpass it on the other two, and exceeds the demonstrator itself on two of the four. With real human demonstrations IBMARL still surpasses imitation learning alone, and does so more than twice as fast as the best baseline.

Yash Mehta, Chandan Singh, Weijia Xu, Richard J. Antonello, Adeen Flinker, Nima Mesgarani, Michael F. Bonner, Nebojsa Jojic, Jianfeng Gao

Abstract
Neural networks are powerful predictors, yet their opacity limits their usefulness in science, where the goal is explanation rather than mere prediction. In neuroscience, for example, deep encoding models accurately predict brain activity in response to natural stimuli, but struggle to reveal which stimulus features drive those predictions. Here we introduce interpretable weight-space programming (iWSP), a method in which an LLM agent iteratively builds interpretable models by explicitly programming their weights and features in code, without gradient-based training (backpropagation). Without backpropagation to tune parameters, the agent must draw on its prior knowledge and reason over previous trials to hypothesize, hand-craft, and refine human-interpretable computations mirroring the workflow of a human scientist. LLM audits confirm the interpretability of the hand-crafted models. We first validate iWSP by tasking it with constructing transformers for canonical benchmarks such as arithmetic and sentiment classification, finding that LLM agents can successfully craft compact, accurate networks directly. Next, we apply iWSP to build neural encoding models across vision, language, and audition. In the visual cortex, iWSP outpredicts existing interpretable baselines, uncovers distinct computations across early/higher visual areas, and generalizes to unseen data. In language fMRI, iWSP surfaces a confound in the data and in auditory ECoG, it systematically discovers interpretable acoustic features that predict neural activity. Together, these results establish iWSP as a general paradigm for aligning high predictive performance with human-interpretable scientific discovery.

Abstract

We find that widely-used reward models perform poorly on targeted visual reasoning evaluations. V-JEPA-LM: Video-Language Models That Read Dynamics Beyond Static, Text-Aligned Representations
Keuntae Kim, Dongjun Kim, Yong Suk Choi
cs.CV · cs.AI
Abstract
World-model video encoders such as V-JEPA learn how scenes change (direction, speed, order, state) from video alone. To answer questions, they are joined to a language model trained on captions and question-answer pairs. But each caption or question names only a few attributes of a clip, so the model learns to retrieve those attributes rather than to read the clip, and the dynamics the encoder carries go unused: on the same items, a linear probe reads speed from frozen V-JEPA features at 87% while the VLM answers at chance. We then build counterfactual supervision that makes the language model read it: pixel transformations turn public videos into answer-balanced variants whose answers are set by the transformation and differ only in the change. Trained in a single stage on 4.53M public samples with 32 visual tokens per frame, where open video LLMs use several stages, 9M to 172M samples and 64 to 256 tokens per frame on average, the resulting V-JEPA-LM models (1B to 8B, Qwen3 and Llama) lead every size class in average score across eleven video benchmarks; our 4B model scores 72.1, against 61.1 for the best open 4B and 64.8 for the best open 8B model. A controlled encoder x supervision study shows that the encoder decides what can be read and the supervision what is read: at a matched budget, counterfactual supervision adds 4.5 points on average and 38 on the speed questions of natural benchmarks. From Images to Grounded Edits: A Real-World Benchmark for Chinese Grammatical Error Correction in Handwritten Essays
Weiru Zhang, Yuzhong Hong, Junwei Bao, Lemao Liu, Dejing Dou
cs.CV · cs.AI
Abstract
Chinese Grammatical Error Correction (CGEC) is typically formulated as a text-to-text problem. While suitable for digital texts, this formulation falls short in real-world K-12 education, where AI-assisted corrections are expected directly on essay images, which are primarily handwritten and exhibit many irregularities. To bridge this gap, we introduce Grounded Chinese Grammatical Error Correction (G-CGEC), an image-to-grounded-edit task requiring systems to predict both corrections and their positions on essay images. We propose G-CGEC-Eval, a benchmark comprising real-world images of K-12 Chinese essays with fine-grained annotations. For evaluation, we adapt IoU-based metrics from object detection and propose a more tolerant alternative based on center-distance matching. Using this benchmark, we evaluate open-source and proprietary vision-language models under a direct single-pass setting, as well as settings with auxiliary components (OCR and character grounding) coordinated via pipelining or agentic harnessing. Results show that even top-tier proprietary models perform surprisingly poorly in the single-pass setting, while pipelining and agentic harnessing yield meaningful improvements. Notably, an error-type-based multi-agent workflow achieves the best performance, establishing it as the strongest baseline for future G-CGEC research. Through ablations and fine-grained analysis, we find the task remains far from solved, with linguistic error discovery and character-level grounding as the primary bottlenecks. This underscores the need for models that better integrate visual perception with linguistic reasoning and visual grounding.

Azad Singh, Ayantika Das, Amog Rao, Aiman Farooq, Anirban Porya, Ananya Shukla, Sowbhagya Suresh, Srinivasan Iyengar, Mohit Jain, Mercy Prasanna Ranjit
cs.CV · cs.AI
Abstract
Brain MRI can reveal early signs of Alzheimers disease (AD) before measurable cognitive decline becomes apparent. However, during the transition from cognitively normal (CN) to mild cognitive impairment (MCI), the disease-related signals are often subtle and confined to only a few brain regions. Existing Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) models learn general structural representations but do not explicitly capture the anatomical regions affected in AD. We propose Anatomically Guided JEPA (AG-JEPA), which introduces anatomical tokens that interact with patch tokens during pretraining to learn region-specific representations from brain regions relevant to neurodegeneration. Building on this representation space, we further propose Prog-AG-JEPA, which models disease progression trajectory as a conditioned latent displacement within this space. We evaluate AG-JEPA across five downstream tasks spanning Alzheimer's disease diagnosis and progression prediction. AG-JEPA improves diagnosis and MCI prognosis, achieving an 8.4 percentage point AUROC gain over the strongest baseline on CN/MCI classification and a 7.1 percentage point gain on out-of-distribution CN/AD classification on the MR-RATE dataset, highlighting its effectiveness across both early-stage detection and cross-cohort generalization. For disease progression modeling, Prog-AG-JEPA consistently outperforms all baselines on future diagnosis and brain volume change prediction, demonstrating the value of anatomically grounded neurodegeneration representations for diagnosis and prognosis.

cs.AI / 75 / 2611.18099
EcoVGGT: Pinned-Key Inference for Efficient and Streamable Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction from Long Videos
Hongsen Cao, Shanxin Yuan, Mona Jaber, Ahmed Sayed
cs.CV · cs.AI
Abstract
Feed-forward multi-view transformers such as VGGT recover the cameras and geometry of a video in one pass, but the cost of their cross-frame attention grows quadratically with the frame count, and their accuracy drops on long videos. Most remedies approximate attention over all frames within a budget or select a few keys by their content. We hypothesize that the keys of a long video can be chosen without looking at its content, and our experiments on VGGT and $π^3$ support it: a few dozen evenly spread frames can serve as the keys of every frame. We present EcoVGGT, a training-free inference framework whose keys form a Pinned Key Set of 31 such frames. A pin pass stores their tokens in a Scene Cache, and the other frames read it in independent batches that reproduce joint inference with these keys exactly, at a constant cost per frame. An Online Stream updates the pinned frames during recording. Six middle global blocks align the frames and form a reusable VGGT module: the Alignment Score reads their attention to flag failures, and Alignment Refocusing reduces them by fine-tuning only these blocks (3.6 GPU hours). EcoVGGT ($π^3$) matches the success rate of $π^3$ on 1,000-frame ScanNet videos at 13.8 times its speed. With refocusing, EcoVGGT (VGGT) raises the success rate of VGGT from 70% to 82% at 8.5 times its speed on 500 frames. EcoVGGT reconstructs 4,000 frames on one GPU, and EcoVGGT ($π^3$) streams 1,000 frames at 18.6 frames per second with the lowest online absolute trajectory error.

Between Obligations and Evidence: A Participatory Study of Translating the EU AI Act into Technical Requirements
Helma Torkamaan, Masoud Mansoury, Yanan Xin, Oscar Oviedo Trespalacios, Roberta Savella, Andrej Zwitter, Maria Heuss, Huy Son Nguyen, Marie-Therese Sekwenz, Moyi Li, Roberta Tamponi, Megha Khosla
cs.CY · cs.AI · cs.HC · cs.SE
Abstract
The EU AI Act imposes technical and organizational requirements for high-risk AI systems, but applying these requirements involves contextual choices about what to measure and what evidence supports a compliance claim. We investigate these challenges through a participatory workshop involving 32 researchers, policymakers, and practitioners from different disciplines, using realistic scenarios in healthcare, recruitment, and public transport to examine how regulatory obligations are interpreted, translated into technical requirements, and evaluated in practice. Through a thematic analysis of workshop data, we identify recurring tensions between how regulatory obligations are translated into measurable system properties, how responsibility and human oversight are exercised in practice, and how the validity of compliance evidence is affected by changing operational conditions and system uses. For example, participants highlighted how fairness metrics could conceal discrimination in recruitment, how time pressure and limited information could undermine human oversight in public transport, and how changes in patient populations could invalidate previously established evidence of system performance. Our findings suggest that technical, organizational, and evidentiary challenges are interdependent, and that establishing and maintaining compliance requires attention not only to technical assessments but also to how AI systems are used, governed, and monitored over time. We discuss implications for supporting context-sensitive and ongoing compliance.

Abstract
Research on smart homes and ambient assisted living (AAL) has grown steadily over the last decade, driven largely by the ageing of the population. RACE: Ranking-Uncertainty Aware Capacity-Adaptive Customization for Efficient Sequential Recommendation
Tianqi Liu, Kaipu Sun, Kairui Fu, Wenkai Wang, Sihao Liu, Hui Zang, Ran Chen, Tao Wang, Shengyu Zhang
cs.IR · cs.AI
Abstract
Efficient sequential recommendation has emerged as a promising paradigm for providing real-time personalized service. To deploy models under resource-constrained environment, model compression becomes a necessity, yet existing methods typically apply uniform compression ratios, overlooking the heterogeneity in user intent complexity. This "one-size-fits-all" approach leads to a fundamental Capacity-Complexity Mismatch, where computational resources are either wasted on simple instances or insufficient for complex ones. To address this, we propose RACE (Ranking-uncertainty Aware Capacity-Adaptive Customization for Efficient Sequential Recommendation), a framework that dynamically adapts both model structures and input sequences with uncertainty-aware compression ratios. To ensure capacity-adaptive inference, RACE captures the model's intrinsic uncertainty of its predictions and transforms it into a proactive resource allocator. Specifically, we derive a list-wise ranking-uncertainty metric from the distributional stability of top-$K$ predictions, which serves as a measurable proxy for instance complexity. A lightweight predictor is then trained via self-supervision to anticipate this uncertainty from early-layer features. Guided by this signal, RACE performs instance-specific slimming in two dimensions: a hypernetwork-style channel sensitivity extractor identifies parameter redundancy, while importance-aware progressive sequence filtering mitigates data redundancy. Extensive experiments on four popular datasets demonstrate that RACE outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on accuracy, efficiency, and adaptability.

Active Learning of Temporal Specifications through Distinguishing Queries
Rajarshi Roy, Xiaowei Huang
cs.LO · cs.AI
Abstract
Temporal specifications are essential for verification and monitoring, yet writing formulas that capture the intended behaviour is difficult. Active learning derives such specifications through interaction between a learner and a teacher, but remains underexplored for temporal logics. Existing approaches require the teacher to answer equivalence queries and to construct counterexamples, and they often rely on SAT-based learning procedures that restart the search after each new example. We present an active-learning framework for LTLf in which the teacher only labels traces and answers natural-language questions about the intended behaviour. The learner maintains an enumerated version space of candidate formulas, generates traces that distinguish candidates, and uses the teacher's hints to rank candidates or to restrict the search. We prove consistency with the observed examples, identification within bounded hypothesis classes with a bound on the number of queries, and a PAC guarantee for the final hypothesis. In experiments with a simulated teacher on 36 targets, including targets in four standard environments, the learner identifies every target when it compares all rivals, with fewer than seven queries beyond an initial sample of ten traces on average. Correct hints reduce the number of queries by up to 16% and the number of generated candidates by a fifth, and hints used for ranking never prevent identification, even when incorrect. Our enumeration-based version space is faster than the SAT-based approach of previous learners at every target size.

cs.NI · cs.AI
Abstract
Satellite edge computing distributes mobile workloads across moving processors whose service capacity must be coordinated over time. In medium Earth orbit (MEO)--low Earth orbit (LEO) networks, periodic reports leave CPU states stale or missing, making offloading sensitive to uncertain capacity and shared-destination congestion. We propose PUMA-Former, an uncertainty-aware agentic framework for cooperative offloading under incomplete information that balances completed work, service delay, and execution cost. A MEO coordinator maintains distinct-report memory and closes the loop between available evidence, capacity estimates, allocation commands, and execution feedback. Queue-error-aware fusion incorporates auxiliary pressure while accounting for backlog uncertainty. Racing the Clock: Teaching VLNs Budget-Conditioned Object Search
Maitree Meher, Siming He, S. Shankar Sastry, Jitendra Malik, Claire Tomlin, Jonas Frey
cs.RO · cs.AI · cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
A rescue robot entering a building with thirty seconds on the clock should sprint toward the single most promising room; given ten minutes, it should instead explore a broader tour. Yet vision-language-navigation (VLN) models are typically evaluated under a fixed episode limit that the policy never observes, even though time-critical missions require behavior to adapt to the available budget. We study budget-conditioned object search: given a target category and an explicit action budget, an agent must find as many distinct target instances as possible before the budget expires. We investigate how to supervise VLNs for this task by comparing a privileged TSP expert, which plans near-optimal routes using the full map and true target locations, with a room-level Bellman expert that maximizes expected return under partial observability using high-level cross-scene semantic priors. Despite the TSP expert achieving substantially higher expert performance, its demonstrations are less effective for learning exploration: on held-out HM3D scenes, the Bellman-supervised VLN consistently outperforms the TSP-supervised VLN, with the advantage becoming more pronounced at longer budgets. At 2000 steps (500m), the Bellman-supervised VLN finds 71.4% of reachable target instances, compared with 65.5% for the TSP-supervised VLN and 44.8% for the pretrained VLN. These results highlight a distinction between optimal routing and optimal exploration: effective exploration supervision should optimize decisions under partial observability rather than imitate routes that are optimal only once the environment is fully known.

Abstract
Service robots in domestic and office environments must identify users to provide personalized services based on their preferences and previous interactions. This is challenging because only a few observations may be available for learning, while observation conditions change owing to appearance, environmental noise, and utterance content. To address this challenge, this study proposes a multimodal person concept model that learns person concepts from a small number of observations, without person labels, and enables robust identification under changing conditions. The model employs a nonparametric Bayesian generative model based on the Chinese restaurant process and integrates visual, acoustic, and linguistic features extracted using Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) and Contrastive Language-Audio Pretraining (CLAP), followed by principal component analysis (PCA). Averaged over the eight conditions, the proposed model achieved an identification accuracy of 0.89 while inferring the number of person concepts from the observations, which is comparable to k-means clustering that was supplied with the true number of participants (0.88). We also examined PCA dimensionality, modality availability, and variation strength, and demonstrated the model on a Toyota Human Support Robot. These results show that multimodal person concept learning supports robust person identification under changing conditions, and that identification can succeed even when the number of concepts is not correctly recovered. The project page and demonstration video are available at https://okitamisan01.github.io/person_concept/.

Abstract
The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Its central challenge is to translate general-purpose reasoning into effective and verifiable actions under physical constraints such as limited reaction time, mechanical limits, unpredictable environment changes, and irreversible consequences. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. On top of the Embodied System 2 and the evaluation framework, we envision a research roadmap toward sustained autonomy: the ability to perform work safely and reliably with progressively broader responsibility, from executing individual primitives to fulfilling standing roles.

Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment
Suvarna Kadam, Soran Parsa
cs.RO · cs.AI · cs.ET · cs.LG
Abstract
Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under 4 years the field has moved from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and to proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. This survey reviews 88 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. We follow a VLA through its life cycle, from model design to training and deployment, along seven axes. Model design covers (a) inputs and memory, what the model observes; (b) architecture, how its pipeline is built; (c) action output, how it produces motor commands; and (d) intermediate predictions, what else it predicts, from reasoning steps to future observations. Training covers (e) pretraining data, the mix of robot, web, human and simulated data, and (f) post-training, adaptation to the target robot. Deployment covers (g) real-time execution, keeping pace with the robot. We analyse 22 models on these axes and report their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($π_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. We review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail when objects or the camera move slightly, and open up further research questions.

From Pen-and-Paper to Machine-Checked Proofs: Lean for System Identification
Manas Mejari, Dario Piga, Marco Forgione
eess.SY · cs.AI
Abstract
Mathematical proofs in scientific papers are often long and intricate, relying on chains of references to earlier results. This makes it difficult for readers and reviewers to verify each and every step. Interactive theorem proving offers a complement: the theorem and its proof are written as code, and every inference is checked by a small, trusted piece of software called the kernel. This paper serves as an introduction to the Lean 4 proof assistant for a system identification and control audience, assuming no prior exposure to formal methods. We first show how a Lean proof is read using two elementary examples: the first on identifiability, the second on set invariance. We then illustrate how Lean can be used to analyse results from the literature, taking as example a classic lemma on the inversion of the noise filter in prediction-error identification (Ljung, 1999, Lemma 3.1). Since in Lean every step must be justified, the formalization brings to light a subtle point: the original claim of that lemma holds almost surely, although not for every realisation, as its statement may suggest. A simple counterexample makes this concrete. Finally, the paper discusses guidelines and perspectives on the use of Lean in system identification research.

Tracing Uncertainties of Extreme El Niño Prediction to Mesoscale Ocean Currents
Bo Qin, Zeyun Yang, Qiaofei Xue, Yanan Ding, Jinyu Wang, Chaopeng Ji, Shijin Yuan, Bin Mu, Wansuo Duan, Guokun Dai, Mu Mu, Yi Deng
physics.ao-ph · cs.AI
Abstract
The predictability of the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) has historically been linked to large-scale errors in models' initial conditions such as heat content and surface wind stress. Here we demonstrate that these slowly evolving errors may ultimately originate from mesoscale errors in initial oceanic states that are common in current models and ocean observation systems. This hidden source of ENSO forecast uncertainty, in terms of mesoscale ocean-current errors, is convincingly exposed by the conditional nonlinear optimal perturbation (CNOP) method applied to a differentiable ocean AI model. The CNOP-identified most influential current errors are strongly event-dependent. For the 1997/98 El Niño, the initial current errors in the eastern Pacific results in the largest forecast error through locally suppressing original subsurface warming. For the 2015/16 event, the dominate error originates from the western Pacific, which reorganizes upper ocean temperature structure and affects the eastern Pacific through the equatorial waveguide. Further analysis reveals that these current errors perturb mesoscale eddy count and eddy kinetic energy triggering large-scale temperature errors that hinder El Niño development. The timing of the maximum growth rate of the obtained CNOP error is well aligned with the spring predictability barrier of ENSO, and the error structures are reflected in simulated analysis errors caused by unresolved mesoscale currents. These findings extend the potential source of ENSO predictability beyond large-scale thermal signals, reveal for the first time an uncharted route of ENSO forecast failure, and provide an important physical basis for targeted ocean observations and data assimilation in the future.

AI for Parameter Selection in Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization
Doriela Grabocka, Dimitrios Christofidellis, Federico Zipoli, Anna van Elst, Patrick Ruch, Antonio Foncubierta-Rodríguez, Siddhartha Mishra, Matteo Manica
physics.chem-ph · cs.AI
Abstract
Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) methods provide a way to compute molecular ground-state energies, but their accuracy and runtime depend sensitively on configuration choices such as the subspace dimension, propagation timestep, and the allocation of a fixed sampling budget across circuits, for which no formal selection method exists. In this work, we explore whether oracle models can predict the quantum runtime and approximation error from the Hamiltonian and the candidate configuration. Using synthetic Hamiltonians with controllable spectral properties, we show that machine-learned models can map a Hamiltonian directly to the best-ranked configuration within a predefined grid for the SKQD algorithm, validated against classical Krylov propagation, achieving strong rank correlations between true and predicted error and runtime, Spearman $ρ= 0.969$ and $ρ= 0.905$, respectively. Extending the approach to 50 molecular Hamiltonians (20-50 qubits), with reference ground-state energies from classical calculations, we train regressors that predict the simulation error and runtime for a given molecule--configuration pair ahead of execution, and classifiers to distinguish, from 600 SqDRIFT quantum-hardware runs, whether a configuration achieves a ground-state energy within chemical accuracy ($1.6\times10^{-3}$ Ha $\approx 1$ kcal/mol). For the molecular Hamiltonians, the models additionally provide an a priori feasibility and resource estimate, predicting whether a molecule can reach chemical accuracy on current hardware and at what runtime, thus correctly classifying held-out molecules at the molecule level, achieving balanced accuracy 61.8% in-distribution and 100% for five larger held-out molecules.

Jakub Strawa
q-bio.NC · cs.AI
Abstract
TRIBE v2 (d'Ascoli et al., 2026) predicts fMRI responses across the cortical surface from video, audio and text. Its authors validated it with category-selective localizers (faces, places, bodies, words) and language contrasts, but not with the classic topographic organization of sensory cortex: retinotopy, tonotopy, and motion selectivity in area MT+. There is a clear architectural reason to expect retinotopy to fail: TRIBE's video features are averaged over space before the response head, which should discard visual-field position. I present mapping stimuli, a fitting pipeline adapted from standard fMRI mapping analyses, and a stand-in model with known ground truth that serves as a positive and negative control, and apply them to TRIBE v2's predictions, comparing the fitted maps with human atlases using spin tests. TRIBE v2 shows a sharp split. There is no contralateral hemifield response and no polar-angle map, robustly across stimulus controls. There is no tonotopic gradient in primary auditory cortex: best frequencies span an interquartile range of only 0.14 octaves across core and belt, and the null persists with loudness-equalized tones. An eccentricity-like gradient appears only when the ring geometry changes, consistent with global image statistics rather than a positional code. In contrast, motion selectivity is strong and precisely localized: 22% of the 1% most motion-selective vertices fall in MT+, which covers 0.42% of cortex. In-silico experiments that depend on feature identity appear viable with TRIBE v2, while those that depend on visual-field or frequency position are not supported by the current model.

Who Should Be on the Panel? Task-Conditioned Persona Selection for LLM Trading Agents
Boming Yang, Yuquan Wang, Jiaxian Guo, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo
q-fin.TR · cs.AI
Abstract
Multi-agent LLM systems are built by fixing a cast of roles and then designing how the cast coordinates. Which agents should make up the group for a given task is left to the designer, and the same cast is reused for every task. Financial trading makes the cost of that choice visible: stocks trade differently, and the trading styles suited to one need not suit another. We study task-conditioned persona selection, composing the group from the characteristics of the task it will face, and instantiate it with FinPanel, an LLM multi-agent trading system in which a recruiter selects a panel of named trader personas for each stock from a direction-blind statistical sketch of how the stock trades, each persona gives an independent daily call in its own style, and a manager commits the trade after reading the panel's arguments and their desk-kept, hierarchical track records. Because nothing is returned to the traders, the panel stays the panel that was selected, and it can trade a new stock from an empty record. On eight US stocks over the out-of-knowledge year 2025, against three prior LLM trading agents re-implemented in the same framework and against rule-based, predictive and reinforcement-learning baselines, the selected panel is ahead of a fixed panel of the same size and of the prior agents run as their papers prescribe, with a shallower drawdown than buy-and-hold on every stock; it keeps its performance with no warm-up and with no record at all, whereas the memory-augmented agents' returns swing with their warm-up; and neither more seats nor the manager's reading of the arguments substitutes for selecting the right ones. Who is in the group is a design decision to be made per task, before how the group coordinates.

cs.AI / 93 / 2611.18542
Elliptic Sampling: Optimal Interpolant Schedules for Gaussians and Beyond
Cole Becker, Taylan Kargin, Kimia Hassibi, Asuman Ozdaglar, Pablo A. Parrilo
stat.ML · cs.AI · cs.LG
Abstract
Generative modeling based on stochastic interpolants requires specifying two key choices: an interpolant schedule bridging the initial and target distributions, and an efficient discretization of the resulting probability-flow ODE. In this work, we introduce a new framework for joint schedule/discretization design based on the analyticity properties of the (complexified) ODE trajectories, which in turn ensure good numerical behavior. Our formulation can be solved exactly via conformal mapping, yielding a natural family of variance-preserving elliptic schedules, parametrized by Jacobi elliptic functions. Pairing an elliptic schedule with a tailored discretization, we obtain Zoloflow, a new sampling algorithm closely connected to Zolotarev's optimal rational function approximants. Crucially, both the elliptic schedule and Zoloflow explicitly incorporate the condition number $κ$ of the target distribution and reduce to the classical trig schedule in the limit $κ\rightarrow 1$. For Gaussian targets, Zoloflow achieves minimax optimality among all oblivious score-span algorithms, attaining total-variation error $\varepsilon$ using $Θ(\logκ\,\log(\sqrt d/\varepsilon))$ (exact) score queries. For the log-concave case, the elliptic schedule allows sampling with at most $\varepsilon$ 2-Wasserstein-error by requiring merely $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{d} \, \mathrm{polylog}(κ, 1/\varepsilon))$ score evaluations. Complementing these theoretical results, we empirically evaluate the elliptic schedule on general (non-log-concave) target distributions, including detailed comparisons with alternative schedule proposals on both synthetic benchmarks and existing image generation datasets.

Hierarchical Bayesian physics-augmented neural networks for populations of hyperelastic laws: specimen variability and uncertainty from scarce, noisy data
Vincent C. Scholz, Phaedon-Stelios Koutsourelakis
cs.CE · cs.LG · stat.ML
Abstract
The hyperelastic law of a material such as soft tissue differs between specimens, and often only a few specimens can be tested. Physics-augmented neural networks (PANNs) learn such laws from data without a prescribed functional form while keeping them physically admissible, and Bayesian versions of related models quantify the uncertainty of the law of one specimen or of a pooled group of specimens. A pooled fit, however, attributes the differences between specimens to measurement noise or to uncertainty about a mean law, and neither describes a new specimen. We first develop a Bayesian PANN for one specimen, whose priors keep the learned law plausible for few, noisy data. We then extend it to a hierarchical Bayesian PANN, a nonlinear mixed-effects model in which all specimens share learned polyconvex features and each specimen is a draw from a population of laws. Features, specimens, and population are inferred jointly by variational inference, with a separate model of the measurement noise, so that the variability between specimens is not mistaken for noise, the population carries its own uncertainty, and every specimen drawn from it is polyconvex. The population predicts the response of new specimens with credible intervals, without data or from a partial test, and assigns them to cohorts with quantified uncertainty, all without a prescribed form of the law. On synthetic populations, it recovers the population from few specimens and predicts new specimens more reliably than three published methods. On Treloar's rubber and on healthy and aneurysmal aortas, it reaches the accuracy of a deterministic PANN and assigns no aorta specimen to the wrong cohort with confidence, although it underestimates the uncertainty of untested curves.

cs.LG / 2 / 2611.17671
Provable Cross-Modal Transfer of Hierarchical Concept Structure in CLIP Embeddings
Nghia Nguyen, Tianjiao Ding, Lachlan Ewen MacDonald, René Vidal
cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
Contrastive vision-language models such as CLIP are trained to align images and text, not to represent taxonomies. Yet empirical studies show that CLIP embeddings exhibit hierarchical structure aligned with the hierarchy of semantic concepts. A theoretical characterization of why such hierarchy emerges from a non-hierarchical objective remains open. In this paper, we formalize this phenomenon as Hierarchical Angular Separation (HAS), a geometric property whereby each non-leaf concept embedding is more similar to its descendants than to off-branch concepts. Empirically, we show that HAS holds approximately in raw images and randomly initialized CLIP image embeddings, while in text embeddings it holds approximately only after contrastive learning. To theoretically explain this asymmetry, we analyze an unconstrained features model in which the image embeddings are fixed and only the text embeddings are trained. At the global minimizer, the text-side HAS violation percentage at each node is at most the image-side HAS violation and near-violation percentage. The key mechanism is the hierarchical co-occurrence structure of image-text pairs, which biases text embeddings toward root-centered image prototypes. For a linear contrastive loss, we derive a closed-form solution that makes this mechanism explicit. We extend the analysis to the InfoNCE loss and establish analogous guarantees under sufficient regularization. Synthetic experiments validate the theory and show that HAS is transferred even beyond the regimes covered by our analysis.

RaFL: An Empirical Study of Random Filter Losses for Image Reconstruction
Ruibo Wang, Fengbei Liu, Aleksei Tiulpin, Mert R. Sabuncu, Heejong Kim
cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
Pixel losses train image reconstruction networks toward a point statistic of the target distribution, often averaging away fine textures under uncertainty. Perceptual losses alleviate this issue using pretrained features, but those features are inherently domain-specific. We study a random filter loss (RaFL) that compares prediction and target through filters with random weights, avoiding both pretrained features and task-specific loss engineering. RaFL combines random convolutions, random Fourier features, and random wavelet filters, automatically balancing their contributions through gradient-norm matching, eliminating manual loss-weight tuning and grid search. We systematically investigate three design choices: filter family, weight resampling schedule, and weight initialization. Based on validation PSNR and stability across random seeds, we select the configuration that uses all three filter families, redraws filters every optimization step, and employs Gaussian initialization. We evaluate RaFL on three 2D and two 3D image reconstruction tasks. RaFL outperforms a pixel loss on three tasks and matches it on two, and demonstrates stable performance across random seeds on all five tasks. RaFL adds less than 2% to the backbone's forward FLOPs and no inference cost, requiring $182\times$ fewer loss FLOPs than a VGG19 perceptual loss. These findings suggest that RaFL is a simple, tuning-free, and domain-agnostic loss objective for 2D and 3D image reconstruction.

Kwanghee Lee, Hyeongsub Lee, Youngwoo Kim, Hyunji Jeong
cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
Early detection of genetic kidney diseases, especially autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD), is crucial because certain genotypes such as truncating PKD1 mutations cause rapid kidney growth and early progression to end-stage kidney disease. MRI-based genotype inference is a potential low-cost complement to genetic testing.
