In order to address these challenges, we propose E3PN, an efficient 3D MRI-based ADPKD genotype classification network that relies on point sampling and E(3)-invariance. Since E(3)-invariance has been shown to improve data efficiency, we also incorporate it into our model through coordinate canonicalization. On our ADPKD dataset, E3PN trains 163 times faster and uses 103x less GPU memory than a 3D ResNet, while also improving F1 from 0.165 to 0.226 over both baselines. These results establish a first benchmark for five-way ADPKD genotype classification from 3D MRI and show that point sampling and coordinate canonicalization yield two-orders-of-magnitude efficiency gains with a modest but consistent improvement in F1.

Learning Anisotropic Prediction Targets for Flow Models
Rayhan Zirvi, Hansheng Chen, Gordon Wetzstein, Leonidas Guibas
cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
Flow-based generative models are trained on a hand-chosen prediction target, such as the velocity, the noise, or the clean data. This choice strongly affects training and sample quality. We introduce Anisotropic Flow Models (AnisoFlow), which replaces a fixed prediction target with a fully learnable one. AnisoFlow learns to scale the noise component of the target anisotropically, assigning a separate nonnegative weight to each direction in a jointly learned basis. A closed-form map recovers the velocity from the network's prediction, leaving the architecture, flow-matching loss, and sampler unchanged. Since recovery is exact, training optimizes the target purely to make it easier for the network to predict. We theoretically analyze the resulting prediction error and predict that the preferred noise weight in each direction scales near-linearly with data variance, in agreement with the weights learned empirically in pixel space. AnisoFlow improves FID from 1.90 to 1.55 over the JiT baseline on ImageNet 256$\times$256, by learning only the prediction target, without representation alignment or architectural changes. In pixel-space text-to-image generation, AnisoFlow improves image quality and, through better-conditioned velocity recovery, largely removes patch-seam artifacts under reduced-precision inference.

Florent Imbert, Simon Corbillé, Hui Han, Elisa H. Barney Smith
cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
Character classification and handwritten text recognition, particularly in specialized tasks, often suffer from limited labeled data, while a large amount of unlabeled data remains unused. To address this challenge, we propose a novel unsupervised domain adaptation-based pipeline to reduce the distribution gap between source and target domains. Our pipeline follows three main steps. In the second step, we perform unsupervised domain adaptation to leverage unlabeled target data, adapting the model to the specific characteristics of the target domain. In the third step, we trained the expert model on source data in the target style. The unsupervised training increases the computation time only during training not in inference. We evaluate the pipeline on both character-level and historical handwritten line recognition tasks, demonstrating its flexibility. We establish a new state-of-the-art on the Safran-MNIST-DLS dataset defined in Track 2 of the DAGECC competition with 76.17% Macro F1-score and achieve competitive results on each of the specific datasets of READ 2018 without using the labels of the target data. Dayoon Ko, Yichuan Wang, Soyeong Jeong, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Gunhee Kim, Sewon Min
cs.CV · cs.IR · cs.LG
Abstract
Screen history can serve as searchable visual memory for people and agents, but unlike an offline corpus, it must be encoded and indexed as each screenshot arrives. OCR-based indexing is fast but loses visual information; visual encoding takes over two seconds. Yet screens change incrementally: most pixels repeat within and across screenshots. This raises a question: do visual encoders need to recompute their representations from scratch for every new screenshot? We present StreamEmbed, a system for incremental visual embedding that exploits this redundancy. Guided solely by pixel differences, it combines spatial token pruning, which skips blocks that repeat a neighbor, with temporal KV reuse, which reuses cached keys and values for blocks unchanged across screenshots. A small learned adapter compensates for pruning and reuse to preserve fidelity to full-screenshot embeddings. On ScreenMemEval, our benchmark of 10,426 questions over 184 hours of screen histories from two humans and a computer-use agent, StreamEmbed sets a new accuracy-latency Pareto frontier on an M2 Pro GPU: it encodes up to 3.7x faster than full-screenshot encoding without sacrificing accuracy and improves accuracy over OCR-based indexing by up to 6.3 Recall@10 points at matched latency. A memory-efficient vector index stores a simulated year of screenshots in 0.13 GB of RAM. Together, these results make real-time indexing of screen history in its native visual form practical, laying a foundation for fully local visual assistants.

Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction
Abhilekha Dalal, Avishek Das, Samatha Ereshi Akkamahadevi
cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) can classify images using background-dominated patches that contain little or none of the target object, showing that they exploit contextual information. Previous studies detected this behavior through classification accuracy but say little about what CNN hidden layers represent. We investigate whether Concept Induction can reveal interpretable visual properties associated with hidden-neuron activations on contextual image patches. Using five agricultural datasets with high, moderate, and low contextual bias, we analyze four CNN architectures at three network depths and five patch locations. Quantitative image descriptors of each patch are encoded in a knowledge graph and used as background knowledge for Concept Induction, which derives logical expressions characterizing neuron activation. These expressions are tested on held-out images using the Mann-Whitney U test and Cliff's delta. Of 48,013 tested neuron-feature pairs, 13,164 (27.4%) were validated. Validation was most frequent at intermediate depths (mean 40.2% at mid layers versus 12.0% at deep layers), and no patch location dominated across datasets. Among the tested descriptors, edge, Gabor, and Tamura texture features had the highest validation rates. Stronger contextual bias did not correspond to higher neuron-feature validation rates: moderately biased Sugarcane had the highest rate (40.5%) and highly biased Cassava the lowest (19.2%). These results suggest that behavioral context dependence and the semantic interpretability of hidden neurons capture different aspects of CNN behavior, and that Concept Induction can complement patch-based bias detection.

cs.LG / 9 / 2611.18559
BAL: Bidirectional Autoregression in Latent Space for Learning Human Movement Representations
Genki Kinoshita, Ko Nishino
cs.CV · cs.LG
Abstract
Learning semantically meaningful human movement representations from 3D pose sequences is essential for human behavior modeling. Existing self-supervised methods derive supervision from artificial assumptions, e.g., invariance to augmentation within a certain range or recoverability of masked regions at a certain temporal scale, introducing heuristic hyperparameters that make the learned semantics sensitive to their settings. We propose BAL, a self-supervised framework that instead exploits supervision intrinsic to human movement: the natural ordering structure of everyday behavior. BAL autoregressively predicts in a semantically abstract latent space, analogous to how humans anticipate others' actions rather than their precise joint configurations. Bidirectional autoregressive prediction, both forward and backward in time, fully leverages these ordering constraints while enabling representation extraction using both past and future context at inference. To suppress representation collapse, BAL decomposes each prediction target into a global component capturing sequence-level semantics and a local component capturing context-independent movement. We prove that the local targets retain nontrivial angular diversity unless the representations are completely collapsed, preserving a meaningful training signal. Experiments on large-scale datasets demonstrate that BAL's representations are highly effective for motion captioning, forecasting, and interpolation, outperforming baselines on most evaluation metrics.

cs.LG / 10 / 2611.17980
Algorithmic Opacity as a Principal-Agent Problem
Ignacio Cofone, Katherine J. Strandburg
cs.CY · cs.LG
Abstract
Concerns about gaming and trade secrecy, frequently invoked to justify algorithmic opacity, can also mask self-serving strategic behavior by decision-makers, who may prioritize private benefits over social welfare. We develop a principal-agent test for when algorithmic decision-making should trigger disclosure obligations even when concerns about gaming or trade secrecy exist. The test, which is anchored in decision quality, relies on two diagnostics that can be disclosed without enabling gaming or revealing trade secrets: the error profile (false positive and false negative rates, overall and for each group) and the distribution of decision outcomes across groups. These diagnostics make visible whether decision-makers align design and implementation with the public interest or instead use opacity to obscure bias, inefficiency, or strategic shirking. Strong disparities across social groups, and asymmetries in the distribution of decision errors across outcome types, should trigger rebuttable presumptions favoring further disclosure. Where ground-truth error rates are unavailable, outcome distributions across groups can support the same presumption, and a decision-maker's inability to produce an error profile is itself diagnostic. The test thus treats fairness metrics as triggers for further disclosure and extends to generative and agentic systems through analogous metrics. The framework informs differentiated disclosure regimes (staged, partial, mediated, or audience-segmented). Once such regimes are considered, the domain of justifiable algorithmic opacity is significantly narrower than commonly assumed: when gaming or trade secrecy risks are legitimate, actionable disclosures (such as training data characteristics and key system design parameters) ordinarily can be mandated without compromising innovation or enabling gaming, even for technically inscrutable algorithms.

Why Finite Intelligence Works in an Infinite World: A Mathematical Theory
Mojtaba Soltanalian
cs.IT · cs.LG · stat.ML
Abstract
An information system often has to learn before it knows what it will be asked. A finite representation can succeed without recording an infinite environment in full when those asks share collective structure, rather than demand unrelated facts scattered arbitrarily across it. We formalize this phenomenon through future-task information, the minimum retained information needed to preserve decision value relative to the available evidence. A joint decision-regret state records posterior comparisons among actions for every permitted task. Under compactness assumptions, every retained representation admits a calibrated replacement based on this state, preserving all task regrets without increasing information; the resulting information-regret curve equals an operational coding rate. Two exact boundaries govern finite preservation: weak-star closure for a fixed action count, and the singular mass of the source-evidence law when all finite action menus are admitted. Collective structure determines information requirements beyond individual-question difficulty. Families with identical source laws, pairwise task metrics, and individual-task rates can have different high-resolution exponents. Covariance-identical coordinate and parity families have different exact rate-distortion curves, with optimal parity retention characterized by a sharp Fourier-entropy inequality. For balanced nested task families, refinement sizes and weighted reach determine the rate within an additive constant, attained by prefix coding under explicit decay conditions. Nonlinear learning and computational separations distinguish preservation from efficient discovery and use. The proposed mathematical theory identifies which shared decision requirements allow finite information to remain useful for infinitely many possible purposes.

cs.LG / 17 / 2611.17412
WETW: Learning Pair-Specific Weighted Euclidean Distances for Efficient DTW Search
Christos Panourgias, Qitong Wang, George Tzagkarakis, Themis Palpanas
cs.LG · cs.AI
Abstract
Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) is the standard distance for similarity search over time series that are misaligned in time, but its cost is quadratic in the series length and lower bounds often prune few candidates, so search over large collections of long series is slow. Learned approximations embed the series to make comparisons cheap, yet compare every pair with the same fixed rule, usually the Euclidean distance, although DTW aligns each pair differently. We observe that, once a pair is aligned, DTW is a Euclidean distance over coordinates selected for that pair. Building on this view, we propose Weighted Euclidean Time Warping (WETW), a learned distance that adapts the comparison of fixed-length embeddings to each pair. A small symmetric network predicts one weight per embedding di- mension and pair without computing an alignment, which halves the error of the best static distance on the same embeddings. The embeddings come from the Weighted Euclidean SEries Encoder (WESEE), whose multi-scale Temporal Feature Extractor keeps the timing information that global pooling removes and raises recall@1 from 0.90 to 0.95, although WETW works with any encoder. We also show that fitting DTW distances is not enough for search, as regression alone misorders the closest neighbours, and that ranking regularization raises recall@1 from 0.79 to 0.95. Our filter-and-verify pipeline, WETW-FV, uses WETW to build a shortlist and exact DTW to check it, so the search is approximate but every returned distance is exact. Over eight datasets, WETW-FV is 11.4x faster than the fastest exact method among the UCR Suite, lower bounds and MESSI-DTW, at a mean recall@10 of 0.93, while the best learned baseline reaches 0.59 at the same speed. On 48K-point series, WETW- FV remains 40x faster than the UCR Suite at a recall@10 of 0.91.

Classification with Large-Step Gradient Descent: Unifying Self-Stabilization and Convergence Rates
Salma Tarmoun, Lachlan Ewen MacDonald, Konstantinos Emmanouilidis, Ziqing Xu, René Vidal
cs.LG · math.OC
Abstract
Neural networks are often trained with gradient descent (GD) using step sizes that exceed classical stability thresholds, a regime where standard convergence theory no longer applies. Despite exhibiting non-monotonic loss and oscillatory dynamics, this regime frequently leads to faster convergence and better generalization. Existing theoretical perspectives offer complementary but incomplete insights: one identifies a self-stabilization mechanism that drives GD toward flat regions but does not yield convergence rates; another derives rigorous rates for specific losses but does not explain the underlying stabilization mechanism; and a third unifies these views for overparameterized least squares, but relies on the existence of a minimizer manifold, which is absent in classification settings. In this work, we extend this unified geometric perspective to exponential-tail losses, including logistic and cross-entropy losses in separable linear classification, where the loss infimum is not attained at any finite parameter. We identify a curvature-defined reference subspace that replaces the role of the minimizer manifold, yielding a coupled dynamical system in which orthogonal oscillations are damped by parallel progress along directions of decreasing sharpness. Under regularity conditions, which we verify for logistic and multiclass cross-entropy losses on symmetric data models, we derive explicit bounds for the iterates, not just the loss, covering both the unstable and stable regimes, and revealing a three-phase damping structure in the latter. Our analysis further uncovers a transient implicit bias induced by large step sizes: although GD converges asymptotically to the max-margin direction, large steps can leave persistent orthogonal residuals and, in certain geometries, amplify them, affecting finite-time alignment with the max-margin direction.

Abstract

Ahmed Shoyeb Raihan, Farzana Islam, Ashish Nimbarte, Imtiaz Ahmed
cs.LG
Abstract
Electric arc furnace (EAF) steelmaking plays an increasingly important role in recycled and lower-carbon steel production. However, variability in charged materials, energy input, and operating conditions makes accurate prediction of molten steel temperature and final chemical composition challenging. Eight industrial data sources are integrated into three progressively enriched predictor representations comprising 19 static, 39 temporal, and 47 history-informed features. Historical outcomes are incorporated only when eligible under an assumed 60-minute measurement-release delay. Using 19,738 temperature heats and 3,341 chemistry heats, twelve tuned regression algorithms are evaluated through chronological forward validation. The proposed historical-feedback representation reduces root mean squared error (RMSE) in 147 of 156 matched algorithm-target comparisons. For temperature prediction, ExtraTrees achieves an RMSE of 12.290 degrees C, representing a 5.54 percent reduction compared with its temporal-only counterpart. Historical-feature ablation and feedback-delay sensitivity analyses demonstrate the importance of recent outcome information and its availability. In a separate retrospective later-period evaluation, lightweight adaptive residual correction reduces temperature RMSE from 12.502 to 12.275 degrees C without retraining the underlying model. Exploratory deep learning comparisons and target-specific feature-attribution analyses provide additional insights into predictive behavior. The results establish historical outcome availability and sequential feedback as important considerations for EAF endpoint prediction.

cs.LG / 21 / 2611.17469
Improved Regret via Variance-Aware Policy Optimization in Adversarial MDPs
Ofir Schlisselberg, Alon Cohen, Yishay Mansour
cs.LG
Abstract
We study episodic adversarial MDPs with unknown dynamics and full-information rewards and achieve regret with leading term $\widetilde O(\sqrt{H^3SAK})$. Here $H$ is the horizon, $S$ the total number of states across decision layers, $A$ the number of actions, and $K$ the number of episodes. This improves the two incomparable prior leading terms by factors $\sqrt{S/H}$ and $\sqrt{H^3}$, leaving a factor $\sqrt H$ to the lower-bound scale. We give a reduction from unknown to known dynamics for \emph{downstream-dependent} algorithms, whose policy at step $h$ is computed only from information at steps $h+1$ and onward. This reduces the remaining leading-order gap to finding a downstream-dependent known-dynamics algorithm with $\widetilde O(H\sqrt K)$ regret, which would yield the tight $\widetilde O(H\sqrt{SAK})$ leading term for unknown dynamics. We instantiate the reduction with VOPO, a policy optimization (PO) algorithm. Its improved guarantees follow from a variance-aware analysis enabled by a novel bonus, which we call the Stability-Matching Bonus (SMB). VOPO achieves $\widetilde O(\sqrt{H^3K})$ regret under known dynamics with full information; its $\sqrt H$ gap to the target $H\sqrt K$ rate is inherited by our unknown-dynamics guarantee. We also show that with known dynamics and trajectory bandit feedback, VOPO achieves $\widetilde O(\sqrt{H^2SAK})$ regret. Both known-dynamics guarantees improve the corresponding state-of-the-art PO bounds by a factor $\sqrt H$. A matching lower bound establishes that the full-information rate is tight in one parameter regime.

cs.LG / 22 / 2611.17472
Do Mixture-of-Experts Routers Benefit from Ensembling?
Viktor Veselý, Sebastian Jitaru
cs.LG · cs.AI
Abstract
Ensembles of neural networks have repeatedly been shown to outperform single networks. Ensemble theory attributes this gain to the accuracy of the individual members and the diversity among them. Mixture-of-experts models already combine many experts, but a single router decides how the experts are weighted for each input. Motivated by ensemble theory, we apply ensembling to the routing step: an ensemble of routers, trained jointly with the experts, proposes the expert weights. We systematically evaluate this approach using underparameterized networks on two image-classification and two tabular-regression tasks. We analyse each experiment with a performance-diversity decomposition of the router ensemble, which, to our knowledge, has not previously been developed for routers. We report four findings. First, more routers lower the validation loss. Second, training routers to explicitly maximize expert diversity improves the mean performance on three of the four tasks. Third, explicitly maximizing router diversity has no effect on performance. Fourth, combining router weights with the arithmetic mean works better than combining them with the geometric mean. Routing benefits mildly from ensembling, but this benefit is offset by the computational cost of training additional routers.

cs.LG / 23 / 2611.17478
ERGo: Energy-Efficient Hybrid Graph Neural Network Training on Processing-In-Memory Architectures
Pratyush Dhingra, Chibuike E. Ugwu, Janardhan Rao Doppa, Partha Pratim Pande
cs.LG
Abstract
Processing-in-memory (PIM) has been proposed as an alternative computing paradigm for training Deep Neural Networks, including Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). Despite these advancements, training GNN workloads on PIM devices necessitates off-chip memory access. This off-chip access is expensive in terms of performance and energy, thereby impacting the overall energy efficiency of the training process on PIM platforms. In this paper, we propose a novel hybrid training framework called ERGo that automatically switches from a full-parameter phase to a parameter-efficient phase during the training process with negligible loss in predictive accuracy. The parameter-efficient phase employs low-rank representations of GNN weights, effectively reducing the number of trainable parameters. This helps in reducing the off-chip access during the end-to-end training process on PIM architectures, leading to notable improvements in both performance and energy efficiency. ERGo outperforms the conventional full-parameter GNN training on a PIM-based platform by up to 3.15x in speedup and enhances energy efficiency by 10.5x. Furthermore, ERGo offers additional advantages when utilized on heterogeneous architectures incorporating non-volatile memory (NVM) and static random-access memory (SRAM). Training on the heterogeneous NVM-SRAM architecture typically utilizes NVM for the forward-pass and SRAM for the backward-pass. However, NVM cells suffer from low device endurance and repeated write operations due to weight updates can lead to poor lifetime. ERGo improves the lifetime of NVM devices by freezing the weights and preventing weight updates in the parameter-efficient phase. Experimental results demonstrate that ERGo improves the lifetime of heterogeneous NVM-SRAM architecture by up to 33x in comparison to full-parameter implementation.

cs.LG / 24 / 2611.17484
SURFACE: Preference Distribution Matching on Pareto Fronts via Scalarization Steering
Liuyuan Jiang, Prasanna Sattigeri, Lisha Chen
cs.LG · math.OC · stat.ML
Abstract
Constructing a finite Pareto representation requires allocating a limited number of solutions across the Pareto front (PF). A desired preference distribution specifies this allocation by assigning more solutions to some PF regions and fewer to others. Scalarization generates Pareto solutions by varying objective weights and solving single-objective problems. However, a distribution of scalarization weights generally does not translate into the same distribution of Pareto solutions along the PF, because the weight-to-solution map is nonlinear and unknown in advance.To account for this distortion, we introduce SURFACE (Scalarization Updates for Redistributing Finite Approximations with Chart Estimation}), which adaptively updates scalarization weights to match a desired PF distribution. SURFACE estimates how each region of the weight grid corresponds to an area of the PF from the current scalarized solutions, then redistributes the weights accordingly. The method supports parallel, warm-started, inexact optimization with standard single-objective solvers. Theoretically, we establish that, under the stated regularity conditions, SURFACE achieves linear convergence of the preference distribution mismatch to a finite-resolution error floor of $\mathcal{O}(N^{-2/(M-1)})$, where $N$ is the number of adaptive weights and $M$ is the number of objectives. Empirically, experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate improved target-mass balance and PF coverage compared with established baselines.

cs.LG / 25 / 2611.17487
Identifying Latent Neural Dynamics with Recognition-Parametrised Gaussian Process Dynamical Systems
Arielle Rosinski, Qianqian Feng, Lior Fox, Maneesh Sahani
cs.LG
Abstract
Neural circuits are densely interconnected systems, and many neural computations are thought to be mediated by the dynamical evolution of activity within these recurrent networks, a hypothesis often referred to as "computation through dynamics". Under this view, understanding computation within real neural populations depends on building models of the dynamical rules governing the temporal evolution of recorded high-dimensional neural activity. Most commonly, such models depend on explicit generative parameterisations of (i) the (nonlinear) intrinsic dynamical flow field and stochasticity, (ii) the (nonlinear) mapping from dynamical state to neuronal activity, and (iii) the variability of individual neural responses given that dynamical state. Misspecification of any of these components can bias estimates of dynamics and latent trajectories. In this paper, we circumvent generation-related issues by introducing the Recognition-Parametrised Gaussian Process dynamics (RP-GPdyn) model. RP-GPdyn models the dynamical flow field using a nonparametric Gaussian Process-defined transition function and models the relationship of dynamics to neural activity implicitly using the Recognition-Parametrised Model (RPM) framework. We show that this generation-free approach uncovers meaningful, behaviourally relevant latent variables and dynamics from both synthetic and experimental datasets.

PF-Exec: Large-Scale UAV Relay Load Balancing with an Anchored Potential Direction Decider and a Per-Dimension Gain-Learning Executor
Liheng Zeng, Hanzhang Jia, Yiran Li, Yi Gao, Bo Ma
cs.LG · cs.IT · eess.SP
Abstract
Relay load balancing in large-scale UAV networks requires joint decisions on association, bandwidth, power and hovering position, while the dimension grows with the number of UAVs and each UAV sees only its local state. This paper splits the decision into two layers. An anchored potential direction decider emits a closed-form target at every instant and returns its direction only. A gain-learning executor maps a local observation into a positive gain per dimension, composes gain and target, and clips the action against the physical single-step bound; the two layers form PF-Exec. The unit gain is the default start, so the executor reduces to the pure potential policy before training. The load is first-order homogeneous in bandwidth, making the bandwidth target closed form, and the position target is the demand-weighted centroid of the served users. The system uses a two-hop access and millimetre-wave backhaul link with soft association, a 3GPP UMa-AV channel with two shadowing layers, an OU-type social-force crowd and a quadrotor physical layer. Evaluation spans all fleet sizes, two crowd scenarios, independent seeds, the published baselines in their own settings and a degraded-information line. In the highest-pressure burst setting the fixed-step comparison breaks down on part of the seeds, losing fairness and mean load at once, while PF-Exec holds fairness near one. Among the ten comparison methods only the greedy heuristic survives that setting; its load variance stays far higher. Under stale global state and position noise the fairness index stays high at both largest-scale scenarios. Flight energy is nearly identical between the learned and the fixed-step executor, and three constants are fixed once across all settings. The convergence properties of both layers, from the truncated gain iteration to the perturbed tracking bound, are proved in the companion analysis.

Adversarially Batched Online Learning
Mingda Qiao, Kerui Wu
cs.LG · cs.DS
Abstract
We introduce adversarially batched online learning, where the learner may update its decision rule only at a restricted set of time steps. Equivalently, the time horizon is partitioned into batches, within which the learner must use a fixed policy. We study this model for four canonical problems: stochastic bandits, online convex optimization, prediction with expert advice, and adversarial bandits. For stochastic bandits, we identify a universal complexity measure based on relative batch growth and establish both gap-dependent and gap-independent regret bounds. We show that the multiplicative penalty relative to the standard gap-dependent complexity can grow superlinearly in batch hardness. For the remaining three, we show that the optimal regret is tightly characterized by the 2-norm of the batch-length sequence. The upper bound is achieved by a batch-oblivious learner who does not know the batch structure, via a generic adaptive doubling trick tailored to this complexity measure. In contrast, the lower bound holds even for batch-aware learners.

Abstract
Causal discovery promises to find which recorded variables drive a process target such as product quality, yield or energy use. Beyond a Single Sign: BiTE for Branch-Preserving Binary Threshold Expansion
Siddharth Gupta, Mikail Yayla, Akash Kumar
cs.LG
Abstract
Binary neural networks (BNNs) reduce model storage and replace many arithmetic operations with efficient bitwise computations, but the single-sign activation limits their representational capacity. Each preactivation direction is compared with one threshold and reduced to one of two values, discarding variation within each sign region. We propose \textbf{BiTE} (Branch-Preserving Binary Threshold Expansion), which evaluates each feature direction against $K$ ordered thresholds and retains the resulting binary responses as separate inputs to the following learned operator. BiTE can distinguish up to $K+1$ ordered activation states while allowing the following operator to learn a separate transformation for each threshold response. It also enables a controllable trade-off between the number of independently constructed feature directions and the number of ordered responses extracted from each direction. On BinaryViT, BiTE improves mean final-epoch Top-1 accuracy from $74.60\pm0.30\%$ to $75.83\pm0.15\%$ on CIFAR-10 and from $42.80\pm0.46\%$ to $44.07\pm0.32\%$ on CIFAR-100, with nearly unchanged trainable parameter counts. A matched uniform-collapse control reaches $75.33\pm0.15\%$ on CIFAR-10, supporting the value of retaining the threshold responses separately relative to the evaluated scalar collapse. Binary VGG-16 experiments further show a favorable observed parameter--accuracy trade-off, including a configuration that achieves $0.64$ percentage points higher mean accuracy with $21.8\%$ fewer parameters than conventional width scaling. Overall, these results support branch-preserving threshold expansion as an effective way to improve binary representations and the observed parameter--accuracy trade-off.

Abstract
Distributed coordination of electric vehicle (EV) charging with the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) keeps EV constraints private but assumes that every participant follows the algorithm. This assumption fails in practice, where a biased operator or a man-in-the-middle (MITM) attacker can send inconsistent signals to different EVs, and compromised EVs can deviate from their prescribed updates. We first prove that attacks by such arbitrarily malicious, i.e., Byzantine, participants do not prevent convergence and therefore remain undetected, and derive the most damaging attack in closed form. We then propose a framework that combines a threshold-signed broadcast with upload recomputation, which detects every deviation without false alarms at linear instead of quadratic communication cost. To keep the optimization running, each affected EV recovers locally with a certified, fleet-aware machine learning price forecast, and we prove that the optimization remains convergent with a recovery cost bounded by the shape of the forecast error. Simulation results with real-world load, price, and charging data show that a biased operator raises the annual cost of disadvantaged EVs by 132.7% and lowers that of advantaged EVs by 49.3% without changing the aggregate load, whereas the defense limits all cost changes to 2.6%. An external MITM attacker raises the cost of its victims by 57.6%, the peak load by 5.8%, and the grid cost by 15.8%, whereas the defense keeps them within 1.1%, 0.2%, and 0.3% of benign operation. With up to 70% of the EVs attacked, the recovery converges in at least 97.8% of the runs.

Offline KL-Regularized Reinforcement Learning with Stochastic Transitions
Andrew Liu
cs.LG
Abstract
KL-regularized reinforcement learning is the objective behind RL fine-tuning of language models, and its data are often collected by the reference policy itself. However, beyond bandits and deterministic transitions, little is known about how much offline data it needs. In this work, we study finite discounted Markov decision processes with unknown stochastic transitions, $K$ state-action pairs and effective horizon $H$. For data that cover the regularized optimum with concentrability $C\geq 4$, every estimator needs $Ω(KCH^3/(ε\max\{ε,β\}))$ samples on some instances when $ε$ is small, where $β$ is the temperature. Under a fixed design, pessimistic soft value iteration with a variance-aware penalty attains this rate with high probability, up to logarithmic factors, when $β\geq 1.4H$. When the data come from the reference policy itself, we show that it covers the optimum up to a power law whose exponent grows as $H/β$; in the same temperature range, this power law alone gives the estimator a sample complexity polynomial in $1/ε$, with no coverage assumption. For $β\geq H$ the power can also increase the sample size needed by a factor polynomial in $1/ε$. Finally, the plug-in estimator, which is minimax optimal with a generative model, can fail on reference data at small temperatures when many actions are rarely seen, while the pessimistic estimator does not.

Neural Simulation-Based Inference for Computational Cognitive Models: A Tutorial
Ti-Fen Pan, Yi Cheng, Anne GE Collins
cs.LG · q-bio.NC
Abstract
Computational cognitive modeling is an increasingly essential methodology to elucidate the mechanisms underlying human and animal cognition. By fitting models to empirical data, researchers can perform parameter estimation, model comparison, and model-based neural analyses to assess competing theories, quantify individual differences, and investigate neural representations. Typically, fitting these models relies on likelihood-dependent methods (e.g., Maximum likelihood estimation or hierarchical Bayesian methods). However, for many reasonable cognitive models, computing the likelihood is computationally intractable, in which case these methods become inadequate. This substantially limits the range of theories that researchers can investigate. Simulation-based inference with neural networks (neural SBI) has emerged as a powerful toolkit to overcome this hurdle. By training deep neural networks on datasets generated through model simulations, neural SBI learns the relation between observable data and cognitive models. This approach enables all the applications of standard modeling while bypassing the likelihood computation, without constraints on empirical data size. Despite its successful adoption across diverse scientific fields, neural SBI remains underutilized in cognitive modeling, partly due to a lack of accessible, domain-tailored guidance. This tutorial addresses this gap by providing a clear workflow for applying neural SBI to cognitive models. We also provide practical use cases and recommendations for reducing the computational and simulation burden, facilitating efficient implementation in real-world research settings. By lowering the technical barriers to neural SBI, this tutorial aims to empower cognitive scientists to investigate richer theory spaces and ultimately propel scientific discovery.

cs.LG
Abstract
Joint world action models (WAMs) generate future actions and states together, modeling world-action trajectories rather than actions alone. However, trajectory imitation does not ensure that these trajectories are task-effective or consistent with dynamics. Pattern-Based Matrix Analysis Under Permutations
Andrei Ermakov
cs.LG
Abstract
Low-rank approximation captures one important notion of matrix simplicity: a matrix is simple if it is well described by a low-dimensional linear model. This paper studies a complementary notion. We consider matrices that become structurally simple only after permuting rows and columns, so that their mass concentrates in a prescribed geometric pattern such as a band, block-diagonal, arrowhead, or related form. We introduce a pattern distance that measures how close a matrix is to a given pattern after optimal reordering, giving a common framework for structural problems such as latent ordering, community detection, and bandwidth minimization. The problem is NP-hard in general; we derive an alternating algorithm based on Linear Assignment subproblems. We further introduce a pattern-plus-low-rank decomposition that combines permutation-structured fitting with a low-rank residual, whose permutation step is pattern fitting on the residual; its gains across pattern families persist at a matched parameter budget and are substantially reduced under a density-matched random-mask control. We evaluate the framework on synthetic recovery tasks, real correlation and graph adjacency matrices, and transformer attention heads, showing that a single universal algorithm recovers qualitatively different forms of matrix structure.

cs.LG · cs.CR
Abstract
Adversarial training is a common technique for making neural networks more robust to small, intentionally crafted input perturbations, and robustness is commonly measured through accuracy under attack. By exploring inter-network representations across different adversarial training objectives, we can analyze whether multiple networks organize their latent representations similarly. We study Multilayer Perceptrons trained on MNIST under three adversarial training regimes and compare the resulting population training dynamics and geometry to those of a vanilla network. We use M-PHATE to embed data samples across training epochs and examine how the geometry of the data changes during training under adversarial objectives. Abstract
Machine learning (ML)-based fault detectors are increasingly proposed for power system protection, yet aggregate metrics can obscure the consequences of false and missed trips. It reports security, dependability, detection timing, decision margins, and episode-level nuisance assertions. Gradient-boosted trees, a convolutional neural network, and logistic regression achieve AUC-ROC values above 0.999, yet at a security budget of $10^{-3}$ their developed-fault missed-trip rates range from 0.007% to 1.99%. Post-clearing false-trip rates of approximately 0.12-0.16% per window coexist with nuisance assertions in 11.3-19.1% of episodes. Among the analysed physical descriptors, missed trips are most consistently associated with higher fault resistance within the sampled range. An analytical current proxy suggests a fault-current gradient only partly reproduced by waveform-derived current (rank correlation 0.62). No single topology state or faulted phase consistently dominates within the sampled domain. Because PROTECT-90 contains fault episodes only, security is assessed on pre-fault and post-clearing windows rather than independent non-fault disturbances.

Delayed Effects of Temporary Weight Decay in Muon Pretraining
Md Rifat Arefin, Ravid Shwartz-Ziv, Ernie Chang, Chinnadhurai Sankar, Rylan Conway, Aristide Baratin, Irina Rish, Adithya Sagar, Patrick Huber
cs.LG · cs.CL
Abstract
A temporary optimization intervention can increase validation loss when it ends yet reduce it at the final training budget. We demonstrate this with temporary weight decay in Muon-trained decoder-only Transformers with 162M, 405M, 1.4B, and 3.4B parameters. For models up to 1.4B parameters, moving a fixed-duration decay window from early to late training reverses its final effect. At 162M, changing window duration alters final loss even at approximately matched total shrinkage. Most strikingly, a schedule selected at 162M and transferred without retuning to 1.4B is 0.26 nats worse than no decay when removed, overtakes it only after 73% of training, and ultimately finishes 0.028 nats better while also outperforming the best sampled constant-decay baseline. Early decay leaves a persistent weight-norm deficit, which keeps relative steps larger after the intervention ends. An approximate norm-dynamics recurrence captures the slow convergence of these relative steps, and fixed-norm replays of the measured trajectories reproduce the performance ordering and a similar loss difference. At 162M and 405M, norm-reset experiments show that smaller weight norms at the end of the decay window explain most of the final loss improvement. A separate Gram intervention exhibits related post-removal norm and relative-step dynamics. These results show that temporary optimization schedules can have delayed effects long after removal and should therefore be evaluated at the intended final training budget, not only at removal.

cs.LG / 44 / 2611.17879
From Local Prediction Error to Final Outputs: A Theory of Expert Sharing in Multi-Step Reasoning
Tiantong Wang, Zhiren Gong, Yixin Wang, Tiantong Wu, Wei Yang Bryan Lim
cs.LG · cs.AI
Abstract
Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models can share experts across layers or computational steps, pooling training information from different contexts. This can reduce local prediction error, but multi-step systems are ultimately evaluated by their final outputs. We study when these local gains survive composition. In specified linear regression models, we derive exact sharing and merging criteria by balancing variance savings against bias from sharing across roles with different target behavior. Under a common fixed linear propagation map, we characterize expected squared final-output risk, retaining error directions and correlations. We construct cases where sharing lowers average local error yet increases final-output error. Within this setting, we also identify when orthogonal full pooling with isotropic noise preserves every local improvement at the final output. For general routed computations, we derive conditional end-to-end error bounds that account for distribution shifts, errors in surrounding components, and changes in expert selection. Separately, we show that correct predictions and route labels alone do not establish that individual experts implement the attributed operations. Controlled experiments realize a strict local-to-final risk reversal. In low-data symbolic settings, trained Transformers exhibit statistically supported teacher-forced one-step gains without statistically resolved free-running final-output gains. Separate intervention tests distinguish successful multi-layer actions from single-expert attribution and generalization to unseen transitions. Together, these results distinguish when sharing improves local estimation, when those gains survive to the final output, and what evidence is needed to attribute reusable operations to individual experts.

cs.LG / 45 / 2611.17886
Quantum Graph Neural Networks: Architectures, Trainability, and Applications
Zeinab Sharifi, Hossein Shirazi
cs.LG
Abstract
Quantum graph neural networks (QGNNs) apply parametrized quantum circuits to graph-structured data, with the aim of overcoming limits of classical graph neural networks in scalability, expressivity, and long-range information flow. Since the first Hamiltonian-based model in 2019, the field has produced many architectures, applications, and claims, but the evidence behind them has not been assessed systematically. This survey introduces a unified formalism that describes every QGNN as an encoder, a graph-conditioned circuit, observables, and an optional classical head, together with a circuit-level condition for permutation equivariance and a resource model for comparing costs. We organize the literature into five architecture families (convolutional, equivariant, message-passing and recurrent, Hamiltonian, and hybrid) and compare them by qubit cost, symmetry, scalability, and evidence. We then review symmetry and geometric inductive biases (permutation, rotation, and Lorentz), the results on expressivity, trainability, noise, and scalability, and applications in high-energy physics, chemistry, finance, healthcare, earth observation, and transportation. An audit of datasets and evaluation practices leads to a minimum reporting protocol. Our main finding is that the design space is rich and symmetry is the best-supported inductive bias, whereas the evidence remains thin: results are mostly simulated, on small graphs, at parity with classical models or with fewer parameters, and no advantage has yet been established on tasks over classical graph data. We identify five open challenges and a research agenda covering provable advantage, equivariant and geometric designs, fault-tolerant implementations, and shared benchmarks. The coverage extends through 2026 and emphasizes recent work.

Federated learning (FL) under differential privacy (DP) is typically evaluated on large client cohorts, where subsampling and cross-client averaging amplify privacy and smooth injected noise. A class-scoping diagnosis yields $50.5\%\pm3.6\%$ accuracy with 6 seeds and 2.02 times chance. We also introduce head-scoped client-level DP, which protects the classification head and alone reduces the achievable $\varepsilon$ by 200 times at a matched round budget; combined with fewer communication rounds it reaches $\varepsilon\!\approx\!10.4$ with utility not statistically distinguishable from non-private training.

Reasoning Models Confabulate to Defend Injected Beliefs About Their Own Requests
Yang Ou
cs.LG
Abstract
Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring and self-report are proposed as levers for AI oversight, on the assumption that a model's account of what it is doing is anchored to what it was asked. We show this anchor can be cut. An activation edit invisible in the prompt text, the injected forming-trace of an off-topic concept, overwrites a reasoning model's belief about its own request: across six models spanning scale, architecture, and recipe, the injected topic becomes the task it reasons about, reports back, and answers: the forced quote-back returns the injected topic in 47 of 48 model-concept cells, and on the four models with clean final haiku the injected answer follows in 99% of generations. Warnings barely move it: even one naming the true topic yields under 5% true-topic answers. Handed the truth in clean tokens, the model confabulates a story about the user ("maybe they got confused and corrected themselves") and answers with the injection anyway, its behavior and chain-of-thought corrupted together: a concrete limit on CoT monitorability. Opening the model up offers no reprieve: it attends to the true request even harder after edits, yet that attention is causally inert, and the injected content rides intact task circuits, so attention maps and circuit attributions read as on-task too; the same edit redirects the request's language and format, not only its topic. What the injection reaches is the model's reference to its own input; what it elicits is its defense of it.

Ahmed Awad, Anirudh Sengar
cs.LG
Abstract
For imbalanced data streams with delayed labels, is it worth adding a streaming oversampler, or does continuous re-evaluation of earlier predictions already recover the cost of the delay? We address this through an extensive experimental comparison of streaming oversampling and continuous re-evaluation under joint class imbalance, concept drift, and label delay. Streaming oversamplers are typically evaluated under the test-then-train assumption of immediate labels; their behaviour under these conditions, assessed through re-evaluation, has not been systematically studied. We close this gap with a decision framework that predicts when oversampling adds value over re-evaluation, organised around three axes: drift density, minority-class learnability, and delay relative to the refinement window, and operationalised as a competition between a recovery channel (re-evaluation) and a minority-coverage channel (oversampling). Method selection should therefore be conditioned on the stream's profile and re-estimated as it evolves, not inherited from synthetic benchmarks.

cs.LG
Abstract
Accurate runoff prediction across large river basins requires models to capture heterogeneous local hydrological responses and their accumulation downstream. River-network connectivity provides a natural structure for this task, but using it effectively requires learning how to combine contributions from subcatchments of different sizes. We frame this challenge as learning to accumulate and introduce RiverScan, a spatial operator based on area-weighted selective state-space recurrence. Lukáš Jochymek, Markéta Vašinková, Petr Gajdoš

Abstract
Published implementations of the Poincaré convolution in Hyperbolic Neural Networks++ (HNN++) map every overlapping image patch to the Poincaré ball before evaluating its score. We show that the clipped score depends on each patch only through two statistics, its squared norm and its inner products with the filters, which a native convolution and a single-channel box filter compute directly. Replacing the convolutions of the official PyTorch Hyperbolic U-Net makes its training step 1.4 times faster with half the memory. Evaluating an existing layer from its patch statistics thus makes Poincaré convolutional networks cheaper to train in the tested models and software stacks.

On the Limits of Sufficient Explanations
Klaus Weinbauer, Peter F. Stadler, Thomas Gärtner, Sagar Malhotra
cs.LG
Abstract
We formally characterize the conditions under which sufficient explanations exist for classifiers on structured data such as graphs and strings. We focus on post-hoc explainers that select parts of the input as sufficient explanations. An explanation is sufficient if the selected part alone ensures the predicted label. Sufficient explanations have been widely adopted across data modalities, including graphs, strings, and images. However, a unified formal analysis of sufficient explanations remains missing. We bridge this gap with four main contributions: (i) We define a general explanation formalism consisting of an instance space, a classifier, and a select relation. The select relation specifies which potential sub-instances or feature subsets can be selected as explanations from each instance; (ii) We show that the existence of sufficient explainers is fully characterized by the interplay between the classifier and the select relation; (iii) We extend this analysis to practically relevant variants of sufficiency, including probabilistic sufficiency and deletion sufficiency; (iv) Based on the formal analysis, we devise statistics that bound the maximum achievable sufficiency for a given task. We evaluate these statistics on language, protein, and molecular classification datasets and find that full sufficiency is unattainable on most of them. Since these bounds hold for every explainer, they provide a task-level reference against which any explainer's sufficiency can be judged in absolute terms.

MarathonTS: a benchmark of long synthetic time series from window assembly and long-series generators
Ilyas Varshavskiy, Sergey Shulgin, Shuhrat Khalilbekov, Bonu Boboeva, Azizjon Azimi
cs.LG
Abstract
Many applications of synthetic time series require a series that is much longer than the windows on which a generative model is trained. Such a series can be assembled from a pool of short generated windows, which requires deciding which windows are taken, in what order, and how their seams are processed, or it can be produced directly by a long-series generator. This study presents MarathonTS, to our knowledge the first open benchmark that compares both families under one protocol. Every method produces a series of the same length on 9 open hourly datasets with 10 seeds. The series is evaluated by its fidelity to the real data (83 metrics in 12 groups, against three real references: train, validation and an out-of-time split), by its utility (models are trained on the synthetic series only and tested on real data, TSTR against TRTR, in 14 classification and regression tasks), by its memorisation of the training data and by its computational cost. The statistical analysis is carried out as a separate stage that reads the published tables and gives the rankings of every method in every slice, the answers to the research questions with tests and uncertainty, and the results of a pre-specified confirmatory family. In the main run, the methods differ in both confirmatory cells; the choice of windows matters more than the processing of their seams, which does not improve the series, and no method trains the downstream models as well as the real series. The top tier of the leaderboard is reproduced on other hardware, whereas the exact places within it are not. For the reproducibility of the study, the code, the data hashes and all results are publicly available (links below).

Yue Xu, Kaiyun Wei, Wenying Duan, Zichen Xu, Shuai Ma, Xiaoxi He
cs.LG · cs.AI
Abstract
Spatio-temporal foundation models (STFMs) require large-scale training corpora, yet synchronized observations and spatial metadata are costly to acquire and curate, limiting the availability of suitable data. By contrast, time-series data are abundant and easier to collect. Since observations at each location form a time series, this motivates a natural question: can abundant time-series data enhance STFMs when spatio-temporal observations are scarce? To bridge this gap, we propose T2ST, a data-efficient training framework that augments limited spatio-temporal observations with selected auxiliary time series and transfers knowledge from a frozen time-series foundation model. Using only 10\% of the original spatio-temporal data, T2ST enables STFMs to achieve overall forecasting performance competitive with or superior to their full-data counterparts across six long-term and twelve short-term forecasting datasets. These results establish a new paradigm for data-efficient training of spatio-temporal foundation models. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/T2ST-65F3/.

cs.LG / 62 / 2611.18238
Small Is Not Dead: Distance-Aware Cluster Revival for e-means
Eyal Weiss
cs.LG · stat.ML
Abstract
e-means (Bessa et al., 2026) is a recent mini-batch k-means algorithm, now part of the FAISS library, that reaches the error of Lloyd's algorithm about an order of magnitude faster on large embedding datasets. Every five mini-batches, it removes every cluster whose running point count falls below a tenth of the average and reuses its centre to split a large cluster. In the authors' ablation, removing this mechanism, called dead-cluster revival, raises the error more than removing any other component added to the moving-average update. The rule, however, considers only cluster size: it cannot distinguish a cluster that is small because it is redundant from one that covers a rare, isolated group of points, and on data with such groups it repeatedly removes centres whose removal is costly. We add a second condition, based on the distance between centres: a small cluster is removed only if its centre is close to another centre, relative to a running estimate of the mean squared error. When a centre equals the mean of its points, this squared distance is an upper bound on the average increase in squared error, per point of the cluster, caused by removing the centre. The condition costs one nearest-centre search for the small clusters every five mini-batches and adds no measurable running time. On the e-means paper's embedding benchmarks, it never prevents a removal, so the results are identical to those of FAISS. On datasets with small isolated groups not used during development, it reduces the test error after two epochs by roughly 10% to 80% relative to count-based revival, and it preserves the advantage of k-means++ initialization; on the other evaluation datasets, it stays within about 1% of the better of revival enabled and disabled. We also report two failure modes that the analysis predicts. This is a CPU proof of concept; GPU implementations are left to future work.

Neural Classifiers as Error-Correcting Codes
Sergey Salishev, Anton Makarov, Oleg Granichin, Renata Avros, Zeev Volkovich
cs.LG · cs.IT
Abstract
A unified framework is proposed that characterizes supervised deep classification as the decoding of error-correcting codes in the Shannon sense. Cross-entropy optimization is shown to align softmax classifiers with maximum-likelihood decoding, where logits map to Hamming distances to class-specific codewords. This correspondence explains the emergence of standard divergence objectives (e.g., Kullback-Leibler, Jeffreys) and provides a geometric basis for the stability of decision boundaries. Furthermore, the geometries of Neural Collapse and LeJEPA are identified as optimal constellations for decoding under isotropic noise. Unlike classical Error-Correcting Output Codes, deep networks are shown to learn effective codebooks in the feature space implicitly. This insight is leveraged to derive novel criteria for lossless quantization, improved knowledge distillation, and architectural regularization based on code redundancy. Finally, this perspective is extended to self-supervised encoders, offering a cohesive explanation for the geometry of latent representations in modern neural models.

ParetoM$^3$: Learning on the Pareto Set under Preference Guidance via Min-Max-Min Optimization
Pei Tang, Songtao Lu
cs.LG · cs.AI · math.OC
Abstract
Learning across multiple inherently conflicting objectives requires selecting desirable trade-offs from the Pareto set. In this work, we study preference-guided multi-objective optimization, where a predefined preference function is minimized over the weak Pareto set of multiple nonconvex objectives. Leveraging a merit function that vanishes on the weak Pareto set, we propose a penalty-based min-max-min formulation, termed the M$^3$ problem, which unifies preference optimization and the enforcement of weak Pareto optimality in a single objective. We further develop ParetoM$^3$, a single-loop first-order algorithm for computing stationary solutions of the resulting M$^3$ problem. Under a local Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz assumption, we prove that ParetoM$^3$ finds an $ε$-stationary point of the M$^3$ problem that is also approximately weakly Pareto optimal for the original multi-objective problem when the penalty parameter is sufficiently large, with explicit non-asymptotic convergence rates. We also show that this stationarity notion recovers existing optimality criteria, including approximate preference stationarity and approximate Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions, under the same assumptions used in prior works. Experiments on a synthetic benchmark, image classification, regression, and math reasoning demonstrate that ParetoM$^3$ reliably attains preferred weak Pareto optimal solutions while achieving strong practical performance. Code is available at https://github.com/tpgoodboy/ParetoM3.

Clean Tickets, Wrong Orders: Catching Silent Errors with a Language Model in Your Browser Tab
Umar Ahmed, Maxim Dunaev, Mohammad Nour Al Awad, Sergey Ivanov, Ivan Khodnenko
cs.LG · cs.CL · cs.HC
Abstract
A small language model that turns a typed order into structure can fail in two ways. It can print something broken, which any system rejects. Or it can print a clean, well formed order that nobody placed. Our companion work calls the second kind a silent error, and it is the dangerous one. We present a web demo in which a fine-tuned 0.8B parameter model runs entirely inside the visitor's browser tab, through llama.cpp compiled to WebAssembly. No order text leaves the device. The page parses orders as the visitor types, scores every ticket with the PIZZA benchmark's own matcher ported to TypeScript, refuses anything off the menu with "?" so the model can face the open web, and lets visitors hunt for silent errors or try to cause one. Run in the browser on all 1,357 PIZZA test orders, the deployed model writes the same output as the server evaluation for 99.0% of them and reaches the same exact match (0.882); we also report its latency on 3 consumer laptops. Demo: https://wrong.cuyor.com

Emulating physics-based XCO2 retrievals across OCO-2 viewing modes with compact neural networks
Xianlei San, Chengxing Zhai, Hui Su, Kuai Dai, Shuangchen Du
cs.LG
Abstract
Operational full-physics retrieval of the column-averaged dry-air mole fraction of carbon dioxide from satellite spectra is computationally demanding. We use singular value decomposition to compress spectra from the Orbiting Carbon Observatory-2 separately by band. Compact multilayer perceptrons use the resulting spectral scores, prior information, and viewing geometry to predict the difference between the full-physics retrieval and its operational prior. Adding the predicted difference to the prior yields an estimate of the retrieved mole fraction. The models were trained on 2015-2019 observations and validated on 2020 observations. In 2021-2025 tests, the mean annual root-mean-square errors relative to the Atmospheric Carbon Dioxide Observations from Space retrieval product were 0.89, 1.35, and 1.43 parts per million for ocean glint, land glint, and nadir observations, respectively. On East Asian nadir observations screened using the published criteria for the spectral Transformer, our model yielded a lower mean annual error than reported for that model in 2020-2022. These results show that compact neural networks using compressed spectra can efficiently emulate the full-physics retrieval across multiple viewing modes and years.

DECODE: Decomposable Environments for COntrolled Sequential-Decision Evaluation
Diego Silva Prado, Bruno Caldas de Souza, Tiago Amaral Penteado de Jesus, Ricardo Guimarães Herrmann, Cristiano Breuel, Cristiano Bevitori Maffia De Oliveira
cs.LG · q-fin.RM · stat.ML
Abstract
When a sequential decision system falls short of an optimum, its score does not reveal which modifications would improve its decisions. DECODE is a benchmark for testing such modifications through controlled experiments on planning and available information. Its consumer-credit collections generator provides five partially observed environments that share known dynamics matrices and vary a memory parameter and process noise. The experiments change selected inputs or planning rules while keeping the rest of the solver fixed. Each comparison specifies the solver's state inputs, response inputs and planning horizon. On the deterministic base environment, which was selected for a large gap between six-month and one-month planning with the hidden state, extending planning from one to six months increases the scores of two gradient-boosted solvers by 15 to 17 percentage points of the exact optimum, computed with the hidden state and borrower traits. Even at this horizon, the best graded solver, whose repayment response is fitted on logs, remains 15.6 points below that optimum. In a specified intervention sequence, three changes to its state inputs and their evaluation during search lower its score by 6.3 points, and replacing its fitted repayment model with the true response, evaluated with hidden borrower traits, then raises it by 15.6, a net recovery of nine of those points. Because the intermediate effects are conditional on the repayment model and the order of the changes, they do not uniquely assign the gap to separate causes. Within these limits, DECODE makes hypotheses about solver performance testable through specified interventions under known dynamics and explicit information conditions.

cs.LG / 68 / 2611.18382
Where Tree Ensembles Decide: Structural Local Explanation with Decision Predicate Graphs
Sylvio Barbon Junior, Luca Longo
cs.LG
Abstract
Tree ensembles are among the most accurate models for tabular data, yet the computation behind a single prediction is spread over hundreds of decision rules. Feature attributions and counterfactuals explain such predictions through the influence of features or a change of the input, leaving open which of the thresholds applied by the trees a prediction responds to, while structural explanations compress these rules and lose the trees behind them. To address this gap, we propose a local Decision Predicate Graph (local DPG), which gathers the rules executed for a sample into a verifiable record of the computation, with a class support that reproduces every explained prediction. From these rules we select the critical predicate, the executed predicate whose crossing is estimated to move the model most strongly toward a chosen class, which offers the user a concrete threshold and the trees that share it, without an external explainer. Across 16 datasets and four ensemble families, it separates differently predicted neighbours better than random and nearest-threshold predicates. A single crossing recovers the true class in 46-69% of wrong predictions, against 29-50% when the same rule is applied to the feature ranked first by TreeSHAP, a measure close to the selection criterion, and on planted faults it finds the faulty threshold in 46-71% of the resulting errors, against 6-21% at random. Although training-free edits of these predicates did not reduce the error on unseen data, the critical predicate complements attributions and counterfactuals by naming where a prediction can be changed.

cs.LG / 69 / 2611.18393
WeatherDreamer: Learning a World Model from Multimodal Earth Observations
Kate Duffy, Elise Penn, Tristan Ballard, August Posch, Thomas J. Vandal
cs.LG · physics.ao-ph
Abstract
Machine learning weather models now rival operational global forecasts but still start from analyses produced by separate, expensive data assimilation (DA) systems, which for global models are typically updated every six hours. Observation-to-observation models avoid this dependence, but most omit the recursive filtering cycle that lets DA systems carry a background state through sparse, irregular observations. We introduce WeatherDreamer, a world model that learns this cycle end-to-end from 18 global observing systems, without reanalysis. It infers a latent state from observations, carries it forward hour by hour with learned dynamics, corrects it each hour with new observations, and can predict what any of its observing systems would measure at any location. At held-out stations, its ensembles beat NOAA's Global Ensemble Forecast System (GEFS) for temperature, dew point and wind out to 12 h and are better calibrated than GEFS and the ECMWF ensemble. Precipitation, cloud-top height and solar irradiance, never inputs at inference, are inferred with skill matching or exceeding operational products at short leads. Against the first guess of NOAA's Gridpoint Statistical Interpolation (GSI) DA system, WeatherDreamer's short-range departures are within 10% of GSI's at surface stations and within 25% for moisture-sensitive microwave channels, but up to five times larger for temperature-sounding channels. Never given observation errors, its learned update assumes errors close to known instrument and retrieval errors of microwave sounders and scatterometer winds. WeatherDreamer also separates viewing geometry from atmospheric state, recovering 83-96% of observed limb darkening and producing seamless nadir-view imagery. Producing an hourly global analysis in about 1.5 minutes on one GPU, WeatherDreamer shows that assimilation and forecasting can be learned together directly from observations.

cs.LG / 70 / 2611.18396
Deep Neural Networks Training Efficiency: Does Grokking Modify its Dynamics?
Eduardo Cueto-Mendoza, John D. Kelleher
cs.LG
Abstract
We study whether grokking, the delayed emergence of out-of-distribution generalization after apparent overfitting, alters training efficiency in deep neural networks, where efficiency is measured as accuracy per unit energy. Using two canonical settings, modular arithmetic and MNIST, we analyze two neural network models under 16-, 32-, and 64-bit precision and use Orthograd to test whether grokking produces noticeable deviations from the typically decreasing dynamics of training efficiency. We consider both short training runs of up to 500 epochs and long training runs of up to 20,000 epochs, and in both cases identify grokking as a sharp improvement in test accuracy after a sustained train-test gap. In the short-horizon experiments, applying Orthograd at progressively stronger levels during early training does not induce grokking, and efficiency remains smoothly decreasing. In the long-horizon experiments, Orthograd induces grokking, but the efficiency curves remain approximately monotonic and show no appreciable local increase at the grokking transition. These results suggest that, in our setting, grokking does not materially alter overall training-efficiency dynamics.

Fuse-PINN: Combining Finite Element Methods and Physics-Informed Neural Networks to enable massively parallel simulations of non-linear PDEs
Stanislas Pamela, Nicolas Pailliez, Emmanuel Franck, Victor Michel-Dansac, Laurent Navoret, Vignesh Gopakumar
cs.LG · physics.plasm-ph
Abstract
Research in the domain of Physics-Informed Neural Networks applied to PDE modelling has advanced significantly in recent years, providing a wide ecosystem of novel methods. However, one frequent limitation is the inability of PINNs to scale out on High-Performance Computing architectures to address more ambitious challenges, particularly non-linear physics models with turbulent dynamics in large spatial domains. Typical studies restrain demonstrations to toy models with simple physics equations, few variables, and rarely any complex non-linear dynamics evolved over long timescales. Part of the AI community has even adopted the idea that "PINNs don't scale". This work proves otherwise. We present a mathematically robust formulation of Finite-Element-Method for Neural Networks (FEM-NN) implemented into a comprehensive, modular framework: Fuse-PINN. This formulation provides the basis for a sclable parallelisation of Physics-Informed Neural Networks on GPU-based HPC systems. Furthermore, an Energy Natural Gradient Descent optimiser is implemented to allow high-precision convergence. Fuse-PINN is demonstrated on physics models relevant to Magnetic Confinement Fusion, including non-linear convective models with fine-scale dynamics. Near-perfect scaling is achieved on multi-GPU runs with increasing problem size.

PACT: A Decoder-Confidence-Guided Two-Round Receiver for Joint Channel and Phase-Noise Estimation in Sub-THz OFDM
Ruilin Wang, Xiaodai Dong
cs.LG · eess.SP
Abstract
Frequency-selective fading and oscillator phase noise (PN) jointly impair coded sub-THz OFDM reception. We propose a decoder-confidence-guided two-round receiver combining a {pilot-aware complex transformer} (PACT) estimator, banded linear minimum mean-square error equalization, and neural normalized min-sum decoding. PACT jointly estimates the frequency-domain channel and effective time-domain PN through interacting complex-valued branches. Decoder-derived pseudo-pilots and continuous constellation-aware confidence guide second-round refinement through PACT inputs and attention, with parameters shared across rounds. Frozen-weight tests further demonstrate applicability across channel/PN models, carrier frequencies, and FFT/bandwidth configurations.

cs.LG
Abstract
Accurate prediction of high-entropy alloy (HEA) strength can guide composition and processing decisions; however, obtaining tensile-test data for HEAs can be costly and experimentally demanding. This study proposes Robust Outcome-aware Sequential Experimentation for High-Entropy Alloys (ROSE-HEA), a sample-efficient framework for predicting ultimate tensile strength (UTS) from alloy composition and processing conditions. ROSE-HEA also surpasses the strongest conventional regressor's mean predictive performance using 125 rather than 185 observations, corresponding to 32.4% fewer training observations. These findings demonstrate the potential of outcome-aware robust sequential learning to improve UTS prediction in HEAs under constrained training budgets.

cs.LG / 74 / 2611.18507
Asymptotic Margins and Feature Confinement in Conic Classification Heads under Softmax Cross-Entropy
Omer Nezih Gerek
cs.LG · eess.SP
Abstract
Classification heads built on conic functions give each class a bounded region of the feature space, and such regions are used to reject samples of unseen classes. We ask whether these regions also keep the features bounded when the head is trained with softmax cross-entropy. For a head whose class scores combine a linear term with a Euclidean norm, we first show that the region of each class is bounded by a quadric of revolution, whose eccentricity is the reciprocal of one learned ratio. We then attach to each class an asymptotic margin, which compares the growth of its score with the growth of all the other scores and can be computed by a second-order cone program. The loss of a class has infimum zero exactly when its margin is positive, and it then approaches zero only along diverging features. When the margin is negative, the loss is coercive and its minimizers lie in an explicit ball. Since softmax depends only on the differences between scores, a class with a bounded region can still have a positive margin, so bounded regions do not confine the features. The result extends to other norms, including the polyhedral conic functions. On CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, all 1210 cells of 67 trained conic heads have positive margins, whereas a prototype head keeps nine of its ten cells bounded, as the theory predicts.

cs.LG / 75 / 2611.18511
On numerical issues of divergences between nearly equal distributions
Brandon Amos
cs.LG · math.NA · stat.ML
Abstract
Barron et al. (2026) recently identified numerical issues in the softmax and soft cross-entropy loss. We show that similar issues are present in standard divergence operations between categorical distributions. This includes the forward and reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence and generalized Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD), as well as Monte Carlo KL estimates and entropy. When the distributions are nearly equal, rounding and cancellation issues affect the forward and gradient computations. These issues can affect settings using divergences in optimization objectives, such as knowledge distillation, reinforcement learning, and proximal methods. We propose numerically stable reformulations and provide example implementations for each setting, validated by property-based tests.

Daniel Grimm, Kyra Jongman, Renato Cordeiro Ferreira, Giuseppe Cascavilla, Alfredo Cuzzocrea, Willem-Jan Van Den Heuvel
cs.LG · cs.CR
Abstract
Monitoring maritime activities is important for safety and security, particularly for detecting illegal activities, but remains challenging due to the size and complexity of the maritime domain. The Automatic Identification System (AIS) is commonly used for this purpose, despite its known vulnerabilities. In contrast, space-based radio frequency (RF) observations provide an independent source of vessel activity but lack ship identification and therefore cannot identify vessels on their own. Following the CRISP-DM process, this study applies a Pair Hidden Markov Model (PHMM) to align individual RF detections with AIS trajectories, using temporal and spatial information while modeling noise and timing discrepancies. We evaluate it on NOAA AIS data with synthetic RF detections against nearest-neighbor baselines and analyze its errors with bias tests on trajectory length, sampling density, stationarity and AIS--RF distance. The length-corrected Forward score reaches a top-1 precision of $0.7776$ on the validation split of multi-candidate detections, where errors are possible, and an AUC of $0.637$ for detecting dark vessels among the detections that pass the prefilter. With a single RF detection, the score mostly reflects the AIS trajectory itself, and its errors favor longer and more stationary trajectories. A log-odds score, which compares the full model with a null model without match transitions, raises precision to $0.8580$ and AUC to $0.852$. Its length-corrected variant no longer shows a significant bias towards stationary trajectories and short inter-message distances, but still favors candidates farther from the RF detection. A time-weighted nearest-neighbor baseline without fitting remains ahead (precision $0.9013$, AUC $0.904$), so this single-detection, hand-designed PHMM formulation approaches but does not beat it.
