Omm Swarup, Shrishail M. Hiremath
cs.NI · cs.LG
Abstract
A model may produce a prediction with high confidence and still get the outcome wrong. This is a challenge in sequential applications, where the system evolves over time and deployment data may differ from the data used during training. To address this issue, we propose PRISM (Prediction Reliability and Intelligent Self-Correction Mechanism), a framework that evaluates prediction reliability after the initial output is generated. It estimates the risk of error and examines predictions identified as potentially unreliable. For these cases, PRISM analyses available signals, retrieves similar situations from historical data, and verifies whether an alternative prediction is supported by the evidence. It can then retain the original prediction, apply a verified correction, or abstain when the evidence is insufficient. We evaluate PRISM on second-innings match prediction in the Indian Premier League (IPL). Ball-by-ball records are converted into over-level observations to capture changes in match conditions throughout an innings. To preserve temporal order, earlier IPL seasons are used for training, IPL 2024 for validation, and IPL 2025-2026 for out-of-time testing. The dataset contains 8,665 over-level observations from 475 matches, with the test partition comprising 2,568 observations from 142 matches. In one experimental record, Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and HistGradientBoosting achieve accuracies of 65.2%, 68.4%, and 70.2%, respectively. The corresponding PRISM evaluation reports a selective accuracy of 82.4% at 88.5% coverage, with a Brier score of 0.126 and an expected calibration error (ECE) of 0.019.

cs.LG / 78 / 2611.17526
User-Schedulable Approximations for Quantized Tensor Programs
Alex Reinking
cs.PL · cs.LG · cs.PF
Abstract
Quantization lets language models run on edge devices, but it sharply increases the number of tensor kernels a library must maintain: every weight format must be paired with every activation format on every instruction set and GPU, with an encoder and a decoder that agree on the stored bytes. In GGML, the tensor library used by llama.cpp, these kernels are written by hand, and only 8 of the 54 weight-activation pairs it runs on our Arm CPU have a tiled matrix-multiplication kernel. We extend Halide's separation of algorithm and schedule to the representation of values. Each approximation is a codec, an encoder paired with a decoder, composed from reusable units: quantizers, integer representations, bit packers and layouts. A new directive, approximate_by, applies a codec to one stage of a program and returns the encoder and decoder as ordinary stages that Halide can schedule, so both decoders fuse into the register tiles of a matrix product. Optional contracts, such as a round-trip law, are checked by property tests; the law exposed an inconsistent block sum in GGML's q8_1. One algorithm and one schedule library generate 347 kernels for 31 of GGML's and KleidiAI's formats on Arm CPUs and Apple GPUs, covering 38 weight-activation pairs for which GGML has no kernel, in 3.7x less code than GGML and KleidiAI. On an Apple M3 Max, our kernels reach at least 95% of the speed of GGML's format-specific kernels in 91.0% of the cases that have one, with a median speedup of 1.13x, and are faster still where GGML falls back to blocked dot products. Counting Apple's BLAS library, which is usually faster for larger f32 products, we still reach that threshold in 86.9% of CPU cases. A port to x86 with Intel's AMX adds 793 lines of code and matches GGML in 83.5% of its cases.

Konstantinos Mylonas, Thrasyvoulos Spyropoulos
cs.RO · cs.LG
Abstract
Image-goal planning with a frozen visual world model depends on how predicted observations are scored against the goal. Mean-squared error (MSE) between embeddings from a pretrained visual encoder can provide a useful signal near a goal, yet favor states across an obstacle that are visually similar to the goal and difficult to reach. We fit a small residual adapter on top of frozen I-JEPA embeddings to improve this latent goal cost. Starting near each goal, a planner collects trajectories from progressively wider regions of initial states. The results indicate that planning experience can improve a frozen visual representation's goal cost without retraining the encoder or the world model.

Beyond Prediction Gain: LASR as a Metric for Action-Grounded Latents in World Models
Yu Zhou, Changsong Lei, Yixiang Wei, Kairui Wen, Huankun Sheng, Ming Li, Yeying Fan, Yu-Hui Wen, Yong-Jin Liu
cs.RO · cs.LG
Abstract
World models that support planning, control, and interpretable reasoning require latent representations that genuinely encode action. Yet the large-scale video data from which such models are learned overwhelmingly lack action annotations. Latent-action models infer action-like variables from observation sequences, enabling action-aware learning from unlabeled videos or trajectories. A fundamental concern, however, remains: a latent that improves future prediction may simply encode the current state, episode progress, task identity, or other predictive shortcuts. Existing evaluation practices---reconstruction loss, rollout fidelity, action recovery, or downstream performance---cannot isolate this distinction. We introduce the Latent Action Support Ratio (LASR), a dimensionless metric that measures, under offline evaluation on held-out data, whether a learned latent provides predictive support for action-related targets beyond the current state and non-action alternatives. LASR provides two complementary summaries: GeoLASR captures typical support, while WorstLASR identifies the weakest case. We instantiate the LASR benchmark with recorded actions and corresponding physical state changes as targets, episode-held-out splits, one predictor type for all comparisons, and positive and negative controls. Experiments on recorded robot manipulation data and a matched multi-task panel demonstrate that LASR detects known action information, rejects representative non-action shortcuts, and enables graded comparisons among imperfect latent representations. We further use LASR to train and audit a lightweight residual adapter, obtaining descriptively better point estimates whose uncertainty is assessed with paired comparisons. LASR thus provides a principled offline protocol for measuring action-related predictive support in world-model latents.

Abstract
Large language and vision-language models have substantially advanced robotic behavior planning from natural-language instructions and visual observations. We propose a safety-aware behavior planning framework based on an explicit Constraint Space that stores reusable textual safety rules. Rather than retrieving rules solely by semantic similarity, we formulate constraint selection as task- and environment-conditioned applicability learning. A lightweight query-conditioned dual-tower model identifies the constraints applicable to the current context, which are then provided together with the original task and image to a multimodal behavior planner. The resulting framework integrates explicit safety knowledge representation,context-aware constraint selection, corrective safety inspection, and test-time knowledge adaptation for safety-aware robotic behavior planning.

Abstract
Robot-use agents (RUAs) have emerged as a promising approach to general-purpose robot control. We show that an open-weight 27B reasoning model can be trained to use real robots natively through reinforcement learning, entirely in simulation and without demonstrations. After training on LIBERO and RoboDojo in simulation, the model succeeds in 60% of our trials on a real dual-arm robot, whereas the base model succeeds in none. On LIBERO, training raises the success rate from 1.6% to 74.4%, approaching GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. Compared with $π_{0.5}$, a vision-language-action model fine-tuned on demonstrations, our model is substantially more robust to changes in the task itself. On LIBERO-Pro, $π_{0.5}$ succeeds in only 0.75% of episodes when assigned a new task in a familiar scene whereas our model succeeds in 57.4%. Finally, the model retains its general capabilities, with no measurable degradation on nine reasoning, coding and vision benchmarks. These results highlight robot-use reinforcement learning as a promising component of foundation-model post-training.

A thorough review on Deep Learning approaches to 2D-to-3D Registration in Medical Imaging
Andrea Borghesi, Julia van Beurden, Maarten van Alphen, Michiel van den Brekel, Luc Karssemakers, Jan-Jakob Sonke, Baris Karakullukcu, Jonas Teuwen, George Yiasemis
eess.IV · cs.CV · cs.LG · physics.med-ph
Abstract
2D-to-3D image registration is a key component for several medical imaging purposes such as intraoperative guidance, enabling the alignment between preoperative 3D volumes and real-time 2D images. While conventional optimisation-based methods often struggle with non-convex objectives, sensitivity to initialisation, and high computational demands, deep learning (DL) has emerged as a promising alternative, offering improved alignment robustness and the potential for real-time performance. This review provides a comprehensive analysis of DL frameworks developed to address the challenges inherent in 2D-to-3D registration. We first outline how core concepts of DL are adapted to the registration problem. We then examine recent methodological advancements from multiple perspectives, including pipeline design, architectural innovations, and domain adaptation strategies. Particular attention is given to quantitative metrics, highlighting how performance is defined and measured across studies. Across the 41 included studies, we find that direct regression is the most common pipeline (23 studies, against 18 iterative ones), that hybrid CNN-attention architectures are gaining ground, and that research remains heavily concentrated on X-ray-to-CT registration. We conclude by identifying the absence of per-case uncertainty estimation and the strong neglect of temporal or sequential modelling as the two methodological gaps most consequential for clinical readiness, and discuss state-space models as a way to add temporal memory within intraoperative latency budgets. Further opportunities lie in more principled domain adaptation and in the integration of foundation models for representation learning.

Convergence Properties of PF-Exec: Homogeneous Structure, Anchored Closed Form, and Gain Dynamics
Hanzhang Jia, Liheng Zeng, Yiran Li, Yi Gao, Bo Ma
math.OC · cs.LG · eess.SP
Abstract
This paper establishes a layered mathematical char acterisation of PF-Exec, the algorithm of the companion system paper. Demand-proportional bandwidth allocation yields an exact first-order homogeneous decomposition, from which the equal-load bandwidth target and its feasibility bound follow in closed form. The logarithmic knee view preserves strict load order and shares its global optimum with the Jain fairness index, while the demand-weighted centroid minimises the position potential. The damped softmax association block possesses a fixed point on the product of simplices. The truncated one-dimensional gain iteration admits a complete characterisation, with geomet ric convergence below the critical gain, initial-value-dependent bounded oscillation at it, and a family of bounded two-state cycles above it. A global invariant error ball and an explicit tracking recursion complete the execution guarantee. A numerical audit confirms the structural identities on every measured state and quantifies the local amplification of the coupled association and resource dynamics. Together, the results explain how closed form recovery directions and bounded adaptive gains provide stable execution without requiring a global Euclidean contraction assumption.

Online Switching Control as Learning with Experts
Masataka Nakamura, Kaito Ito, Hideaki Ishii
math.OC · cs.LG · eess.SY
Abstract
This paper addresses the problem of online switching control for discrete-time nonlinear systems where a controller is sequentially selected from a finite set of candidates. The goal is to generate an adaptive sequence of controllers to minimize the cumulative cost under unknown disturbances and cost functions. Specifically, we consider a full-information feedback setting where realized disturbance effects and cost functions are available after each selection. Using counterfactual costs computed from historical data, we reduce the online switching control problem to the experts problem with switching costs. Then, we propose an adaptive switching algorithm based on the Hedge algorithm with a switching suppression mechanism. Notably, the proposed algorithm achieves an expected regret of $O(\sqrt{T\log N})$, where $T$ is the time horizon and $N$ is the number of candidate controllers, while ensuring uniform boundedness of the state. This matches the optimal regret order for the standard experts problem.

Contrastive local learning in large nonlinear analog circuits
Pragyan Pandey, Peter Wu, Deema Alyahya, Eric Y. Ma
physics.app-ph · cs.LG
Abstract
Machine learning in physical hardware promises to reduce training cost by colocating computation, memory and adaptation within device physics. Contrastive local learning networks (CLLNs) offer a compelling route: an electrical circuit relaxes to equilibrium in free and weakly clamped states, and each adaptive element updates from local voltage contrasts rather than backpropagated error signals. Yet whether nonlinear transistor implementations can scale beyond proof-of-concept circuits, and what design principles control that scaling, have remained unknown. Here we show that nonlinear analog circuits trained by contrastive local rules can learn larger machine-learning tasks and remain robust under hardware nonidealities. Using device-level circuit simulations, we systematically explore initialization, operating regime, loss-dependent clamping and training parameters, and scale CLLNs beyond ten thousand adaptive edges. The same local rule trains classifiers that reach the accuracy regime of conventional digital methods on Ionosphere and handwritten-digit benchmarks, and a network that autoregressively generates language over a 122-token vocabulary. These results establish the feasibility of local learning in large nonlinear analog circuits.

physics.soc-ph · cs.LG
Abstract
Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) have recently been applied to epidemic modeling and forecasting with promising results. Here, we conduct a systematic comparison of a PINN-based framework and a stochastic mechanistic model. Abstract
Recent advances in speech neuroprostheses have enabled new communication avenues for people who have lost the ability to speak due to neurological illness or injury. These systems can decode neural activity during attempted speech into text by relying on language priors to achieve high decoding accuracy. This performance currently comes at the cost of using a compute-intensive language modeling stack, presenting a major bottleneck for real-world use. Further, current language models incur highly variable and disruptive inference latencies, limiting communication throughput and preventing users from reliably participating in natural conversation. We introduce FETTUCCINE, an end-to-end brain-to-text framework that leverages the speed and portability of automatic speech recognition (ASR) models to address the aforementioned practical challenges, while achieving usable (<10%) word error rates (WER), efficient adaptation to neural non-stationarities, and reliable performance on mobile devices. We also show that our models can be successfully finetuned to extend usable performance to future days. Beyond performance, our approach enables localization of the neural features most relevant for decoding via gradient-based saliency maps, which we show align with well-established physiological priors across different ASR model families. Taken together, our results show that FETTUCCINE overcomes core infrastructural and computational barriers, yielding a new class of portable brain-to-text communication.

cs.LG / 91 / 2611.18378
Structure makes quantum circuits trainable
Wenbo Xie, Wojciech Szpankowski, Ananth Grama
quant-ph · cs.LG
Abstract
The scalability of variational quantum algorithms is limited by barren plateaus, where gradients vanish exponentially in system size. This worst-case result raises fundamental questions about the trainability of quantum AI models. We present a major advance towards resolving this trainability question by answering whether a given quantum circuit has a barren plateau. We do this using an analytical framework that bounds the gradient variance for arbitrary input states $ρ$ and observables $\mathcal{M}$ by decomposing the unitary representation of the associated compact Lie group. When the circuit segments on both sides of a gate form $2$-designs, we obtain the variance exactly and prove a sufficient condition for trainability: if $ρ^{\otimes 2}$ and $\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}$ are sufficiently concentrated on the same irreducible invariant subspace of polynomial dimension, and the differentiated gate moves this subspace by an inverse-polynomial amount, then the gradient variance is at least inverse-polynomial. We further present a sampling algorithm that estimates the gradient variance from runs of the ansatz, without constructing the representation-theoretic projectors. Our algorithm decides whether the variance exceeds a tolerance $τ$ with the number of runs polynomial in $1/τ$, including cases in which the bounds are inconclusive. This resolves the question of the trainability of the circuit before expensive training procedures.

Abstract
Autonomous experimentation systems require characterization models that can interpret heterogeneous measurements and maintain predictive performance as data accumulate. Developing such models involves addressing domain mismatch between data sources, overconfident predictions on unfamiliar inputs, and catastrophic forgetting during sequential updates. In this work, we present a proof-of-concept framework for uncertainty-aware continual interpretation of two-dimensional X-ray diffraction (XRD) data. Predictive uncertainty is also evaluated on unfamiliar and noisy inputs. The contribution is the integration and evaluation of these capabilities for sequential XRD interpretation. The framework provides a basis for future integration into autonomous experimentation systems, with experimental planning, instrument control, and closed-loop deployment remaining areas for future development.

Training-point consistency of kernel ridge regression with varying correlation scales
Weiyan Mu, Shifeng Xiong, Yuming Shen
stat.ML · cs.LG · math.ST
Abstract
The scale parameter in kernel ridge regression plays a pivotal role in modeling the correlation structure of data. In modern applications, sampling density and the underlying correlation range often change simultaneously, making a fixed-scale assumption highly restrictive. However, establishing asymptotic theories under varying scale parameters is fundamentally challenging. To address this gap, this paper develops a unified theoretical framework for kernel ridge regression under varying scale parameters. We establish that, on uniform tensor grids with product exponential kernels, the consistency of the training-point risk holds if and only if the effective local sample size diverges to infinity. This sharp criterion universally covers all regimes along arbitrary deterministic scale sequences, seamlessly unifying the long-range, fixed-scale, and short-range. Furthermore, we provide an explicit, signal-independent construction of a deterministic regularization penalty that guarantees this consistency. Numerical experiments illustrate the criterion and the corresponding penalty across all the three regimes.

Abstract
Foundation models produce high-dimensional latent representations in which suitably designed statistical decision rules can be effective yet analytically tractable. Together, these results establish LaMe as a practical framework for estimating and summarizing multivariate mediation systems under explicit structural and measurement assumptions.

cs.LG / 97 / 2611.18394
Lipschitz-Constrained Discriminators in Generative Modeling: Asymptotic Behavior and Support Mismatch
Eduardo Pinto, Panagiota Birmpa
stat.ML · cs.LG
Abstract
We study the asymptotic behavior of optimal discriminators associated to $L-$Lipschitz regularized KL-divergences of two probability distributions $Q$ and $P$ that interpolate between the rescaled Wasserstein$-1$ metric by some parameter $L>0$ and the KL divergence. The parameter $L$ plays the role of a Lipschitz constraint and is viewed as a regularization parameter: small values of $L$ enforce a more geometry-aware discriminator, while as $L$ increases the geometric regularization is progressively relaxed. It is a known result [3] that such divergences converge to the KL divergence as $L\to\infty$. In this paper, we characterize the asymptotic behavior of the optimal discriminators as $L\to\infty$ in different regimes: Firstly, under $Q\ll P$ and the Radon-Nikodym derivative $\frac{dQ}{dP}$ is bounded from above and bounded away from zero. Secondly, when $Q$ and $P$ are discrete distributions satisfying $Q\ll P$, and finally when $Q$ and $P$ are non absolutely continuous discrete distributions. This result motivates training strategies which involve discriminators, such as adversarial generative models. In practice, the data distribution is often represented by a discrete empirical measure, while, especially at the early stages of the training, the model distribution may have little or no overlap with the empirical data distribution.

Contrastive latent representation learning with denoising score matching
Christopher Marouani, Grigory Bartosh, Christian A. Naesseth
stat.ML · cs.LG
Abstract
Contrastive representation learning methods operate by pulling different views of the same data sample together while pushing them away from views of different samples. A fundamental challenge is the estimation of the repulsive force, which typically requires large batch sizes, can exhibit high variance, and is susceptible to the curse of dimensionality. We introduce ScoreCLR, a contrastive representation learning method that offers a novel way to estimate this repulsion term. We consider the framework of representation learning by maximizing the mutual information between two latent views of a sample. By deriving the gradient of the Shannon entropy with respect to the encoder parameters, we obtain an equivalent objective that involves the score of the latent representation. We then use denoising score matching techniques to estimate this score with a lightweight network that is trained jointly with the encoder. We further propose regularization techniques using different noise levels and a Gaussian prior on the latent representation. ScoreCLR replaces the batch of explicit negative samples in contrastive methods with a learned repulsion whose cost is linear in the batch size rather than quadratic. On CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-100 it does so with minimal loss of linear-probe accuracy compared to SimCLR under a shared recipe, while producing projector outputs of higher effective rank, closer to the Gaussian prior.

cs.LG / 99 / 2611.18480
Bregman geometry in regular exponential families: dual representations and relative smoothness
Mame Diarra Fall, Éric Barat, Nicolas Dobigeon
stat.ML · cs.LG · math.OC
Abstract
Regular exponential-family distributions are ubiquitous in statistics due to their appealing and convenient properties. Notably, they can be expressed in terms of Bregman divergences, revealing fundamental connections between statistics, convex analysis, and information geometry. However, this geometric structure has not been systematically exploited to characterize these distributions with respect to their expectation parametrization. Building on a projective characterization, we establish a general construction of equivalent dual Bregman representations, together with normalization and uniqueness results. These representations express log-densities as ordinary Bregman divergences in dual coordinate systems, providing geometric characterizations of the parameter space. So-called $β$- and $α$-scaled dual representations emerge as particular instances of this framework. We further derive dual Bregman representations of conjugate priors and establish that they preserve conjugacy. Applications to classical distributions illustrate the geometric structures underlying seemingly different statistical models. Moreover, we show that these representations provide natural reference geometries for establishing relative smoothness of the primal Bregman representation of the distribution, with explicit bounds that are sharp in the scalar and separable settings. An illustrative application to the Poisson distribution sheds new light on several works in the literature, providing complementary geometric justifications for reference geometries while deriving existing algorithms.

cs.NE / 1 / 2611.17455
TSGP-2: On the Non-Linearity of Symbolic Regression Problems and How to Exploit It with Model-based Semantic Genetic Programming
Philipp Anthes, Alina Geiger, Martin Briesch, Franz Rothlauf
cs.NE
Abstract
Model-based variation operators such as the one used in Transformer Semantic Genetic Programming (TSGP) are affected by possible biases of their training data. TSGP is trained, in particular, on programs generated by Genetic Programming (GP) runs on linear target functions, which leads to a bias toward linear offspring. This bias can hinder search performance on problems that require non-linear solutions. This work has two goals: first, it investigates the linearity of standard test problems such as the Feynman and real-world benchmark problems. While most of the structure of real-world problems can be well approximated by linear structures, the Feynman problems exhibit varying levels of non-linear structure. Thus, a proper variation operator of a search method should be able to create both types of programs, linear as well as non-linear ones. Motivated by this observation, we propose TSGP-2, which follows the functionality of TSGP but uses as training data programs from GP runs on both linear and non-linear target functions. As a result, the training data includes a lower share of programs that behave linearly (the share drops from 83% to 37%). With a higher share of non-linear structures, TSGP-2 outperforms TSGP on problems with non-linear structure, while performing similarly on linear problems. In addition, TSGP-2 is less biased toward linear offspring and is able to propose a higher number of meaningful non-linear offspring earlier during search, which coincides with better solutions. The findings underscore the importance of training data for a learned variation operator and emphasize that the biases of search operators should be aligned with the problems they are applied to.

In an era where linguistic creativity is challenged by algorithmic authorship, we pose a provocative question: can artificial intelligence (AI) write an infinite poem? Creating Steering Vectors via Input Optimisation
Batuhan Akar
cs.CL · cs.LG
Abstract
Activation steering influences a language model's behaviour by adding a steering vector to its residual stream during generation, but it requires white-box access to modify the hidden states at every generation step. We introduce ActGCG, a modification of Greedy Coordinate Gradient which optimises a suffix to match the target activations obtained by adding a steering vector to the residual stream. cs.CL / 3 / 2611.17563
Benchmarks Don't Talk Back: Learning Through Self-Disagreement
Soheil Abbasloo, Peng Cheng
cs.CL · cs.AI · cs.LG
Abstract
A benchmark usually measures a language model alone with a question, and deployment rarely looks like that: answers get contested, and models give up correct ones under counter-reasoning that is itself wrong. We show that what fails there is not a shortfall of accuracy but a capability in its own right -- knowing when to hold a position and when to yield to a better one -- which we call dialectical competence. It stands on first-answer accuracy without following from it: a model can be made materially better at answering and no better at this, and training for accuracy alone can drift against it. Nor can it be bought at inference, by self-reflection or by sampling more. Taking a cue from the dialectical tradition in philosophy, in which productive opposition arises within a position rather than being imposed on it from outside, we hypothesize that this capability can be trained from opposition the model supplies itself. It states a position, generates the strongest case against that position -- its antithesis -- rules on whether the case stands, and is trained on that ruling, with no second model at training or at deployment. We call this learning through Self-Disagreement: reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards throughout, where the same check that scores an answer against its reference scores the ruling too, so no reward model, no preference data and no human annotation enter anywhere. The capability is trained rather than elicited: weak in the base model, it arrives with the reward, is not paid for out of first-answer accuracy, and holds against challengers the model never trained against, including ones stronger than itself. The opposition a model needs in order to improve, it turns out, need not come from outside it.

Abstract
Cameroon Pidgin English (a West African Pidgin English variety) and Camfranglais are widely used in Cameroon, yet to our knowledge, neither has a dedicated spoken Natural Language Inference (NLI) resource. We introduce CamSpokenNLI, a pilot dataset of 114 scenario-matched spoken NLI items in each variety (228 in total), recorded by one native speaker from Buea. Each item pairs premise audio and text with a hypothesis, an NLI label, and English glosses. The author assigned labels, so the dataset is an exploratory resource rather than a mature benchmark. The NLI experiments use the reference text; the audio is evaluated separately. XLM-RoBERTa baselines with five-fold item-level cross-validation reach 44.7% on Pidgin and 46.2% on Camfranglais (chance 33.3%). Still, neither is statistically distinguishable from hypothesis-only input, so hypothesis wording may account for much of the accuracy. Under disjoint-item cross-variety transfer, where a test item's counterpart is never seen in training, accuracy is 40.1% (Pidgin to Camfranglais) and 45.6% (Camfranglais to Pidgin). A counterpart-leak audit raises this to 60-64%, illustrating how counterpart leakage can overstate transfer. Zero-shot Whisper transcription of the recordings performs poorly: the best word error rates are 66.6% for Pidgin and 45.5% for Camfranglais, and with automatic language identification, Whisper Large-v3 labels 93 of 114 Pidgin recordings as Yoruba or Swahili. Together, the results suggest that such resources need disjoint-item evaluation and hypothesis-only controls, and that speech recognition must be adapted to these varieties before spoken NLI can be tested end to end.

Small Open-Weight Language Models for Crowd Decision-Making: Capabilities and Limits of Output-Level Fusion and Linguistic Aggregation
David Herrera-Poyatos, Cristina Zuheros, Carlos Peláez-González, Andrés Herrera-Poyatos, Rosana Montes, Francisco Herrera
cs.CL
Abstract
Small open-weight language models can run crowd decision-making (CDM) on an institution's own infrastructure, which keeps the checkpoint, the inference settings and the opinions under its control. Their judgements vary across repeated queries; whether repeating and aggregating them, often through the 2-tuple linguistic representation, settles the decision rather than the labels is untested. Five open-weight models of 8-36B total parameters on one consumer GPU and a hosted reference classify 474 TripAdvisor reviews of four London restaurants ten times each; three open-weight models repeat the protocol on 600 reviews of twelve Madrid restaurants. Continuous evaluations preserve resolution: snapping each review leaves 5 distinct values against 21-65, and snapping at every level at most two collective values. Sensitivity to the participants persists once run-to-run variability is reduced: in London, redrawing the reviewers keeps the winner on 47-81% of replicas against 86-100% for redrawing the runs. With simultaneous intervals over every pair of alternatives under crowd resampling, no system on either corpus names a single winner: in London every system keeps all four restaurants as candidates, and in Madrid the candidate sets hold five to eight of the twelve. Extra runs and output-level fusion stabilize the winner but do not remove that sensitivity, and the fusion does not beat its best member. The three larger open-weight models name the hosted reference's London winner and at least match its per-review correlation with the reviewers' stars, so small open-weight models can support local CDM, reported as candidate sets with margins rather than forced rankings.

Abstract
We introduce GDP_xlsx, a benchmark of 70 realistic workplace requests written by professionals in 13 domains, each paired with an expert rubric of binary criteria and with the complete workbook or CSV file it refers to. Together the files hold 2.18 million populated cells, and the rubrics 791 criteria. Agents work in a container with spreadsheet tools, and an agentic judge that can open the file applies the rubric. Completeness and exclusivity criteria require set-valued answers to be found in full and with nothing extra. We rank models by strict pass@1, the share of attempts that meet every criterion. Across 20 model configurations, including frontier models from Google, Anthropic, and OpenAI, the top-scoring one, Gemini 4 Argon, passes only 38.3% of attempts, and the median score is 19.3%, leaving wide headroom. We release the evaluation harness and three public tasks; the other 67 are held out to protect the leaderboard from contamination.

LuthorSQL: A One-Pass Text-to-SQL Model as System 1 for a Slower LLM Agent
Ibrahim Memon, Armin Karamzade, M. Hanif Jhaveri, Roy Fox
cs.CL · cs.AI · cs.DB · cs.LG
Abstract
Frontier LLM agents that query databases with tools are among the most accurate text-to-SQL systems, but per-query calls to a hosted model are slow, costly, and expose sensitive organizational queries to external APIs. For example, a Claude Opus 5.5 agent serving 10,000 queries a day costs over $70,000 a year at list price. To what extent can local hardware already owned by an organization offload this burden without compromising the agent's accuracy? We present LuthorSQL, a non-autoregressive 7B text-to-SQL model that serves as a rapid System 1 gate for a slower LLM agent (System 2), escalating queries only when uncertain. LuthorSQL makes a single forward pass to predict slot scores over a bounded grammar for query plans, compiles and runs up to 32 candidate plans, and chooses an answer using a trained validator. On its own, LuthorSQL reaches 68.03% execution accuracy on BIRD's hidden test set at 0.84 s per question on a single GPU, maintains its accuracy in 9 GB with 8-bit quantized weights, and transfers zero-shot to Snowflake, BigQuery, MySQL, PostgreSQL, and DuckDB within 3.1 points of its native SQLite accuracy. Combined with Claude Opus 5.5 in a pre-registered confidence cascade over 227 questions on databases published after Opus's training cutoff, LuthorSQL answers nearly half of all questions locally and cuts API cost by 33.3% at the same accuracy. Finally, contamination probing shows that Opus reproduces 48-92% of public benchmark gold queries verbatim without seeing the schema; however, its tool-assisted accuracy holds on renamed schemas and reaches 95.6-97.4% on post-cutoff databases. Code and data: https://github.com/IAMIbrahimmemon/luthorsql

RAG-FGO: Dynamic Optimization of Query-Rewriting Agents for Generative RAG
Qigan Sun, Xudong Wang, Chaoning Zhang, Chenghao Li, Jiaquan Zhang, Jiehui Xie, Sungyoung Lee, Jie Zou, Ning Xie, Peng Wang, Yang Yang, Heng Tao Shen
cs.CL
Abstract
In retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), query rewriting serves as a core interface that aligns user intent with external knowledge bases, and its effectiveness directly determines overall system performance. However, generative retrieval faces three critical challenges in real-world deployment: noisy retrieval feedback, the difficulty of jointly optimizing discrete prompt templates and continuous decoding-control parameters, and the limited generalization of static rewriting strategies under semantically complex scenarios. To address these issues, we propose RAG-FGO, a dynamic optimization framework for generative retrieval strategies. RAG-FGO formulates query rewriting as a non-differentiable black-box optimization problem in a mixed discrete-continuous space, and represents prompt templates and decoding parameters as adaptively optimizable query-rewriting agents. The framework performs global search to enable semantic jumps and structural reorganization that escape local optima, while applying local perturbations for fine-grained alignment and precise refinement. In addition, RAG-FGO introduces an elite-preserving memory pool that accumulates high-quality strategies across iterations, thereby mitigating the impact of stochasticity and noisy feedback on optimization stability. Experimental results on a diverse suite of benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed method, supporting its applicability to complex semantic retrieval tasks.

CarryOn: Learning What to Keep in Context for Long-Horizon LLM Agents
Lihuan Li, Ruiyi Yang, Jiru Wang, Yifan Duan, Xiachong Lin, Hao Xue, Yang Song, Flora Salim
cs.CL · cs.AI
Abstract
Long-horizon LLM agents can coordinate tools and applications to complete complex, multi-step tasks. As their interaction histories grow, effective context management becomes essential for reliable and efficient execution. Existing methods reduce context through summarization, pruning, or history selection. However, shorter contexts do not necessarily support better decisions: summaries can omit exact details needed for later actions, while retaining entire interactions can carry substantial irrelevant information. We propose CarryOn, a lightweight context manager that pairs compact execution state with fine-grained, source-linked evidence. A learned selector and adaptive retention policy keep relevant evidence available without additional LLM calls for context management. We introduce two complementary evaluation metrics: $S_α$ prioritizes task success while accounting for resource use, and normalized cost per success (NCS) measures the resources spent per successful task. Across AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Tau2 Telecom with three LLM Actors, CarryOn achieves 75.7% macro-averaged task success, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.7 percentage points. It also achieves the best average scores on both metrics.

PaperCritic: Training AI Reviewers to Assess the Soundness of Papers
Hyun Ryu, Ji Yong Cho, Eunho Yang, Moontae Lee, June Yong Yang, Sean Welleck, Carolyn Rose
cs.CL
Abstract
As AI conference submissions grow rapidly, AI reviewers are being considered as an alternative. However, simply imitating human reviewing is problematic because reviewing serves multiple purposes, including evaluating papers' soundness, interestingness, and clarity, the last two of which are subjective. In this work, we focus solely on evaluating soundness, the most important and objective criterion, and define the learning objective as jointly maximizing (1) precision, based on the soundness and substantiveness of review points, and (2) recall, the coverage of high-quality reference review points. Under this problem formulation, we construct a quality-labeled review point dataset, PaperSound-23K, using 1K ICLR submissions and 23K human-written and LLM-generated review points. Using this dataset, we train an AI reviewer model, PaperCritic, through reinforcement learning with the F1 score of precision and recall as the reward. Our experiments show that PaperCritic outperforms frontier models and models trained with other AI reviewer training methods on an in-domain test set. Surprisingly, learning to review ICLR submissions also transfers to reviewing Nature-family papers and even to out-of-domain reviewing tasks (e.g., code review, legal contract review, and feedback on story writing).

cs.CL
Abstract
Evaluating grammatical knowledge in language models requires benchmarks that cover diverse linguistic phenomena and are validated against human judgments. Our findings highlight how training data quantity, composition, and model architecture shape grammatical generalization in Romanian.

Abstract
We study encoder models that pick one of several options in a single forward pass, without generating text. Can You Trust Your Finance Agent? Drift, Diagnosis and Auditable Intervention on One Shipped Financial Research Agent
Cosimo Galeone, Anna Ettorre, Ginevra Mariani, Minsu Park, Giuseppe Ettorre, Daniele Ligorio
cs.CL · cs.AI
Abstract
Can you trust a finance agent you did not build? We take the reference agent shipped with the Vals Finance Agent Benchmark v2, leave it untuned, and ask four questions a leaderboard score does not answer. Is the score stable? Seven identical runs on 18 questions pass between 105 and 123 of 149 rubric criteria, and the bill varies by up to 17%. When the agent fails, who owns the fix? We classify 20 failed trajectories, from three runs of nine held-out questions, by the layer whose owner would remedy them, under a taxonomy frozen in advance: 13 belong to the agent's instructions, 3 to its tools, 4 to the model, none to its data. Can that diagnosis be trusted? Four blind readers from three model families, given only the traces, name the same fault as our LLM analyzer in 94% of cases, the rate at which they agree with one another (93%). Can a change be trusted? An optimization loop reads the traces, makes one targeted change per round and keeps it only if it clears a fixed threshold. Under a cost objective its final configuration costs 0.34x the baseline on the held-out questions (95% CI [0.22, 0.58]) at accuracy within noise. Under an accuracy objective it gains 14 points on the development questions and 1.5 on the held-out ones (CI [-5.3, +7.2]). Judged against the measured run-to-run noise, under a rule written before the test was opened, the first is a real saving and the second is not. This is a case study of one agent and nine test questions, not a general result.

In the Eye of the Beholder: Human Label Variation in Textual and Visual Entailment
Monica Riedler, Jasmin Orth, Raffaella Bernardi, Barbara Plank
cs.CL
Abstract
Human Label Variation (HLV) is increasingly recognised as an informative signal that reflects meaningful differences in how people interpret the same input. While HLV has been studied extensively in Natural Language Inference (NLI), much less is known about HLV in Visual Entailment (VE; Xie et al., 2019), where the textual premise is replaced by an image, keeping the hypothesis and label space fixed. We introduce VariNLI-VE, a dataset for studying how HLV changes between paired NLI and VE instances. We re-annotate a subset of originally neutral SNLI (Bowman et al., 2015) instances and their visual counterparts, with the same annotators evaluating both the textual and visual versions. Each annotation includes a label, confidence score, and explanation, yielding 2,250 annotations. Labels and explanations are collected jointly, providing ecologically valid explanations. Because the image may reveal information not stated in the text, one might expect disagreement to decrease in VE. Instead, we find no clear overall reduction: disagreement decreases for some instances while it increases for others. Explanations reveal further variation, as annotators can retain the same label while changing their rationale. When evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) on the paired data, we find lower accuracy, weaker alignment with human label distributions, and generally higher uncertainty in VE than in NLI.

cs.CL / 16 / 2611.17963
Toward Universal Translatability: On the Statistical Ontology of Generative AI
Katia Schwerzmann
cs.CL
Abstract
This paper argues that the problem of translation functions both as a point of origin and as a normative horizon for contemporary generative AI. In current discourse around ''world models,'' their attainment is framed as the achievement of internal consistency, cross-modal alignment, and physical grounding. Taken together, these demands converge on an ideal of the model as a universal translation machine capable of establishing lossless correspondences across linguistic, visual, and physical representation spaces. I show that this ideal presupposes a metaphysical commitment that I call a statistical ontology: the belief that reality has an underlying, computable statistical structure that can be recovered through sufficient data and modeling, rather than being shaped and accessed through situated human interpretation and labor. Tracing this commitment back to Warren Weaver's 1949 Memorandum on machine translation, I outline how translation was historically recast as code deciphering and, later, as the alignment of probability distributions across structured outputs. Finally, I contrast this paradigm with approaches in Translation Studies that foreground opacity, power asymmetries, and the translator's positionality. I argue that generative AI's statistical ontology is pre-Kantian, pre-critical and obscures the infrastructures and human labor that make machine translatability---as alignment---possible in the first place.

FEND: Far-End Detection in Outbound Voice AI Calls
Abhishek Kumar, Suraj Agrawal, Aashraya Sachdeva
cs.CL · eess.AS
Abstract
When a conversational Voice AI agent places an outbound call, the far end may be a live person, a voicemail greeting, an interactive voice response (IVR) system, or increasingly another AI system answering on the callee's behalf. Standard Answering Machine Detection (AMD) reports only whether a human or a machine picked up, a design meant for human call centres, where one machine is as uninteresting as another. That single bit does not tell a Voice AI agent what to do, since leaving a voicemail message, navigating an IVR and holding a short exchange with another AI are three different actions and a two-valued label cannot distinguish them. In 1,000 randomly sampled calls from our own outbound deployment, a live person answered 48.1%, a voicemail system 44.6%, an AI 6.9% and an IVR 0.4%. Our proposed framework, FEND, reads the far end's audio and its streaming transcript and runs one detector for each automated class (voicemail, IVR, AI callee), with human the default when nothing fires, returning a single label within 20 seconds. On a public benchmark the voicemail components reach 100% accuracy without ever misclassifying a live human, where Asterisk's AMD misclassifies 30 to 66% of them. For the AI callee, acoustic features do not transfer across recording conditions, but speech timing does, separating AI from human at 0.952 ROC-AUC, because synthesised speech leaves far shorter gaps between words. On production traffic, separating a live person from any automated far end reaches F1 0.979, and only 2.9% of live people are given an automated label, the error that matters most for an agent acting on the result.

Agent-as-a-Judge
Runyang You, Hongru Cai, Caiqi Zhang, Qiancheng Xu, Meng Liu, Tiezheng Yu, Yongqi Li, Wenjie Li
cs.CL
Abstract
Artificial intelligence has progressed from task-specific systems toward general models spanning perception, generation, reasoning, and autonomous interaction, making reliable evaluation essential for comparison, deployment, and post-training feedback. At scale, modern evaluation pipelines increasingly rely on LLM-as-a-Judge to produce scores, preferences, and critiques; in its fixed-context setting, the judge relies on the supplied evaluand and evaluation materials. Yet a reliable verdict may require evidence beyond the supplied evaluand and context---either because critical environmental states remain unrecorded in the static execution log, or because controversial factual claims require external verification. Passive judges cannot obtain that missing evidence before deciding, especially when evaluating agent trajectories, executable artifacts, or domain-specific reports. Recent work therefore develops active evaluators that seek missing evidence and update their grounds for judgment before deciding. We bring these advances together through Agent-as-a-Judge, focusing on how evaluators construct and control checking processes within the broader LLM-based judging landscape. We define the scope through evaluation-directed checks and the use of their results, covering both structured workflows and observation-driven control. We organize the design space by multi-agent collaboration, tool integration, memory, and planning, and show how these mechanisms supply the checks and evidence on which a verdict rests. We consolidate published scores and reproduce missing entries for key agentic judges; the comparison reveals uneven agentic gains and motivates dedicated agentic evaluation. We conclude by characterizing application demands and open challenges in cost, reliability, safety, and governance that condition trusted deployment of agentic judges at scale.

VEPO: Mitigating Multilingual Hallucinations in LVLMs via Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization
Chengpeng Fu, Xiaocheng Feng, Wenshuai Huo, Yichong Huang, Baohang Li, Duyu Tang, Ting Liu
cs.CL
Abstract
Large vision-language models (LVLMs) achieve strong multimodal performance, yet they remain prone to object hallucination---generating answers that are linguistically fluent but visually ungrounded. This issue is especially severe in low-resource languages, where weaker cross-modal alignment causes models to rely more on language priors than on image evidence. Existing remedies either require costly multilingual annotations or external translation/reward models, or intervene at inference time in ways that can distort the output language. We propose VEPO(Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization), a training-time method that transfers reliable visual grounding from English to other languages without external models. VEPO first localizes key visual evidence using English queries via mid-layer cross-modal attention, then constructs preference pairs that share the same correct answer but differ in evidence sufficiency (full image vs.\ key-region masked image). Direct preference optimization on these pairs encourages low-resource inputs to prefer answers supported by complete visual evidence. Experiments on multilingual POPE and MME with Chinese, French, and Hindi show consistent hallucination reduction and perception gains, with the largest improvements on the lowest-resource language. Analyses further confirm that VEPO sharpens question-relevant attention and that evidence-guided masking outperforms random masking.

cs.CL / 20 / 2611.18104
Contagious Fingerprints: When LLMs Inherit Another Model's Identity
Zailong Tian
cs.CL
Abstract
The fingerprint by which a classifier recognizes the LLM behind a text is usually treated as a fixed property of the model. We show that it is contagious: a model that continues a conversation begun by another model inherits that model's fingerprint. This undermines audits of API relays, which can serve the first turns of a conversation with the model a customer pays for and silently hand the rest to a cheaper one. After such a handoff, detection of the substitute by a text classifier drops from 0.97 to 0.72 (DeepSeek replacing Claude Sonnet) and from 0.87 to 0.54 for a same-vendor downgrade (Haiku), and retraining on handoff data repairs only substitutes seen in training. Preregistered experiments trace the contagion to imitation of the history's style. History also moves a model away from its own fingerprint: active probes are not camouflaged, but when calibrated on fresh conversations they flag \emph{honest} models on long contexts at rates up to 1.00, and a standard MMD equality test rejects all nine models we test. Both failures come from comparing a history-dependent reply with a history-free reference. We propose \textsc{Lineup}, which samples references from the claimed model \emph{on the same history} and tests the audited reply by its rank among them. \textsc{Lineup} controls false positives by exchangeability and needs no fitted threshold or log-probabilities. With contemporaneous references on two closed models, its false-positive rate is 0.056 (preregistered bound 0.10), and it detects 0.81 of substitute sessions across six pairs after four audited turns.

Folding Language Models into Themselves
Ziyang Zhang, Yubin Jing, Yuyao Li, Haofan Wang, Yichen Gong
cs.CL
Abstract
Knowledge distillation usually trains a compact student from scratch, which needs a large amount of data even though the teacher's weights already encode most of what the student has to learn. In this work, we explore building the student directly from the teacher's weights. We propose Concertina, which folds every group of N adjacent teacher layers into one shared block that is executed N times. Each loop step carries a low-rank correction initialized from the layer it replaces, and a per-token gate mixes these corrections, to let every token recover the behavior of the original layers from a single shared block. We further design a distillation data recipe that balances general, mathematical, academic and targeted multiple-choice data, to recover all capabilities of the teacher together rather than one at the expense of another. On Qwen3-4B, a 2.27B Concertina model distilled on 22B tokens retains about 90% of the teacher on commonsense and mathematical reasoning and 84% on average across eight benchmarks. It outperforms same-size dense and layer-sharing baselines, as well as Qwen3-1.7B, which is trained on over a thousand times more data. These results suggest that a compact model need not be retrained at scale, since folding the teacher into itself and distilling it on the right data recovers most of its capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/myboker/concertina.

GT-FENG: General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding in Language Models *Author(s)
Jiayu Feng
cs.CL
Abstract
Whether a language model's answer is correct often depends on context: the same value can be right under one jurisdiction, date, or legal definition and wrong under another. We introduce GT-FENG (General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding), the first general framework to unify contextual/temporal correctness and rule-based error attribution across law, policy, and other rule-governed domains. GT-FENG provides a foundation for evaluating language models in rule-governed domains and for future benchmarks and evaluation toolkits. We release GT-FENG as an open-source Python package, gtfeng.

Learning Word-Level Explanations of Text Reuse in Latin Literature with Little Annotation
Julian Schelb, Michael Wittweiler, Marie Revellio
cs.CL
Abstract
Detecting an intertextual reference is only the first step; philologists also need to know how a later author reworked the source. We explain a reuse pair word by word: each word of the reusing passage is first aligned to its source word, then labeled with the operation that transformed it, such as a copy, a change of inflection, or a substitution. Such word-level operations are expensive to annotate, and only a few corpora annotated at this level exist for text reuse in Latin literature. We describe a procedure that needs only a limited number of annotations: synthetic pairs, built by applying known operations to Latin text, train a first model, and active learning chooses the pairs an annotator corrects. Among several architectures, a model that links and labels together performs best: a pointer network that, for every reuse word, chooses a source word and names the operation in the same step. We show that with active learning, 200 pairs chosen by uncertainty are enough for the model to match one trained on every annotated pair of the 1,490 references of the Loci Similes benchmark. We release our code, word-level predictions for all references, and an interactive demo.

Mingming Su, Yushan Wei, Sen Song, Xinche Zhang
cs.CL
Abstract
When model predictions track an unfolding human affect trajectory, alignment may reflect narrative position, affective words, or text-specific reader-response dynamics. Under crossed participant-text resampling, all nine identity intervals remain above zero; joint-control advantages are robust for valence and directional but less certain for arousal. High shape agreement still coexists with scale bias. LitArc-Bench identifies which components of literary reader response are recoverable beyond shallow explanations and why shape, calibration, and human variability require separate evaluation.

cs.CL / 28 / 2611.18471
Do Verifier Judgments Survive Abstraction? Measuring Verification Transport for LLM Agent Evaluation
Disha Sheshanarayana, Sai Linisha Reddy Goli
cs.CL · cs.AI
Abstract
LLM-based verifiers are increasingly used to evaluate agent executions, but their reliability is typically measured under a single representation of the trajectory. In deployed agent systems, the same execution may be exposed through composed tools, delegation boundaries, or compacted histories, changing what execution evidence is directly observable to the verifier. It therefore remains unclear whether correct verifier judgments persist across such abstraction boundaries. We introduce Verification Transport, a reliability test that measures judgment stability under abstraction-induced changes in execution observability. We construct matched native, abstracted, and restored views of the same frozen executions while holding the task, realized trajectory, outcome, ground truth, evaluation criterion, and verifier fixed. Across four agent-evaluation benchmarks, seven verifier backbones, four verification protocols, and three abstraction boundaries, we find substantial transport failure, reaching about 31% under macro abstraction and delegation. Verification protocols exhibit distinct failure modes: direct verification more often produces incorrect verdicts, whereas evidence-grounded and deliberative protocols tend to abstain under limited observability. This suggests that lower transport failure rates do not necessarily imply greater reliability. Restoring the corresponding execution provenance recovers 85.5% of transport failures, while recovery is lower when provenance is lossy or unavailable. These results show that native-view accuracy alone is insufficient to characterize verifier reliability when execution evidence crosses abstraction boundaries.

cs.CL / 29 / 2611.18485
Interactive Program Induction with Language Model Priors
Robin Shing Moon Chan, Ryan Cotterell
cs.CL · cs.AI · cs.LG
Abstract
Program induction refers to the process in which a learner aims to recover a program from a few given input-output examples. This paradigm broadens programming access by letting users demonstrate the rule-based behavior they want to apply to unseen data, instead of having to implement it themselves. However, recovering a program from finite input-output examples is fundamentally ambiguous, as a finite set of input-output examples is theoretically consistent with infinitely many programs whose output diverges on unseen inputs. We view this problem from a Bayesian perspective, where, given a prior $p^0$ over programs, identifying the target program means concentrating posterior probability mass on it by observing additional input-output examples. In the interactive program induction setting, the learner chooses maximally informative inputs and queries an oracle, such as the user, for their outputs. We derive a greedy query-selection algorithm and show that it terminates after $\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}γ\log 1/p^0(f^\star))$ informative oracle queries, and returns the target program $f^\star$ up to approximate equivalence when no other class outweighs it under the prior. The algorithm uses a language model as the prior under a PAC-style relaxation of program equivalence, where the query-selection objective is estimated using importance sampling. On the CodeARC benchmark, the algorithm improves accuracy over the LM only agent by up to 22 percentage points while observing up to 10.5 fewer input-output examples.

cs.CL / 30 / 2611.17900
AmbiSteer: Selectively Eliciting Ambiguity-Aware Responses in Vision-Language Models
Monica Riedler, Samuele Antonelli, Barbara Plank, Raffaella Bernardi
cs.CV · cs.CL
Abstract
Vision-language models (VLMs) often exhibit overconfidence, providing firm answers even when visual evidence is insufficient. A prominent case is referential ambiguity in Visual Question Answering (VQA), where multiple entities in an image are plausible referents of a question. Addressing such cases requires recognizing when ambiguity is present: ambiguous questions can be handled by asking for clarification or enumerating plausible referents, while unambiguous questions should receive a direct answer. Current VLMs struggle with this distinction, often defaulting to a single interpretation. To investigate whether activation steering can serve as an inference-time mechanism to elicit such behaviors, we introduce AmbiSteer, a lightweight framework to selectively elicit ambiguity-aware behavior. Across three VLMs, we find that steering alone has limited and model-dependent effects, while prompting is generally more effective. Combining prompting and steering substantially increases clarification and enumeration behaviors, but also elicits them on unambiguous cases. AmbiSteer addresses this using a linear classifier to predict input ambiguity and condition the steering direction accordingly, increasing the proportion of inputs handled appropriately from 59.0% to 77.0% on average across models. Our work sets the ground for lightweight approaches to reduce model overconfidence in uncertain multimodal settings.

PerfArena: Ranking LLMs by Code Performance
Rui Rua, Hailemariam Mersha, Joao Paulo Fernandes, Karim Ali
cs.PF · cs.CL · cs.SE
Abstract
Industry now relies on LLMs to write a large share of production code. Although that code is mostly, but not always, correct, correctness alone does not determine whether it is fit for repeated and extensive use, which depends on what the code costs in time, memory and energy. Most code-generation benchmarks nonetheless evaluate correctness in isolation, and most performance-oriented benchmarks cover few languages and rarely measure energy consumption. To address this gap, we present PERFARENA, an open-source, reproducible arena that ranks LLMs by the performance of their generated code across multiple languages and competitions. PERFARENA executes the solutions of each LLM on a Unix-like host and reports correctness together with latency, peak memory, and energy consumption. Correctness oracles, performance oracles, profilers, and code-generation workflows are pluggable in PERFARENA, enabling the integration of new judging backends and measurement tools without changing the pipeline. We evaluate PERFARENA on two competitions: CLBGComp, 10 compute-intensive benchmarks from the Computer Language Benchmarks Game, and LeetCodeComp, 99 LeetCode problems whose accepted solutions differ measurably in latency. Currently, PERFARENA holds 9,455 correct and measured solutions from 13 state-of-the-art LLMs across 7 programming languages. Across both competitions, got-6-astra is the only LLM that no other LLM beats significantly in latency, energy consumption, or peak memory, solving 99.7% of LeetCodeComp and 100% of CLBGComp. All PERFARENA leaderboards and measured solutions are publicly available, providing a continuously updated reference that helps developers choose an LLM per language and metric, instead of relying only on correctness rates or pricing.

cs.MA / 1 / 2611.17482
Collective Tree Exploration with Predictions
Romain Cosson, Yichen Huang, Michael Mitzenmacher, Ali Vakilian
cs.DS · cs.MA
Abstract
We study collective tree exploration with subtree-size predictions. In this problem, a team of $k$ mobile agents needs to explore an unknown rooted tree as fast as possible, and whenever a new vertex $u$ is discovered, the team receives a prediction $\hat n_u$ of the number of nodes $n_u$ in the subtree rooted at $u$. We provide algorithmic guarantees under two error measures: the maximum error $E=\max_u|\widehat n_u-n_u|$ and the multiplicative distortion $μ= (\max_u\widehat n_u/n_u)(\max_u n_u/\widehat n_u)$. For the maximum error, we present a deterministic algorithm that explores any tree with $n$ nodes and depth $D$ in $O(\text{OPT}+E)$ rounds, where $\text{OPT} = Θ(n/k+D)$ is the offline benchmark, and we show that there is no deterministic algorithm that admits an $O(\text{OPT})+o(E)$ guarantee. For the multiplicative distortion, we present several algorithms that induce a competitive ratio in $\widetilde O(\min\{ \sqrtμ\log k, μ\log\log k+μ^{1.5} \})$, where the $\widetilde O(\cdot)$ may hide logarithmic factors in $μ$.

cs.MA / 2 / 2611.17520
Only as Safe as the Weakest Agent: A Break That Appears Only When the System Is Multi-Agent
Keegan Wang, Anantika Mannby
cs.MA · cs.AI
Abstract
Major industry multi-agent systems like Codex and Claude Code can be broken by attacks that have long been unsuccessful against modern frontier models. AI Safety as a discipline has largely focused on finding the bounds and vulnerabilities of AI models used in isolation. Yet as humans increasingly deploy these models as agents and multi-agent systems, the same attacks that had been thought to be patched when using a model in isolation can break these same models under specific circumstances. We show that these system breaks are not purely theoretical, but can break Claude Code, Codex, and a reference harness, all unmodified and used with their own sub-agent mechanisms. We show the attack by asking agents to answer questions from a store of files on disk in which we have planted a forged record. Searching the store themselves, the strong model orchestrators commit almost zero harmful revisions in over 1,000 trials. However, if that same orchestrator model delegates to a cheaper sub-agent, which is what these harnesses are built to do, the same models on the same facts fail on 20/180, 44/180, 75/180 and 553/900. Thus, even when the system is driven by a strong model, the system's safety lands near the weak reader's, not the orchestrator's. Simulations over 18 models show the same effect in the delivery channel where five aggregators, who on their own were almost never unsafe, repeat a corrupted sub-agent's error in 751/757 trials. The safety of real-world agentic systems that use strong orchestrator agents and weaker sub-agents, and multi-agent systems in general, is defined not by the safety of the strong agent but by the safety of the weakest agent.

TradeGauntlet: Evaluating Compliance Controls for Autonomous Trading Agents
Sandip Nandi, Aishwarya Bose, Apurba Bose, Soovadeep Bakshi
cs.MA · cs.AI · cs.CE
Abstract
Autonomous trading agents need controls that prevent problematic orders without making legitimate tasks impractical. We study this aspect of agentic compliance with TradeGauntlet, a replayable limit-order-book simulator that separates detection, prevention, and execution cost. In 1,680 simulated sessions with Claude Haiku 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5, all conduct alerts were self-matches. Gates eliminated those alerts by rejecting self-crossing orders, while the aggregate-gate condition reduced Sonnet's two-sided fills by about 15%. A conduct reminder reduced Sonnet's alert rate but reduced Haiku's fills without a detectable alert benefit. These results show why zero alerts alone do not establish an agent's compliance. Scripted probes and two search methods test weaknesses beyond those observed in the LLM runs. The searches find split-order schedules that evade the implemented conduct rules; a learned baseline catches all 2,400 tested searched episodes but also flags 15% of a large maker's sessions. Aggregate caps reduce the response-dependent gain unevenly, with smaller reductions when only near-touch depth affects prices. A response fitted to five Nasdaq stocks and a separate ABIDES experiment expose the dependence of these estimates on market assumptions. A distinct adversarial prompt elicited refusals from Sonnet in all 240 rounds, yielding no candidate strategies to evaluate. TradeGauntlet provides reproducible tests of controls around trading agents; the experiments do not establish legal compliance or real-market detection accuracy.

Meta Build Systems as Logic Programs
Alex Reinking
cs.PL · cs.SE
Abstract
Meta build systems such as CMake, Meson and Bazel derive command graphs from project descriptions, user choices and machine facts. Imperative scripts, search paths and special cases make routine decisions interact poorly: search order can choose compatible packages without an explicit project choice, packagers must patch author flag preferences, and cross-builds need annotations or nested build trees for tools run during the build. Our central insight is to express configuration as relational inference: objects follow from sources, flags and libraries from dependencies, and language standards from transitive requirements. We introduce buildlog, a meta build system whose stratified logical core isolates build derivation from impure environmental probes. It rejects ambiguity in toolchain or package selection and explains derived facts through provenance. Author constraints and user preferences use distinct, statically checked input relations. Libraries can ship consumer rules, including Qt's moc/rcc rules, using the same facts for installed and built code. Using a compiled program in a build rule lets buildlog infer its build-machine configuration. We evaluate buildlog on eleven case studies of ten projects, validating configurations through execution and artifact checks while reporting remaining flag differences and platform limits. In Halide, generators link an LLVM-based compiler and run during the build to emit WebAssembly or Android code; where CMake requires nested builds, buildlog derives build-machine and target-platform configurations in one graph. A supporting formal account establishes termination, within-round probe-order independence, and agreement between fresh and validated cached runs under explicit finiteness and probe obligations. The results show that confining impurity to fact discovery yields a small, uniform and explainable configuration semantics.

Ibragim Badertdinov, Alexander Golubev
cs.SE · cs.AI · cs.LG
Abstract
Standard coding-agent benchmarks primarily evaluate binary task success, treating execution as a black box and obscuring the behaviors that produce those outcomes. We release the deterministic parser together with the full annotation and analysis pipeline alongside an annotated trajectory subset.

Abstract
Running open-weight language models locally on a single consumer GPU requires replacing expanding multi-turn exploration histories with short, stateless code inspections. Studying Gemma 4 models across 211 real-world Python issues in 86 repositories, we uncover a systematic directional relevance asymmetry: zero-temperature relevance scores on enclosing scopes and upstream callers consistently separate true structural neighbors from hard lexical distractors, whereas scores on downstream callees, peer methods, historical co-change partners, and dense node embeddings remain near chance. Likelihood calibration prevents overconfident smaller models from chasing false-positive callers while lifting exact localization on lexically hard targets from 12.8 to 23.4 percent at 31B within a single-GPU budget.

Abstract
We study feedback-driven black-box processes across software engineering and machine learning. A Unified Device-Level Physical Framework for DRAM Read Disturbance
Zhaoqiang Bai, Haocong Luo, Xinhe Wang, Xujing Li, Yuelin Jiang, Xuelu Huang, Guilei Wang, Hao Meng, Ataberk Olgun, Ismail Emir Yuksel, F. Nisa Bostancı, Jikun Wang, Onur Mutlu
cond-mat.mes-hall · cs.AR · physics.app-ph
Abstract
Read disturbance in dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) depends on both local device interactions and the electrical conditions imposed by shared array circuitry. Here, we combine DIMM-level characterization results, device measurements, and TCAD simulations to interpret RowHammer, RowPress, and ColumnDisturb within a unified device-level physical framework. We relate charge evolution at the floating storage node to three key mechanisms: trap-assisted electron migration (EM), capacitive coupling (CC), and sense-amplifier-mediated disturb (SAMD). EM is local and trap-sensitive, while CC weakens with wordline separation. SAMD extends disturbance through shared column circuitry by modifying unselected-cell leakage under rail-level bitline biases, a contribution supported by the approximately ninefold higher OFF-state leakage measured relative to retention-like conditions for a high-voltage storage state. These distinct spatial ranges account for localized RowHammer and RowPress failures and remote ColumnDisturb populations with abrupt changes at subarray boundaries. We further interpret prolonged RowPress activation as a transition from an initial RowHammer-like transient to sustained leakage driven mainly by SAMD under held wordline and bitline biases. Within this device-level framework, trap effectiveness and storage-state-dependent leakage provide an interpretation of previously puzzling failure signatures, including the earlier onset of 0-to-1 RowHammer failures despite the predominance of 1-to-0 failures at high hammer counts. The same framework explains the observed RowPress and ColumnDisturb polarities and the duration-dependent reversal of neighboring- and passing-wordline effectiveness. These results provide a physical basis for developing compact DRAM reliability models across access patterns and array configurations.

Elasticy: A Runtime-Reconfigurable Architecture for Dynamic Streams
Konstantin J Hossfeld, Nathan Sobotka, Gina Sohn, Tun-Yu Chang, Kunle Olukotun
cs.AR
Abstract
Reconfigurable Dataflow Accelerators (RDAs) have demonstrated high performance and energy efficiency for dense, regular tensor applications. However, conventional RDAs with static configurations show dramatically reduced performance and efficiency on irregular tensor workloads with data-dependent, non-uniform, dynamic tensor shapes. We propose Elasticy, a dynamically reconfigurable dataflow accelerator that employs streaming and tile abstractions to minimize the overhead of dynamic support. By encoding dynamic shapes into streams using rank-variable expressions, Elasticy exploits data-dependent tensor shape dynamism to minimize off-chip data traffic and wasted compute cycles. Elasticy employs stream-embedded reconfiguration information to trigger fine-grained runtime scheduling decisions. Elasticy also introduces a novel distributed linked streaming memory (DLSM) that more effectively uses on-chip memory to minimize off-chip data traffic and support dynamically sized tensors. Both reconfiguration and shape dynamism incur minimal overhead in Elasticy: reconfiguration occurs infrequently at the granularity of a stream of tiles, while rank-variable expressions enable lightweight handling of dynamic dimensions. On a Llama 3-8B decode layer, Elasticy achieves 92.6% of its roofline performance, compared to 69.7% on an H100. On evaluated GEMMs, the DLSM reduces total off-chip traffic by up to 61.4% relative to the best evaluated cache configuration, with no additional burden of programmer-managed memory. Elasticy's distributed runtime scheduler overlaps dependent kernels, speeding up kernel pairs by up to 1.42x. Programs can also run in both space and time: each phase of a layer's graph is pipelined spatially, while phases fold onto the same fabric over time. Compared to a traditional spatial accelerator, Elasticy achieves a 6.34x-31.67x improved area delay product on full layers.

SIMTurn: Lightweight Divergent Subgroup Scheduling for Intra-Warp Synchronization
Nikhil Rout, Joe Samuel Rosh, Blaise Tine
cs.AR · cs.DC · cs.PL
Abstract
Stack-based reconvergence enables efficient SIMT execution with a shared warp program counter and compact control-flow state. However, its fixed serialization of divergent paths can deadlock programs that rely on intra-warp synchronization: a spinning subgroup may prevent a resource-holding sibling from executing. Independent Thread Scheduling (ITS) enables finer-grained progress through per-thread execution state, but introduces additional hardware cost. We present SIMTurn, a lightweight compiler-hardware mechanism that enables forward progress among divergent subgroups while retaining stack-based reconvergence. SIMTurn combines a compiler-inserted yield instruction at potentially blocking loops with a bounded pool of resumable subgroups. The scheduler allows a waiting subgroup to yield to a sibling that can make progress, without requiring independent program counters for every thread. We implement SIMTurn in the compiler, simulator, and RTL on the open-source Vortex GPGPU and evaluate it against baseline IPDOM and per-thread ITS schemes. Across the evaluated GPU thread configurations, SIMTurn introduces only 17-24% scheduler-area overhead relative to IPDOM, compared with 39-91% for ITS, and stays within 8% of IPDOM on workloads that never need cross-subgroup progress. On kernels with genuine cross-subgroup parallelism, SIMTurn tracks ITS closely and is up to 2.7x faster than a software-serialized IPDOM baseline.
