cs.AR / 5 / 2611.18523
Mutable Inference: Architecting LLM Accelerators for Inference-Time Learning
Yimin Gao, Liangtao Dai, Melika Morsali, Junting Huo, Mircea Stan
cs.AR
Abstract
Long-context LLMs must retain growing histories, but full attention makes each new token increasingly expensive. Inference-time learning (ITL) offers another approach. The model keeps training on the context during inference, retaining what it learns in its weights. Recent work shows that ITL can match full-context attention quality while attending only to a recent fixed-size window. This bounds attention costs but adds Learning (backward computation and weight updates) alongside Prediction (the forward pass) costs. Inference accelerators assume fixed weights, while training accelerators target complete training steps. This work identifies two opportunities. Attention's non-matrix operations leave enough idle matrix capacity for Learning, while selected layers can tolerate delayed weight updates with little quality loss. We exploit both with Learning While Serving (LWS), an inference accelerator architecture that shares compute hardware between latency-critical Prediction and deadline-flexible Learning. Hardware-managed per-layer weight versioning allows Learning to publish updates while in-flight computations read older versions. Resumable reduction blocks let Learning kernels span fragmented idle windows and yield to Prediction, while dedicated softmax-gradient units relieve vector contention. On modeled datacenter and edge NPUs, LWS hides Learning within Prediction, sustaining Prediction-only throughput while preserving 99.3% of fully fresh Learning's quality benefit for TTT-E2E 3B at 128K tokens. Across nine models, LWS achieves 1.6x the throughput of serial execution and 1.3x that of the best prior co-execution architecture. RTL synthesis shows that LWS adds 1.5% to NPU core area without another MAC array. By efficiently turning idle inference capacity into learning capacity, LWS establishes inference-time learning as a first-class workload for future LLM accelerators.

Abstract
Event-camera segmentation requires persistent neural state despite sparse input events, making memory organization a central constraint on embedded implementation. Component layouts and simulation evidence quantify local implementation costs and expose storage replication and service scheduling as scaling constraints. WaferTetris: Efficient Freeform Mapping on Wafer-Scale Accelerators
Dayou Du, Congjie He, Andrew Boutros, Luo Mai, Vaughn Betz, Jianyi Cheng
cs.AR · cs.CL
Abstract
Wafer-scale accelerators offer massive compute capacity through hundreds of thousands of processing elements (PEs) interconnected across a single wafer, but realizing this potential requires efficient application mapping. Their scale makes programming these systems and mapping applications onto them challenging. Existing approaches rely on template-based mappings to maintain productivity, but these templates restrict the mapping design space. In particular, they impose fixed geometric layouts over the PE grid, often leaving compute and memory resources underutilized. In this work, we present WaferTetris, a system that removes these geometric restrictions through freeform mapping. Its Tetris-like strategy flexibly packs computation across the wafer, improving PE utilization and usable memory capacity while preserving PE-task code. We develop a recurrence-based throughput model that captures branch-and-join dependencies to guide spatial parallelism, and an architecture model with per-color gates that enable existing FPGA place-and-route algorithms to enforce WSE routing constraints. We evaluate WaferTetris on Cerebras WSE-3 using LLM decoding and scientific stencil benchmarks. For LLM decoding, it achieves up to $4.42\times$ the system throughput of a template-based automatic mapping baseline on the same fabric size. Compared with the manually optimized WaferLLM, WaferTetris supports $1.8\times$ the context length for LLaMA3.1-8B (80k versus 44k), while delivering equal or higher per-user throughput at matched context lengths, up to $1.10\times$.

Wei Cheng, Yiming Li, Junyao Zheng, Cheng-Bin Chen, Tingjun Chen
cs.NI · cs.AR · eess.SP
Abstract
Next-generation wireless research relies on software-defined radios (SDRs) with more channels and larger bandwidth. The USRP family is one of the most widely used SDR platforms, and much of the wireless software ecosystem is built on top of the USRP hardware driver (UHD). However, each USRP model comes with a fixed channel count, bandwidth, and streaming capability. Off-the-shelf radio frequency system-on-chip (RFSoC) boards, on the other hand, integrate up to 16 analog-to-digital converters (ADCs) and 16 digital-to-analog converters (DACs) operating at several gigasamples per second (GS/s), but existing UHD applications cannot directly use them. We present uhd-rfsoc, an open-source FPGA design and host software stack that turns these boards, including the 16-channel ZCU216 and the RFSoC 4x2, into standard UHD devices without modifying UHD. Through standard UHD calls, applications can configure the carrier frequency, channel count, the per-channel I/Q sample rate, and the wire format. uhd-rfsoc combines timestamped, flow-controlled sample transport in the FPGA with data plane development kit (DPDK) packet processing and vectorized sample conversion on x86 and Arm hosts. The built bitstreams for a number of RFSoC board configurations with varying RF data converter rates, channel counts, I/Q sample rates, and wire formats all successfully pass UHD's built-in streaming benchmarks. We further demonstrate compatibility with unmodified GNU Radio flowgraphs, a custom GPU-accelerated transceiver digital signal processing pipeline, as well as the OCUDU and Duranta 5G radio access network stacks using both software and commodity user equipment.

The State Is Not the Bottleneck: A Paged-State Gated-DeltaNet Tile on a Tri-Mode Multicast Mesh, Bit-Exact from RTL to Silicon
Cong Xu, Ravi Sankar
cs.RO · cs.AR · cs.LG
Abstract
Linear-attention language models replace a growing key-value cache with a recurrent state of fixed size. On an edge FPGA, this state can exceed the on-chip memory and must be paged to external memory. This paper asks whether such paging dominates the cost of decoding (generating tokens). We built a hardware tile that computes one complete layer of a custom 369-million-parameter hybrid Gated DeltaNet model on an Agilex 5 FPGA and pages its recurrent state. The model state needs 6.2 MiB, whereas the device has 0.87 MiB of block memory. Paging adds 26.0 MB of memory traffic per decoded token, at most 11.2 percent of the calculated total. On the board at 75 MHz, with synthetic weights, one token takes 11.15 ms in this layer. The matrix-vector products with the weights take about 62 percent of this time and the state phase, including the paging, 13 percent. The tile is one node of a multicast mesh of tri-mode processing elements. We measure the error of the fixed-point arithmetic against floating point and check that the hardware agrees bit for bit with a fixed-point reference. In simulation, the state logic agrees with the reference on all 18 recurrent layers. On the board, six builds pass all 30 runs of 64 tokens and two runs of 1,024 tokens. For this model and memory interface, the delivery of the weights limits the decoding performance, and the recurrent state does not. State paging is therefore practical for such a model on an edge FPGA.

cs.CR / 1 / 2611.17554
Online Allocation with Differential Privacy
Jianyi Yang, Xingyu Zhou, Mostafa Mushsharat, Shaolei Ren
cs.CR
Abstract
We study private online allocation problems, where allocation decisions must satisfy differential privacy (DP) to protect sensitive user information while optimizing performance under limited resources. We first formalize differential privacy notions suited to different privacy requirements in online allocation and establish the fundamental performance limits of any algorithm under these definitions. Based on these insights, we propose PPOA, a privacy-preserving meta algorithm for online allocation, and establish sufficient conditions under which PPOA preserves Joint DP (JDP) or Local DP (LDP) while achieving asymptotically near-optimal performance. Building on these conditions, PPOA can be instantiated into a variety of concrete algorithms. In particular, we present the specific designs, which include PPOA-DMD that preserves JDP and LDP and PPOA-FTRL that preserves JDP. We analyze their performance under both adversarial and stochastic settings and characterize the fundamental trade-offs between DP and allocation performance. Finally, we demonstrate the superior performance of the proposed algorithms via numerical experiments on AI model routing in battery-powered edge systems.

cs.CR / 2 / 2611.17596
Tractable Importance Sampling for Monte Carlo Privacy Accounting
Galen Andrew, Arun Ganesh
cs.CR · cs.LG
Abstract
Evaluating tight Differential Privacy (DP) parameters for DP matrix factorization with privacy amplification by sampling requires analyzing high-dimensional, correlated Gaussian mixtures. Because integrating these mixture distributions to find the exact privacy failure probability ($δ$) is analytically intractable, these mechanisms are typically analyzed using Monte Carlo accounting. However, standard sampling suffers from prohibitive variance when estimating small $δ$ associated with strict privacy guarantees, which in turn requires a large sample complexity, making Monte Carlo accounting inefficient. We demonstrate that Monte Carlo accounting can be made much more efficient via importance sampling. We give a shifted importance sampling proposal distribution parameterized by a stretch factor $α> 1$. We prove a closed-form bound $B(α) < 1$ on the support of the importance-weighted estimator that holds identically for both directions of the hockey-stick divergence, show that $B$ is log-convex so that the optimal $α$ is a one-dimensional root-finding problem, and show that the resulting estimator seamlessly plugs into the KL-Hoeffding verification protocol used by past work, with a sample complexity reduced by exactly a factor of $1/B(α^*)$.

Binarization as an Attack Surface in Weightless Neural Networks
Lucas Senos Coutinho, Matheus Vargas, Pedro Teixeira, Heudson Tosta Mirandola, Ori Rottenstreich, Priscila Machado Vieira Lima, Leandro Santiago, Daniel Sadoc Menasche
cs.CR · cs.AI · cs.LG
Abstract
Weightless Neural Networks (WNNs) are lightweight classifiers that binarize their inputs and learn by storing binary patterns in lookup memories, which makes them attractive for resource-constrained tasks such as intrusion detection. Because a small perturbation that crosses no binarization threshold leaves the binary input unchanged, WNNs may appear naturally robust to adversarial examples; a perturbation that does cross a threshold, however, can still change the prediction. We study this tension in WiSARD, the canonical RAM-based WNN, and in two variants that replace its memories with Bloom filters, Bloom WiSARD and BTHOWeN. A continuous perturbation reaches these classifiers only by crossing an encoding threshold, changing at least one binary tuple, and redirecting a memory query; we formalize this mechanism with a threshold-crossing bound and a response-margin certificate for standard Boolean WiSARD. Controlled MNIST experiments compare linear and data-driven thermometer encodings under model-agnostic sensitivity probes, a score-based Simulated Annealing attack, and surrogate-transfer attacks. Changing only the encoding while keeping each model otherwise fixed shows that encoding granularity can trade a small clean-accuracy loss for a large decrease in attack success. On the Bot-IoT, CICIDS, and UNSW-NB15 intrusion-detection datasets, adversarial perturbations lead WNN detectors to classify many attacks as normal traffic and to raise false alarms, even when their aggregate accuracy remains high.

Debashis Das, Sourav Banerjee, Saraju P. Mohanty
cs.CR
Abstract
Healthcare organizations collect huge amounts of patient data from clinical records, medical tests, monitoring systems, and healthcare services. Such data can underpin intelligent healthcare analytics but remains distributed across institutions and cannot be shared directly due to privacy, security, and trust concerns. Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative learning without sharing raw patient records, but the exchanged model information remains vulnerable to privacy leakage, tampering, replay, and unauthorized access. In this paper, we propose iGRAIL, a blockchain-enabled generative FL framework for trustworthy healthcare intelligence. iGRAIL combines local generative learning for data augmentation with differential privacy to protect sensitive information during local training. Model communication is secured using cryptographic mechanisms, and blockchain-based verification and smart-contract access control ensure that only authorized, authentic, fresh, and integrity-preserving updates are accepted for federated aggregation. Increasing the strength of differential privacy reduces the privacy-loss parameter $ε$ by about 72.5% for the cancer dataset and 87.5% for the diabetes dataset, with only a limited loss in prediction performance. These results indicate that iGRAIL supports secure and scalable federated computing while preserving privacy, model integrity, and controlled participation, at the cost of additional computational overhead.

cs.CR / 5 / 2611.17747
Evaluating the Robustness of PRNU Source-Camera Attribution to the Fingerprint-Copy Attack: An Open Toolkit with Triangle-Test Detection
Zeno J. M. H. Geradts
cs.CR
Abstract
Photo-Response Non-Uniformity (PRNU) is the de facto standard trace for sensor-level source-camera attribution, and a high Peak-to-Correlation-Energy (PCE) between a questioned image and a camera reference fingerprint is routinely treated as strong evidence of common origin. The fingerprint-copy attack of Goljan, Fridrich and Chen (2011) shows that this signal can be forged: an adversary who estimates a target camera's fingerprint from that camera's publicly available images can inject a scaled copy of it into an unrelated photograph, producing a forgery that clears a PCE decision threshold despite never having been taken by the target device. We present prnucf, an open, auditable toolkit that pairs a reference implementation of the (additive) fingerprint-copy attack with the triangle-test detector and a cohort-calibrated decision procedure, so that practitioners can quantify how fragile a PRNU attribution is against this attack on their own pipelines. On a controlled multi-camera Monte-Carlo benchmark the attack reaches an arbitrary target PCE at a mean PSNR of 59.2 dB (visually lossless), yet the triangle test separates forgeries from innocent images with an area under the ROC curve of 0.999, detecting every forgery at a cohort-calibrated 5% false-positive operating point. We discuss why a fixed significance cut-off is not safe in casework, the limitations of synthetic validation, and the open problem of extending explainable detection to the modern, GAN-based generation of fingerprint-transfer attacks. The toolkit is released under the MIT license with an explicit dual-use statement.

Yifan Cai, Linh Thi Xuan Phan
cs.CR · eess.SY
Abstract
Many cyber-physical systems (CPS) are multi-mode: they switch between operating modes with different tasksets and timing parameters in response to physical events or user activity. We show that these mode changes create a timing side channel: by altering the interference experienced by co-running tasks, they let an unprivileged co-resident observer infer sensitive events, such as a homeowner's arrival or a vehicle's route, from its own execution timing and, on multicore platforms, its core assignment. Existing schedule-based attacks and defenses miss this channel because they assume single-mode systems or target a specific, known victim task. We present STAMP, a lightweight attack that needs no knowledge of task parameters or scheduling policy, and works whether or not the system exposes its modes. On a ZoneMinder home-security deployment, STAMP identifies the modes with 94.7% accuracy on unseen hardware, using under 0.5% of one core. On ROS 2 AMCL with the Oxford RobotCar dataset, it identifies which of seven route segments a vehicle traversed with 86.0% accuracy on unseen platforms. A schedule-randomization defense lowers detection recall to 0.53, at which setting only 53.9% as many jobs finish on time. At higher noise ($ε=10$), on-time job completion reduces to 14.4%, while introducing new leakage in 14.1% of the tasksets.

cs.CR / 7 / 2611.17883
Securing Multi-Agent Systems by Design
Xilong Wang, Xiaoxing Chen, Neil Gong
cs.CR · cs.AI · cs.CL
Abstract
Multi-agent systems solve complex tasks by decomposing them into subtasks, delegating the subtasks to agents, and aggregating the agents' responses using an aggregation rule. Malicious agents can manipulate the final solution by providing malicious responses to their assigned subtasks. In this paper, we first propose response manipulation attacks (ReMA), which iteratively optimize malicious responses to maximize attack effectiveness. Specifically, we formulate untargeted and targeted attacks as optimization problems and develop an approach to approximately solve these problems by iteratively refining malicious responses based on feedback from a surrogate multi-agent system. To defend against such attacks, we further propose SecMAS, a secure aggregation rule that leverages the semantic similarities among agent responses to select the response that is most consistent with a sufficiently large subset of the other responses. We theoretically establish security guarantees for SecMAS against both untargeted and targeted attacks. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that existing aggregation rules are vulnerable to ReMA, whereas SecMAS effectively defends against all evaluated attacks, including adaptive variants of ReMA, while maintaining utility comparable to that of existing aggregation rules.

Anna V. Guglielmi, Linda Senigagliesi, Marco Baldi, Stefano Tomasin
cs.CR
Abstract
We consider a physical layer authentication scenario in which a legitimate user (Alice) communicates with an authenticator (Bob) through a reconfigurable intelligent surface (RIS), controlled by Bob. An attacker (Trudy) aims to impersonate Alice by suitably precoding her messages and still transmitting them through the RIS. This two-part study analyzes this attack-defense interaction between Bob and Trudy under perfect (Part I) and partial (Part II) channel knowledge by all the agents. Part I formulates the joint RIS selection and precoder design under perfect channel knowledge as a finite zero-sum game, establishing exact channel subspace conditions for attack indistinguishability alongside equilibrium mixed strategies. Part II extends the defense to incomplete channel information, where each agent observes only its own channel, by formulating a finite-action Bayesian game and characterizing the resulting Bayesian Nash equilibrium. Simulations in geometric and 3GPP TR 38.901 UMi-NLOS channels reveal that authentication performance is governed by available spatial degrees of freedom, channel correlation, and operating signal-to-noise ratio. The proposed equilibrium strategies effectively balance communication rate and security, outperforming conventional benchmark policies.

Abstract
Web caches reduce origin load and latency, but automated traffic can either warm them or evict objects shoppers reuse, lowering the cache hit ratio (CHR). Existing bot defenses generally infer automation from User-Agent (UA) strings or behavior rather than identify sessions that impose cross-session cache harm. We instead formulate the task as ranking sessions by eviction damage and propose consequence-grounded session ranking. A single chronological replay attributes each eviction-induced miss by a protected shopping session (a victim) to the session whose insertion last evicted the object. A behavioral model predicts the target from session behavior alone. Selected sessions bypass the cache but remain origin-served. This comparison conditions on the victims that remain, so we also report victim bypass. Local replay and retraining on a second store reproduce harm concentration and a top tier dominated by sessions without bot tags. These results establish attributed cross-session harm as an outcome-based target for retrospective cache audits and selective intervention design.

Hello Again: Exploiting Replay Protected Monotonic Counters to Brute-Force Windows Hello PINs
Christian Werling, Matti Finder, Hans Niklas Jacob, Julian Prein, Niclas Kühnapfel, Jean-Pierre Seifert
cs.CR
Abstract
Windows Hello is the primary authentication mechanism on over one billion Windows 11 devices, supporting PINs as short as four digits. Such low-entropy PINs are viable only because the TPM's anti-hammering protection mitigates brute-force attacks. Most modern x86 platforms rely on firmware TPMs (fTPMs) that persist their non-volatile (NV) state, including the lockout state, to SPI flash. On newer platforms, rollback prevention of this state is delegated to the flash chip via Replay Protected Monotonic Counters (RPMCs), a JEDEC-standardized mechanism operating over a physically accessible bus. We present the first practical security analysis of RPMC in shipping x86 systems. Using low-cost interposer hardware, we identify multiple implementation flaws, two of which fundamentally undermine RPMC's security model: First, Winbond SPI flash - the leading NOR flash vendor with over 25% market share - leaks its HMAC key under voltage glitching, enabling arbitrary counter forgery. Second, AMD's NV controller does not verify RPMC increments, so an interposer can suppress them unnoticed. Moreover, AMD platforms without RPMC flash appear to lack NV rollback protection altogether: on two AMD desktops, reflashing an old image resets the fTPM's lockout counters. Both flaws enable rollbacks of the fTPM's NV state (AMD, Intel, or Pluton), including its lockout counters. We demonstrate this end-to-end on locked, encrypted hardware: our setup finds a 4-digit Windows Hello PIN in 40 hours instead of 69 days. A compromised PIN also grants access to device-bound keys and Hello-based enterprise SSO. The same primitive defeats BitLocker's TPM+PIN pre-boot protector, reducing the worst-case time for a default-length 6-digit PIN from 19 years to 20 days.

Hua Yang, Aditya Bhardwaj, Shidong Pan, Bowen Xu
cs.CR · cs.AI · cs.SE
Abstract
Coding agents increasingly review code, yet whether they catch the privacy defects that laws such as the GDPR require software to avoid remains largely untested, partly because few datasets connect real privacy defects to their accepted fixes and review context. We present PrivacyPR, a dataset of 4,131 merged GitHub pull requests (PRs) from 1,469 repositories, created between June 2018 and January 2024. Each PR includes its commits, diffs, review discussion, and LLM-generated labels for privacy relevance, fix type, and GDPR principles. PrivacyPR provides 1,169 high-confidence privacy fixes and 1,643 non-fix PRs, together with Verified, a manually checked split of 250 privacy fixes and 250 non-fix PRs, and Small, a 100 + 100 subset for evaluation. On Verified, both annotators endorsed 72% of the LLM's GDPR-principle assignments. Its evaluation harness reverts each fix to reintroduce the defect. On Small, two coding agents detect only 42-51% of defects under a generic review prompt, but 88-91% when explicitly asked to check privacy, while flagging 11-15% of non-fix PRs. These results show that privacy defects can be detected by coding agents, but that detection depends strongly on review intent and comes with false alarms. Data, code, reviews, judge verdicts, and an extensible evaluation harness are publicly available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23744765.

cs.CR
Abstract
Inertial sensors of smartphones are a well-known privacy risk, as they allow inferring sensitive user movements and activity without explicit permissions. Among these sensors, the magnetometer has received little attention, as it is assumed to measure mainly the Earth's magnetic field. In this paper, we show that electric trains generate distinctive magnetic fields that can be captured by a smartphone magnetometer. CANTraj: Recovering Vehicle Trajectories from CAN Signals in Naturalistic Driving
Yuhang Wang, Jingxin Yang, Chuheng Wei, Suyang Chen, Haozhuang Chi, Hao Zhou
cs.CR · cs.DB
Abstract
Open driving releases and usage-based insurance programs often claim to prevent privacy breaches by omitting GPS records when sharing or collecting vehicle data. However, removing GPS coordinates from driving logs does not erase the location information carried by vehicle signals. While prior route-inference attacks have demonstrated that speed, steering, and driving events can be matched to road networks to reconstruct trajectories, existing studies rely on inconsistent prior assumptions and evaluation metrics and are tested only on small, restricted datasets, making their results difficult to compare and their real-world effectiveness uncertain. We release CANTraj, a naturalistic driving dataset pairing decoded CAN events with time-aligned GNSS trajectories (3,940 drives, 454 drivers), and CANTraj-Bench for evaluating trajectory recovery (3,675 drives, 431 drivers, 1,380 hours, 85,919 km). On 776 evaluation drives from 94 unseen drivers, we compare six re-implemented attacks and benchmark baselines under explicit information-access protocols, paired comparisons, and metrics incorporating failures. Experiments show that combining steering signals with road-network constraints yields the largest gain: With a known start and heading, adding steering to speed raises the online map filter's five-minute route recovery from 56% to 87%, with a median average displacement error of 43 m versus 921 m for map-free dead reckoning. Offline beam search recovers 88% of test routes with the heading and 81% without it. Because methods fail on different windows, we also evaluate an LLM harness that selects among candidate routes on 659 five-minute windows; its best configuration recovers 79% of routes on test and 83% on few-shot, outperforming the best baseline on test and matching it on few-shot. Data and code at huggingface.co/datasets/HenryYHW/CANTraj and github.com/WangYuHang-cmd/GPS--CAN.

cs.CR / 16 / 2611.18449
Deterministic ECDSA on secp256k1: the RFC 6979 assumption, a counterexample, and the state of deployed implementations
Ilia Prosianoi
cs.CR
Abstract
RFC 6979 derives the ECDSA nonce as $k = F_x(h)$, with $F_x$ HMAC_DRBG keyed by the signing key x. We isolate the assumption its security rests on, A*: at known public key $Q = xG$, the signing transcript under nonces $F_x()$ is indistinguishable from the transcript under uniform nonces. A* suffices, and it is neither PRF security of HMAC nor auxiliary-input PRF security (AI-PRF) of $F_x$. A function $F'(x, h) = P_{Ext(xG)}(h)$ is a PRF under standard assumptions yet leaks x from one signature; the same holds for unmodified RFC 6979 over a compression function that keeps the PRF and dual-PRF properties used in proofs of HMAC, given a collision-resistance premise. A* and AI-PRF are separated in both directions. A* does not imply AI-PRF if the one-bit hidden number problem is hard. Conversely, in the standard model: any AI-PRF can be modified so that it stays one while two chosen-digest signing queries reveal x; under DDH with one public random point, or decisional square Diffie-Hellman without it, there is an AI-PRF whose key is recovered from signatures on two ordinary hashed messages; and a third function, under PRF security and DLP, violates A* while the scheme stays unforgeable. With an ideal compression function, A* follows tightly from DLP in the presence of a randomized-ECDSA signing oracle, and a nonce key independent of x removes the assumption. The secret HMAC message block is $2^{-127.65}$ from uniform on secp256k1, $2^{-32}$ on P-256 and 0.336 on brainpoolP256r1. An audit of 31 secp256k1 implementations finds that 28 sign deterministically without added entropy and that the raw-message deviation from bits2octets of libsecp256k1 before 0.2.0 survives in NBitcoin, dcrd and btcec v2. Low-R grinding needs no assumption beyond A*, and every nonzero Fourier coefficient of the low-R nonce set is below $2^{-116.9}$ unconditionally. We give test vectors for the untested $h >= n$ branch

cs.CR / 17 / 2611.18463
VellSSLH: Sound Type-Guided Selective Speculative Load Hardening for LLVM IR
Swarn Priya
cs.CR
Abstract
Constant-time cryptographic programs written in C can still leak secrets during speculative execution. Mispredicted branches may allow instructions to access secret data and expose it through observable behavior. Speculative load hardening (SLH) addresses these leaks, but protecting values unnecessarily can introduce significant runtime overhead. We present VellSSLH, which selectively inserts speculative load hardening at LLVM IR. Programmers specify Public and Secret inputs in annotated C programs, and our analysis infers which values may become secret during speculation. The transformation then inserts misspeculation-flag operations and protections only where needed. VellSCT, our extracted checker, validates the resulting LLVM IR. We formalize speculative constant-time (SCT) security by extending Vellvm's LLVM semantics with speculative execution, attacker directives, and leakage observations. Our security type system reasons about SSA joins, symbolic values, poison, pointer provenance, and observable faults, and supports declared declassifications and trusted external services. In Rocq, we prove that programs accepted by the checker satisfy SCT, and that a reference hardening transformation preserves architectural behavior. Evaluated optimized variants are rechecked for SCT but are not covered by this preservation proof. Our mechanized guarantees apply at LLVM IR; security of the generated binary additionally depends on backend and hardware assumptions. We evaluate VellSSLH on 24 cryptographic entries on a bare-metal Intel processor. VellSSLH achieves 4.03% lower geometric-mean runtime than LLVM SLH, with 13 comparisons favoring VellSSLH, eight favoring LLVM SLH, and three inconclusive. For X25519, VellSSLH improves runtime by 22.69% over LLVM SLH with only 1.86% overhead over unprotected code. Compared with Ultimate SLH, VellSSLH achieves 19.65% lower runtime.

cs.CR / 18 / 2611.18363
Adaptable Watermarking: Transforming Image Watermarking Models via Lightweight Adapters
Mehrdad Saberi, Vishal Asnani, Shruti Agarwal, Soheil Feizi, John Collomosse
cs.CV · cs.CR · cs.LG
Abstract
We explore the adaptability of neural image watermarking models; the extent to which one watermarking method can be transformed into another through lightweight adaptation. We introduce a framework that enables encoder-decoder watermarking architectures to become modular and upgradable, supporting multiple watermarking styles without re-training full models. Adaptation is performed by attaching a compact parameter-efficient adapter and fine-tuning under a multi-domain residual alignment loss (spatial, gradient, and frequency) while preserving bit accuracy via a secret-loss term. We evaluate this approach across a suite of recent watermarking models, including TrustMark, InvisMark, RoSteALS, HiDDeN, and WAM, demonstrating that adapted encoders closely reproduce target residuals and that adapted decoders recover messages with near-optimal accuracy under standard augmentations and distortions. We show that cross-model adaptation, with applications in interoperable and upgradable watermarking, can be achieved with as few as 1-2M trainable parameters per component (e.g., the 2M decoder adapter used in our multi-decoder inference pipeline).

cs.CR / 19 / 2611.18358
Quadratic APN Functions of $\mathbb{F}_2^{10}$ with a Self-Equivalence of Prime Order
Emanuele Bellini
cs.IT · cs.CR · math.CO
Abstract
Almost perfect nonlinear (APN) functions of $\mathbb{F}_2^n$ have the lowest possible differential uniformity. The only known APN permutation in even dimension, in dimension six, is equivalent to a quadratic APN function with a nontrivial linear self-equivalence; no APN permutation is known for even $n \geq 8$. In a published collection of about 3.8 million quadratic APN classes of dimension eight, at most 0.34% have a nontrivial linear self-equivalence. In dimension ten, the 25 literature classes considered here all have one. We study all quadratic APN functions of $\mathbb{F}_2^{10}$ with such a self-equivalence, which usually confines them to a much smaller linear space. Up to conjugacy and power similarity, self-equivalences of prime order have 299 types. We settle 298 of them. Elementary lemmas exclude 280 types, and exhaustive searches confirm this for 242 of them. The other 18 types are settled by exhaustive searches with exact equivalence certificates. Up to extended affine equivalence, the 298 types contain 29 classes of quadratic APN functions, four of them new. This also classifies the quadratic APN functions with coefficients in a subfield. Exactly 24 classes contain one with coefficients in $\mathbb{F}_4$, and all 3 that contain one with coefficients in $\mathbb{F}_{32}$ are among them. The open type consists of the quadratic canonical triplicate functions. They correspond to maximum additive Hermitian rank-distance codes. Three different searches find only the invariants of 16 classes, all among the 29. We conjecture there are no others. No quadratic APN function in the 298 settled types is Carlet-Charpin-Zinoviev equivalent to a permutation.

cs.CR / 20 / 2611.18055
DeCompCert: A Verified Decompiler for CompCert-Generated AArch64
Swarn Priya, Basavesh Ammanaghatta Shivakumar, Thomas Gilray, Kristopher Micinski
cs.PL · cs.CR
Abstract
DeCompCert is a Rocq-verified decompiler for AArch64 assembly generated by CompCert. It reconstructs CompCert C through nine reverse passes, combining deductively verified transformations with translation validation. Untrusted OCaml and Datalog analyses propose candidate reconstructions, while verified validators accept only candidates that satisfy the required semantic correspondence. The composed correctness theorem guarantees that every defined, safe behavior of an accepted C reconstruction is matched by the input CompCert assembly; under a safety assumption, the two programs are observationally equivalent. We evaluate DeCompCert on approximately 2.16 million lines of C. With exact function and call signatures, it reconstructs and recompiles all 3,173 eligible contexts under both unoptimized and optimized CompCert pipelines. Without retained signatures, its cumulative inference portfolio validates 2,606 of 3,164 contexts (82.4%), while the platform-plus-IDA configuration validates 2,407 contexts (76.1%). Native execution and output-quality experiments further evaluate the generated C and characterize the remaining limitations in signature recovery and source resemblance.
